Xvivo · XVIVO-SE · the new Memo conveyor's card of 22 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote in the layers behind the Xvivo memo

These are the Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions it worked through before the Stock-specific Investment Case A&J Memo on Xvivo was written: 51,913 words, against 14,231 words in the memo Analyst's draft.

Word for word. Every answer below is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written. Two things are changed, both layout only: each group's judgement sits above the questions it sums up, because it was written last; and each score is shown as a pill. A script checked that every word of every answer is on this page, in order.

The Investment Analyst's terms: it calls Layer 1 "the first set", Layer 2 "the second set", and Layer 3 and Layer 4 "the third and fourth sets". "The Consumer" is the Investment Analyst who works the next layer. "Our" means our own forecasts, as opposed to the sell-side's. Acronyms are explained under "Terms used" at the end, and on a computer by hovering over a dotted term.

On request: a clarity-edited version, with a summary of the most important messages and a comparison with the memo, as made for Xvivo Perfusion on 30 October 2026.

How to read the scores

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning below its title.

The letter beside each score is the system's own grade of that score (A from 90, B from 75, C from 50, D from 25, F below 25). ❌ marks a D, ❌❌ an F.

Layer 1

Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

82 questions in 10 groups · 23,373 words · written 22 October 2026, 23:27 to 23:47 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
Xvivo (XVIVO-SE): first set of questions, the foundational components of the investment case

Written 22 October 2026, the day of the Q3 2026 report. All money is SEK millions unless stated. "Company basis" margins are the company's own presentation (gross margin 74 percent in FY2025); FactSet's standardised lines give lower gross margins (65.7 percent in FY2025) and are flagged where used. Consensus is FactSet's median at 22 October 2026 with a latest estimate date of 6 October 2026, so it predates the Q3 report; where I say "consensus" I mean this pre-results set unless stated.

Orientation for the Consumer, in brief

  • Xvivo is a Gothenburg organ-preservation company: proprietary fluids (Perfadex Plus, STEEN Solution), perfusion machines (XPS for lung; Heart Assist Transport; Liver Assist; Kidney Assist Transport), single-use kits, and a United States services arm (organ recovery, normothermic regional perfusion, FlowHawk software). Three reported segments: Thoracic (lung and heart; 70 percent of Q3 2026 sales), Abdominal (liver and kidney; 25 percent) and Services (5 percent).
  • FY2025 revenue 812 (-1 percent reported, +3 percent organic); nine months 2026 revenue 733; Q3 2026 revenue 252.6 (+34 percent reported, +32 percent organic) with EBITDA 52.0 (21 percent margin) and reported EPS 1.42. Cash 313, net cash about 193 on FactSet's definition (leases in debt).
  • The three facts that set the model: (1) the lung consumables engine has recovered hard (United States EVLP disposables +183 percent in Q3, +92 percent year to date) but Q3 carried about 19 of non-recurring thoracic revenue and the largest customer, over 10 percent of revenue, has been approved to run its own platform and will buy less; (2) the heart system is CE-marked (11 August 2026) and its United States PMA was received by the FDA on 28 September 2026, with a decision expected late 2027 to early 2028 and a launch "targeted" for the end of 2027; (3) margins below the gross line are being held down by a loss-making Services unit (gross margin -19 percent in Q3), abdominal mix (61 percent), heavy capitalised development (131.7 in nine months) and now a SEK 39 a year amortisation of the CE-mark asset.
  • The sell-side (four brokers, 100 percent Buy, median target 330 against a price of 223.4) models revenue 995 / 1,210 / 1,606 / 2,137 for FY2026 to FY2029 and EPS 3.38 / 6.08 / 9.47 / 14.80. The company's own targets are an EBITDA margin above 30 percent and an EBIT margin above 20 percent by 2027, and a 70 percent abdominal gross margin by 2027; it gives no numerical guidance.

External change forces

C5510 questions
C-0170C-0228C-0352C-0464C-0540C-0656C-0733C-0868C-0942C-1057

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The external change forces are net positive and structural on demand, with the industry's shift to DCD organs, OPO reform and hospital capacity outsourcing pulling machine perfusion into use whatever Xvivo does, and with regulators having delivered the two approvals (CE mark; PMA acceptance) that were the case's main overhangs in the last six months. Against that, three external forces moved against the company in the same six months: competitive intensity rose with funded low-cost liver rivals and a lung customer becoming a self-supplier; the macro regime turned to a discount-rate shock with a likely Riksbank hike into an unhedged, krona-reporting, dollar-earning company; and the supply side cost growth and margin through a manufacturing transfer. The Consumer should build the model on an industry adoption rate that compounds (volume is the reliable leg), a company capture rate that is lumpy and faces a known 2027 step-down in lung, pricing that is flat to down in real terms outside lung fluids, and reported figures that carry a currency drag. The forces favour a 20 percent-plus revenue grower; they do not by themselves deliver the margin targets.

C-01

End-market demand: structural or cyclical change, and visibility

C70

Question asked To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

Judgement. End-market demand for machine perfusion is changing structurally, in the company's favour, with visibility well beyond two years, but the structural force is a penetration story inside a procedure base that grows only mid-single digits, and the company's own reported demand is far more volatile than the end market because it is routed through a few large customers and launch timings. The Consumer should model two layers separately: a sector-level perfusion adoption rate that compounds steadily, and a company-level capture of it that is lumpy.

The structural layer, which is the reliable part. Three forces act independently of Xvivo. First, donor mix: donation-after-circulatory-death donors reached a near 50/50 split with brain-death donors in the United States in 2025 and grow at over 7 percent a year, and such organs need machine perfusion to be assessed; 55.8 percent of United States DCD donors underwent normothermic regional perfusion in Q1 2026. Second, policy: the CMS rule ranking organ procurement organisations in three tiers, with Tier 3 facing automatic decertification, was upheld by a Florida federal court on 28 September 2026, so decertification actions start in late 2026 and current OPO agreements expire in January 2027; underperformers must pursue marginal organs, which is perfusion demand. Third, capacity outsourcing: hospitals lack perfusionists and surgeons (over 76 percent of surveyed hospital executives report staffing shortages), which pulls evaluation into centralised hubs. The evidence that the force is sector-wide is competitor growth: TransMedics +37 percent in 2025 and its services +29 percent in Q2 2026, Terumo's Organ Technologies (OrganOx) +39 percent in its first fiscal quarter of 2026, Paragonix +65 percent organic under Getinge. The addressable market is put at USD 2.9 billion by 2033 at about 7 percent a year, with overall penetration at about 4 percent, and the unmet need is ten times supply (1.7 million needed against about 170,000 transplants).

The ceiling, which the Consumer must not model through. Transplant procedures themselves grow 5 to 7 percent a year, and 2025 United States volumes were weaker than that (lung +4 percent, liver +7 percent, heart and kidney -1 percent). Perfusion penetration is cost-capped: United States lung EVLP sits at 10 to 20 percent of transplants after a decade, expert testimony caps it at 20 to 30 percent of transplants on current economics (a Duke surgical director sees 40 to 50 percent over five to ten years only if cost and evidence improve), a three-litre STEEN run costs about USD 21,000 and a full XPS run about USD 40,000, and 90 percent of United States centres still use free static cold storage for standard-risk organs. So the structural force raises the rate of adoption; it does not remove the budget constraint that sets the asymptote.

The company layer, which is cyclical in its own way. Xvivo's quarterly revenue went -15 percent (Q2 2025), -5 percent, -1 percent, +10 percent, +34 percent, +34 percent (Q3 2026) on a flat end market, because one customer destocked in 2025 and restocked in 2026, because the XPS hardware shortage of early 2025 resolved, and because heart sales under derogation rose from 3 to 22 to 24 a quarter. Q3 2026's 183 percent United States EVLP disposables growth is against the destocked Q3 2025 base; about 10 of it was inventory accumulation by the largest customer ahead of that customer's own platform launch, and 8.5 was initial finance-lease recognition. Underlying thoracic growth in Q3 was therefore closer to the high 30s percent than the reported 51 percent, and underlying group growth closer to the low-to-mid 20s than 32 percent.

Visibility. Structural layer: two-plus years, high. Company layer: management itself says it has little demand visibility beyond six months, which is the honest reading.

C-02

Competitive intensity: extent and direction

❌ D28

Question asked To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

Judgement. Competitive intensity is rising, clearly and in every organ except lung preservation fluids, and the direction turned sharper in the last six months in two ways the Consumer must carry into the model: a customer has become a competitor in lung, and two liver rivals have been funded to scale at price points below Xvivo's. This is the external force most likely to cap the margin leg of the case.

The last 18 months, as a sequence. Getinge bought Paragonix (September 2024, USD 477 million, 11x sales) and Paragonix went to five organs with KidneyVault. Terumo bought OrganOx (October 2025, about USD 1.5 billion, about 20x sales) and now reports Organ Technologies revenue +39 percent with a 15 percent margin and a 20 percent full-year margin target, plus a 25 percent ten-year CAGR ambition. Bridge to Life's VitaSmart won FDA De Novo clearance in January 2026 at a USD 15,000 to 20,000 per-case price (OrganOx about USD 30,000 to 45,000; TransMedics USD 80,000 to 100,000), raised USD 110 million in August 2026 to scale commercial and clinical field organisations, and in Europe NICE now recommends routine NHS funding for ex-situ liver perfusion devices including VitaSmart. TransMedics launched CHOPS into cold preservation, is promoting NOP Connect as a digital ecosystem, and holds over 1,000 United States heart cases and 20 hubs with 22 aircraft. Traferox's X°Port has 510(k) clearance for cold lung transport and its TorEx EVLP is used in two major Canadian centres pending FDA approval.

The two changes of the last six months. First, the largest customer (Lung Bioengineering, a United Therapeutics subsidiary; 132 of 2025 revenue, over 10 percent of the group) received approval for its own EVLP platform in Q3 2026 and Xvivo says sales to it will decline, with STEEN and some components remaining in the customer's platform at a lower value; a former United Therapeutics engineer attributes the move partly to the cost of working with Xvivo. DNB Carnegie had sized the exposure at 5 to 6 percent of 2027 revenue; management did not quantify the timing or size. Second, an October 2026 surgeon interview reports a centre running about 95 percent of its normothermic liver cases on OrganOx and 5 percent on TransMedics, chiefly on cost and ease, which shows how quickly local economics move share in liver.

Where intensity is not rising. Perfadex Plus holds about 90 percent of static lung preservation with patent cover to 2035, and nobody else has STEEN or the XPS kit. The generic-fluid pressure (SERVATOR P where original patents expired) is a pricing nuisance, not a share threat, although an Organ Recovery Systems specialist expects preservation-solution prices to be stable to slightly down because three suppliers each hold about a third of the United States solution market.

For the model. Treat abdominal pricing as flat to down in real terms, treat the United States heart launch as a share-taking exercise against an entrenched incumbent whose customers complain about price (an opening) but who owns the logistics and the digital layer (a block), and treat lung as defended on product but exposed on concentration.

C-03

Disruption forces: strengthening or disproved, and direction

C52

Question asked To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

Judgement. The headline disruption threats are being disproved as threats inside the forecast horizon and read as expanders; the live disruption is economic, not scientific, and it acts against Xvivo's pricing rather than its relevance. There is also one disruption working for Xvivo: the regulatory shortcut opened by a competitor's clearance.

Disproved or distant. Artificial organs are decades away on expert testimony; xenotransplantation (United Therapeutics' EXPRESS trial cleared in May 2026; a pig kidney explanted at 271 days) is 10 to 15 years from routine use and would still need perfusion to assess suitability, and Xvivo's fluids enabled both first United States pig-to-human transplants; cryopreservation of whole organs is 10 to 15 years away on a Paragonix engineer's view; BMI OrganBank's room-temperature kidney platform is preclinical. AI in perfusion is retrospective modelling only. Normothermic regional perfusion inside the donor is a complement that Xvivo now sells (first five United States NRP contracts in Q1 2026). None of these changes the 18-month to four-year numbers.

Strengthening, against pricing. The disruption that is real is the arrival of clinically adequate cheaper systems: hypothermic oxygenated perfusion at USD 15,000 to 20,000 a case (Bridge to Life), homemade ECMO-based circuits at UPMC and Mass General to avoid the USD 40,000 STEEN run, regulated cold transport (X°Port) where assessment is not needed. The pattern is "the cheapest clinically adequate system wins" when hospital budgets are flat, which supports Xvivo's category (cold perfusion) against TransMedics' warm platform and simultaneously commoditises Xvivo's own abdominal prices.

Strengthening, for Xvivo. Bridge to Life's January 2026 clearance created a legally marketed predicate for hypothermic liver perfusion, which is why Xvivo paused the 215-patient DeLIVER PMA trial and now plans a 510(k) submission in H1 2027 with an estimated early-2028 United States launch, against 2029 on the clinical route. That is a regulatory disruption worth roughly a year of United States liver revenue and the cost of a trial, if the FDA accepts comparability.

C-04

Political and regulatory change

C64

Question asked To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

Judgement. Regulatory change in the last six months has been the single largest positive development for the case, removing the European heart overhang and starting the United States clock, and political change (the OPO rule) is a demand tailwind; the residual regulatory risk is now about timing and payment rather than approval of the European product, and the Consumer should model regulators as supportive in substance and slow in time.

What has happened. CE mark for Heart Assist Transport on 11 August 2026 after a six-to-twelve-month delay caused by a perfusion-solution component change (ahead of DNB Carnegie's assumption of Q4). French national derogation (December 2025) and national reimbursement (18 May 2026), with Hôpital La Pitié-Salpêtrière reporting 11 transplants that would otherwise not have happened. FDA receipt of the heart PMA on 28 September 2026, supported by the 141-patient PRESERVE trial (92.1 percent 30-day success; 91.4 percent one-year survival), Breakthrough Device designation, a further 60-patient Continued Access Protocol with CMS cost recovery. The Florida court ruling of 28 September 2026 upholding the CMS OPO performance rule. Health Canada approvals for Liver Assist and Kidney Assist Transport. Management has said it will seek CMS coverage and sees the RAPID pathway for breakthrough devices as a route to faster Medicare access.

What has not. No FDA advisory panel date exists; RedEye expects a decision between late 2027 and early 2028 and the company now says launch is "targeted" for the end of 2027 "subject to FDA review and approval", narrower wording than before. Reimbursement codes exist in France but are still being negotiated in the Netherlands, Belgium and Austria; the sector rule of thumb is that revenue lags approval by about twelve months. United States liver devices are generally absorbed into the Medicare cost report rather than paid through a dedicated code, so a 510(k) is not a payment. Tariffs: nearly half of group sales are United States-made lung consumables and are shielded, but heart, liver and kidney disposables are European-made and DNB Carnegie flags the risk that tariffs cannot be fully offset by price. The OPO transition will create operational friction in late 2026 and early 2027 before it drives adoption.

C-05

Input costs, availability, supplier concentration

❌ D40

Question asked How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

Judgement. The supply side is a net headwind today and a planned tailwind from 2027 to 2028, and it has already cost revenue and margin in 2026 in ways the Consumer should carry: the manufacturing transfer constrained kidney and liver growth in Q3, inventory write-downs hit gross margin, and the company told investors it receives no volume discounts, so unit-cost gains must come from process learning over one to 1.5 years rather than from purchasing scale.

Structure. All commercial manufacturing is outsourced to a small network of Western European and United States subcontractors; lung XPS production is in the United States, heart, liver, kidney and disposables in Europe. The tenfold disposable-capacity project (about 50 of capital) completes in Q4 2026 and abdominal production moves from the Netherlands to Sweden; management promises lower cost of goods from 2027 and a 70 percent abdominal gross margin by 2027. Sterility prohibits substitution of materials or recycling; Dextran 40 is a critical input; supplier concentration and dual-sourcing ratios are not disclosed; logistics are outsourced to UPS and DSV.

Evidence of friction. Q3 abdominal growth slowed to 12 percent (kidney +19 percent from +72 percent in Q2) on "a core-component replacement and related regulatory process", with higher growth expected again only in early 2027; Q3 gross margin was hit by inventory write-downs; the heart CE mark was delayed by a component change; inventories reached 29 percent of sales in 2025 before unwinding to a net working capital of 5.6 percent of trailing revenue in Q3 2026. A strengthening krona also raises the SEK cost base relative to USD and EUR revenue, which is a supply-side cost effect as well as a translation effect.

For the model. Hold abdominal gross margin in the low 60s through 2027 with improvement in 2028; treat the Q4 2026 clean-room validation as a dated risk; treat cost of goods as a learning curve, not a step.

C-06

The company's own revenue and demand cycle

C56

Question asked Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

Judgement. The company's own cycle turned in Q4 2025, accelerated through Q3 2026, and is now at the point where the reported growth rate is at its most flattering and about to decelerate on arithmetic alone: comparatives harden from Q1 2027, the Q3 timing benefits (about 19) do not recur, the largest lung customer steps down, kidney is constrained until early 2027, and Services is still shrinking. The underlying engine (new lung accounts and hubs, European heart) is still rising, so this is a deceleration from an inflated peak, not a turn down.

The series. Reported revenue growth: +17.5 percent (Q1 2025), -15.2, -4.7, -0.6, +10.2, +34.0, +33.5 percent (Q3 2026); organic: 23 percent (Q1 2026), 36, 32. Two-year stacked growth on Q3 is about 13 percent a year, which is the honest run-rate once the 2025 trough is netted out. Heart under derogation went 9, 24, 22 a quarter. United States EVLP disposables +56, +69, +183 percent. Abdominal 24 (constant currency), 26, 12 percent. Services +11, -25, -28 percent.

What turns next. Q4 2026 needs about 262 of revenue to meet consensus FY2026 of 995 (+16 percent against Q4 2025's 226), which is achievable from a Q3 underlying base of about 234 plus Q4 seasonality and the first commercial European heart quarter. From Q1 2027 the comparatives are +10 to +34 percent quarters, the lung customer decline bites (DNB Carnegie's 5 to 6 percent of 2027 revenue is the only sizing on file), and the consensus lung line of 647 for FY2027 (+13 percent) requires new accounts to grow faster than 25 percent to offset it. The cycle therefore peaks in reported terms in H2 2026 and the question for FY2027 is replacement, not recovery.

C-07

Macro force: level at which it bites

❌ D33

Question asked To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

Judgement. The macro force that matters is currency, and it bites hardest at the company level because Xvivo is an outlier even within a sector of unhedged Nordic exporters: about 55 percent of sales in USD and 35 percent in EUR against a largely SEK cost base, zero hedging (no policy, no instruments), and a consensus macro view and a Riksbank now likely to hike in November 2026 that both point to a stronger krona. Second-order, the discount-rate regime bites at the sector level for a long-duration growth stock. Transplant demand itself is non-cyclical, so the macro force reaches reported earnings and the multiple, not volumes.

Sizing. A 10 percent USD/SEK move is about 45 of sales (5 percent of group), about 2 points of EBITDA margin (about 16 of operating profit) and about 0.36 of EPS; a 10 percent EUR/SEK move is about 10 of EBITDA. Financial items carried a -66.6 currency loss in 2025 and a +32.7 gain in 2024; Q3 2026 net profit included about +7.1 of exchange gains on cash, roughly 0.22 of the 1.42 EPS. Translation cut 2025 sales by 5 percent and Q1 2026 by 13 percent; Q3 2026 had +2 points. Intesa Sanpaolo forecasts USD/SEK 9.15 by end-2026 and 8.67 by late 2027 from 9.70 in July 2026, a path that would take roughly 5 to 10 percent off reported sales growth over the next 18 months.

The regime. The weekly environment note (16 October 2026) reads a discount-rate shock (United States 10-year about 5.1 percent, the highest since 2007), Fragility at Double Underweight, Sweden cut to Neutral on rates, and a market that "refuses re-ratings" and rewards simple self-funded cases. Xvivo is small (market capitalisation about 7.1 billion SEK), thinly traded (about USD 2.8 million a day), shorted (8 to 9.5 percent), and its return case has no cash leg, so it sits on the wrong side of every tilt in that note except that it is self-funded. Hospital budget pressure (staffing shortages; capital caps) is the one macro force on volumes, and it slows adoption at the margin.

C-08

Industry or sector key driver accelerating the company's demand

C68

Question asked To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

Judgement. Yes, strongly in lung and prospectively in heart: the industry's shift to DCD donors, the federal pressure on OPOs and the outsourcing of scarce clinical capacity are the drivers that produced Xvivo's +183 percent United States EVLP disposables in Q3 and the three activated OPO hubs, and the same drivers are why the heart system's European launch starts with eight centres already using it under derogation. The acceleration is through the company's hub-and-spoke route rather than through hospital capital purchases, which is the right route for a market whose constraint is labour rather than machines.

Mechanism and evidence. OPO hubs let a region evaluate marginal lungs without each hospital owning a USD 500,000 XPS and a trained team; UPMC raised utilisation 200 percent with on-site technical support; the first hub pilot (Indiana Donor Network with Perfusion Solution Inc) completed ten procedures, OneLegacy in California launched the first Western United States EVLP centre in July 2026, and management reports three activated hubs against a four-to-five target. The sector digest's own rule for this cohort is that "a trial, approval or payment decision creates the market" for a small maker with a new consumable: the CE mark and French reimbursement are exactly that event for heart.

Caveat. Management disclosed no procedure volumes or revenue from the hubs, the Oklahoma OPO precedent shows hubs can fail on low case volumes and staffing, and a Duke surgeon has yet to see organs sent to a third-party hub from his own centre. The driver is accelerating demand; the hubs' productivity is not yet evidenced in numbers.

C-09

Other material external dynamics

❌ D42

Question asked What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

Judgement. Two dynamics outside the usual categories matter. The negative one, which is the most important new external fact of the quarter, is customer vertical integration: the largest customer becoming a self-supplier in lung. The positive one is the strategic-buyer floor under the equity: two acquirers paid 11x and about 20x sales for Xvivo's competitors in the last two years, and the market now reads Xvivo as the only listed multi-organ perfusion platform, which bounds the downside in a way the earnings do not.

Customer insourcing. Lung Bioengineering (United Therapeutics) was 132 of 2025 revenue, built about 10 of inventory in Q3 2026 and will reduce purchases as it moves to its own approved EVLP platform. United Therapeutics has a nationwide network of procurement surgeons, supports hundreds of transplants, and is interested in proprietary CLES technology, so it has the means to compete on the device and the service layer, not just to stop buying. The exposure is partly cushioned (STEEN and other components stay in the approved platform) and partly unknown (timing and size unquantified by the company). For the model this is a revenue step-down in lung during 2027 of a size the Consumer must choose, with DNB Carnegie's 5 to 6 percent of 2027 group revenue as the only published anchor.

Strategic floor. Terumo paid about USD 1.5 billion (about 20x sales) for OrganOx; Getinge USD 477 million (11x sales) for Paragonix. Xvivo's enterprise value is 6,849, about 6.9x FY2026 consensus sales. This does not help the earnings model but it matters for the trough-multiple question and for the sell-side's willingness to stay at Buy.

Also present, smaller. Short interest of 8 to 9.5 percent of shares as squeeze fuel on regulatory news; the RedEye analyst's disclosed share ownership; the European medtech pattern that worries clear through multiple expansion at range lows.

C-10

Each thematic driver in turn, then all together

C57

Question asked Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

Judgement by level.

  • Value chain. Tailwinds: donors (DCD mix), OPOs (decertification pressure, hubs), surgeons (clinical evidence; cost pushback against TransMedics), European payers (France). Headwinds: hospital budgets and staffing (adoption ceiling), the largest customer insourcing, competitors funded by Terumo and Getinge and Bridge to Life, cold-solution price stability. Net: moderately positive on volume, negative on price and on lung concentration.
  • Industry (organ preservation and machine perfusion). Tailwinds: 7 percent market growth to USD 2.9 billion at 4 percent penetration; every competitor growing 37 to 65 percent; the "10x value" of a perfusion run over static storage; the 510(k) predicate opening. Headwinds: consolidation into giants; a technology race where the cheapest adequate system wins; regulators slow. Net: positive.
  • Sector (medtech consumables) and market. Tailwinds: the sector sits at the 2nd percentile of its own valuation range and worries clear through multiple expansion; healthcare is receiving money leaving technology. Headwinds: a discount-rate shock, Fragility Double Underweight, Sweden tightening, a market refusing re-ratings, unhedged currency into a stronger krona. Net: negative for the multiple, neutral for the business.

All together. The external picture is a strong structural demand tailwind at the industry level, partly offset by a competitive and pricing headwind at the value-chain level and by a macro and currency headwind at the company and market level. The demand tailwind has two-plus years of visibility and the headwinds are already in evidence, so the shape for the model is revenue growth that is easier to believe than margin expansion, and reported SEK figures that will understate constant-currency delivery if the krona strengthens as forecast. The single external event that would change this picture most is the FDA's handling of the heart PMA; the single external fact most likely to be under-modelled is the lung customer's step-down.

The internal change forces are real, broad and resourced but execution-dependent and unevenly delivered: the product and market entries (European heart commercialisation, the United States heart filing, the liver 510(k) route, kidney expansion, OPO hubs) are the engine and are moving; the standards and leadership changes (cost programme, finance director redeployed to services, board refresh) are the right responses to the 2025 setbacks; but there is no chief-executive change, no raised guidance, no pricing lever, no capital return, a loss-making services leg that the strategy now depends on, an unfilled CFO seat and a customer turning into a competitor. The Consumer should model an investment-led company whose revenue leg is driven by its own launches and whose margin leg is a promise the record has supported in single quarters (Q4 2025 at 25 percent; Q3 2026 at 21 percent) and not yet in a year.

C-11

Changes in chief executive, key leaders, board, major shareholders

❌ D44

Question asked To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

Judgement. No change at chief executive (Christoffer Rosenblad since November 2022, an internal operator-track successor after the founder-era chief executive's death), and the one material executive change, the finance director's redeployment to run strategy and the United States services unit from 1 April 2026, signals both the right diagnosis (United States commercial execution is the weak point) and an unresolved cost (the CFO seat has had no named successor for eight months during the most launch-intensive year in the company's history). The board refresh is a modest positive. Net: a change that recognises the problem without yet proving it is fixed.

The facts. Kristoffer Nordström became Senior Vice President Corporate Strategy and Business Development on 1 April 2026, retaining the CFO title until a successor is appointed; the search has run since February 2026; the Q3 report still names him CFO and principal investor-relations contact. Experts rate him "very good" and his presentations "exceptionally clear", so the redeployment is of a strong executive, but one executive now carries finance, strategy, business development and the services turnaround. The April 2026 AGM elected Emil Billbäck (former Bonesupport chief executive, United States launch experience) and Anne-Karen Hunt (Demant), replacing two long-serving directors; both are independent of major shareholders. Earlier, the North American commercial leadership "underperformed and was let go"; Mark Reade (two decades in devices) leads North America. The Q2 2026 call spoke of 10 to 13 additional United States commercial hires for the heart launch; headcount rose to 207 in Q3 from 197 in Q2 and about 193 in June 2025, not broken down by function. No activist; Bure Equity (14.27 percent, chairman dependent on it), AP4 (8.7 percent) and Eccenovo (5.7 percent) together control the nomination committee with 29 percent of votes.

What it signals. Management acts rather than denies: the service failure got the company's best operator. But the leadership report calls the permanent CFO succession "the single most important execution risk over the next 12 months", the employee net promoter score fell from 51 to 27 in 2025, and the Q3 report names no owner for the United States commercial launch. For the model: no change in strategic direction from leadership; a modest risk to financial-control quality and to forecast discipline while the seat is shared.

C-12

Change in strategy or strategic priorities; articulation and resourcing

C54

Question asked To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

Judgement. The strategy has broadened materially in the last twelve months, from a device-and-consumables vendor into "the preferred partner throughout transplantation", combining lung leadership with heart and liver launches, kidney expansion, OPO hubs, NRP services, FlowHawk and a scaled supply chain, with 2027 framed as "a springboard to 2028". It is well articulated in operational terms and visibly resourced; it is not yet articulated in financial terms (no bridge from the building blocks to the 2027 targets, no hub economics), and the one leg that has been running longest, organ-recovery services, is failing. The Consumer should model the strategy as raising the revenue pool and the investment intensity together, and pushing dependable earnings conversion into 2027 to 2028.

Articulation. The operational plan is specific: four to five OPO hubs (three activated); 175 perfusionists through Perfusion Solution Inc; tenfold disposable capacity by Q4 2026 with abdominal manufacturing consolidated to Sweden; a three-tier European heart rollout (eight existing centres, then about 30 priority centres with funding pathways in France, the Netherlands, Austria and the United Kingdom, then regional expansion); a United States heart launch targeted for end-2027; a Liver Assist 510(k) in H1 2027 for an early-2028 launch; broader United States kidney launch in 2027. The "10x value" framing (a perfusion run's sales value is about ten times static storage) explains the economics. Volume-based incentives (perfusion volume growth of at least 12 percent a year, twice the market) were added to the long-term plan.

Resourcing. Nine-month 2026 investment was 171 (138 intangibles, mostly regulatory programmes; 31 property, plant and equipment); operating expenditure keeps rising with headcount; the EUR 20 million facility (EUR 8 million drawn) and 313 of cash fund it. Management confirmed at Q3 that investment in commercial capabilities, infrastructure and supply chain continues despite the 21 percent EBITDA margin.

Where it is weak. Services revenue fell 28 percent organically in Q3 with a -19 percent gross margin after six quarters of decline; contract conversions "are taking longer than originally anticipated"; FlowHawk grew 0 percent in Q3 against 48 percent in Q2 without explanation; partnership economics (revenue shares, minimum volumes) are undisclosed. A strategy that spans more of the transplant process is also a strategy with more simultaneous execution risk, which is why the memo's riskiness tests mark it down.

C-13

Guidance raised, mid-term targets set or raised

❌ D38

Question asked To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

Judgement. Nothing has been raised. The company gives no numerical guidance; its 2027 targets (EBITDA margin above 30 percent, EBIT margin above 20 percent, abdominal gross margin 70 percent) were reaffirmed, not raised, in July 2026 and were not repeated with new numbers at Q3; the only quantitative steer in 2026 was that FY2026 EBITDA should land in line with consensus. The sell-side already sits below the 2027 targets (DNB Carnegie 26.6 percent EBITDA and 17.2 percent EBIT; FactSet median 26.8 percent EBITDA), so the targets function as ambition rather than as a guidance floor, and there is no capital-markets-day mechanism to reset them. This is not a force for the case; it is an absence that leaves the sell-side to model the heart line unaided.

C-14

Standards improving: execution, operations, governance, cost discipline

❌ D48

Question asked To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

Judgement. Standards are improving in cost discipline and planning and deteriorating in the commercial execution and people metrics, so the net is mixed and the Consumer should credit the operating-leverage evidence while refusing to extrapolate it. The specific improvements are real: the H2 2025 cost and cash programme produced a record 25 percent adjusted EBITDA margin in Q4 2025 and three consecutive quarters of positive operating cash flow (65, 63.5, 53); rolling forecasts replaced annual budgets; the United States field force was restructured toward the South and West; supply-chain resilience became a named strategic focus area; the Q3 2026 EBITDA margin of 21 percent on a 70 percent gross margin shows operating costs were held while revenue rose 34 percent. The specific deteriorations are also real: Services gross margin -19 percent against +37 percent a year earlier; inventory write-downs in Q3; FlowHawk at 0 percent growth; the employee net promoter score from 51 to 27 and commitment from 4.16 to 4.04; no turnover or absenteeism disclosure; surgeons lost to rivals for lack of regional logistics support. Governance is clean (unqualified audit, single share class, no restatements, zero whistleblower cases) but has not changed. Management attributed part of the Q3 margin to seasonally lower summer activity and said investment continues, so the Consumer should not take 21 percent as the new run-rate.

C-15

Perimeter or composition reduction

❌ D34

Question asked To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

Judgement. None, and the direction is the opposite: the company is adding lower-margin activities (organ-recovery services, NRP, hub operations) rather than exiting the one unit that has lost money for six quarters. No divestments, no simplification, no exits in the last twelve months; the services unit is now framed as launch infrastructure for the United States heart product, which makes an exit less likely, not more. For predictability this is negative: the group's gross margin will keep carrying a labour-heavy node with 18 to 33 percent industry margins and currently negative margins. The only perimeter reduction on record is the 2023 PrimECC write-down. Neutral-to-negative for the case.

C-16

Entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly

C72

Question asked To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

Judgement. This is the strongest internal force and it is being executed sensibly, in a staged way that trades speed for lower launch risk. The heart system is the largest product entry in the company's history and is now in commercial launch in Europe and under regulatory review in the United States; liver has a credible United States route for the first time; kidney has Canada and a broader United States launch in 2027; the hub model opened the Western United States. The sensibleness shows in the three-tier European heart rollout (centres with funding pathways first), the pivot to a 510(k) for liver after a predicate appeared, and the use of partners rather than an owned fleet. The cost is that the staged approach means a gradual revenue build rather than a step, which the Consumer must respect: compassionate and derogation sales were 7 percent of H1 2026 revenue, heart was 22 in Q3, consensus has heart at 92.5 for FY2026 (implying about 37 in Q4), 181 in FY2027 and 336 in FY2028; Australia's 40 to 50 percent penetration of brain-death heart transplants is the proof that adoption can be fast where support is strong, and the European trial network (15 clinics, about 25 percent of European heart centres) is the installed base. Geographic entry is mostly within existing footprints (United States South and West; French reimbursement; Canada) rather than new countries, which keeps the perimeter manageable.

C-17

Real price rises: real, nominal, or latent

❌ D40

Question asked To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

Judgement. Price is a defensive lever at present, not a growth lever: rises in 2026 have been nominal offsets (tariffs, a weak dollar) and in abdominal the company has given price back through mix and "temporary price pressure in key markets"; the one real pricing opportunity, converting European heart sales from compassionate-use pricing to commercial pricing after CE mark, is latent and will show in thoracic gross margin from Q4 2026. The record: pricing actions held disposable gross margins at 84 percent through 2023's inflation (real pricing power in lung, applied); in Q1 2026 management "successfully increased prices to offset tariffs and a weaker United States dollar" (nominal); in Q2 abdominal gross margin fell to 57 percent partly on pricing conditions (negative); thoracic gross margin fell to 80 percent in Q3 from 89 percent on a higher machine share of sales (mix, not price). Experts call STEEN at about USD 7,000 a litre and Perfadex "too expensive" for high-volume clinics, and cold-preservation solution prices are expected flat to slightly down. Management has not noted pricing as a lever in its commentary beyond the tariff offset. For the model: real price contribution near zero in lung, negative in abdominal through 2027, positive in heart as compassionate converts to commercial.

C-18

Mergers and acquisitions step-up

❌ D44

Question asked To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

Judgement. No step-up; the acquisition phase (Organ Assist 2020, which created the abdominal segment; STAR Teams 2021, costly to integrate; Avionord 2022; FlowHawk late 2024 for 57) is over and growth is organic (acquired growth 1 percent in 2025, none in 2026). The April 2026 AGM buyback authorisation was framed as facilitating possible asset acquisitions, but no transaction, joint venture or disposal appears in the Q3 materials and capital is going to organic launches. The integration record is mixed: Organ Assist worked, STAR Teams cost 22 of one-offs and the services unit it created is the group's weakest, FlowHawk is "an option rather than a proven EPS contributor" with 0 percent Q3 growth. Not a force; a neutral with a slightly negative integration history.

C-19

Use of the balance sheet, shareholder returns

❌ D36

Question asked To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

Judgement. The balance sheet is being used for one thing, growth investment, and nothing is mooted on returns: no dividend ever, no buyback programme, consensus dividend zero through FY2028, and the EUR 20 million revolver (EUR 8 million drawn, maturing January 2028) funds working capital. Net cash is about 193 on FactSet's definition (313 cash less 121 debt including leases) and the equity ratio is 87 percent. Consensus capital expenditure of 210 / 226 / 240 / 245 for FY2026 to FY2029 (21 to 15 percent of revenue, against peers at 0 to 8 percent) says the balance sheet will be drawn down rather than returned: consensus free cash flow is -99 in FY2026, 0 in FY2027, 103 in FY2028. The board's authority to issue up to 10 percent without a vote and the incentive plans' up to 1.35 percent dilution are the only equity actions in prospect. For a reinvesting launch-stage company this is the right choice; as a change force it is absent.

C-20

Other material positive corporate change forces

C58

Question asked What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

Judgement. Three positive forces not captured above are plausible and dated. First, the liver 510(k) route: a submission in H1 2027 and an early-2028 United States launch, roughly a year earlier and materially cheaper than the 215-patient trial, not yet in any broker's formal numbers (RedEye calls it upside it has not capitalised). Second, the end of the capitalisation cycle: nine-month 2026 capitalised development of 131.7 (primarily heart and liver regulatory work) should fall once the heart PMA is decided and the liver route is set, which is the mechanism by which operating cash flow (182 in nine months) becomes free cash flow. Third, mix conversion in heart: shifting existing compassionate-use programmes to commercial pricing "will instantly improve product gross margins" (DNB Carnegie), and consensus heart revenue of 92.5 for FY2026 implies the step begins in Q4 2026. A fourth, smaller: the abdominal manufacturing consolidation lowers unit cost from 2027 over a one-to-1.5-year learning curve.

C-86

Negative corporate change developments present or plausible

❌ D30

Question asked What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

Judgement. The negative developments are concrete and several are new this quarter. (1) The largest customer's approved in-house EVLP platform and the coming decline in sales to it, unquantified. (2) Services at -28 percent organic with a -19 percent gross margin and contracts converting slower than planned, now the sixth quarter of decline. (3) The CFO seat unfilled after eight months while the same executive runs the services turnaround. (4) FlowHawk growth collapsing to 0 percent in Q3 from 48 percent, unexplained. (5) Kidney growth constrained by a component replacement until early 2027 and European liver growth moderating as the business matures. (6) A new SEK 39 a year amortisation of the CE-mark asset over ten years, which lowers EBIT and EPS even if EBITDA grows as planned, plus 131.7 of nine-month capitalisation that will amortise. (7) Inventory write-downs and a thoracic gross margin down nine points year on year on machine mix. (8) Employee sentiment down sharply. (9) Plausible: a further regulatory slip (every date in the record has slipped), an FDA request for more heart data, FDA refusal of the liver 510(k) comparability. None is fatal (net cash; a growing lung engine) but together they are the "false friend" shape the risks report identified: a revenue beat coinciding with earnings-quality erosion.

C-87

Controlling actors indicating scope for strategy change

❌ D48

Question asked To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

Judgement. None indicated. Bure Equity (14.27 percent; its representative chairs the board and the remuneration committee; another leads the nomination committee), AP4 (8.7 percent) and Eccenovo (5.7 percent) exert coordinated governance through the nomination committee rather than public demands; there is no activist, no proxy contest in three years, and Swedish institutions hold over 35 percent of votes. The 2026 board additions brought United States commercial experience, which is consistent with the current strategy rather than a change of it. The one scenario to hold is a placing or sale by Bure, which would be read as a signal; low probability. For the model: strategy continuity, no external pressure for a sale, a buyback or an exit from services.

C-21

Together: strength, breadth and resourcing of positive corporate change

C52

Question asked Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

Judgement. The positive corporate changes are broad (four organs, three segments, two continents, products and services and manufacturing), well resourced (171 of nine-month investment, a growing field force, a tenfold capacity project, a strong partner network) and, in the product-entry leg, strong, with the heart launch in Europe and the United States filing as the two largest company-delivered events in its history. They are not uniformly strong: there is no leadership change to drive them, no guidance raised, no pricing or capital-return lever, and the services leg has consumed investment for six quarters without output. The honest characterisation is a launch programme riding an external demand inflection, not a transformation, and its strength is concentrated in the thoracic product line where the company's advantages are deepest. For the model: the internal forces justify revenue growth of 20 percent-plus for three years; they do not justify assuming the 2027 margin targets, because the same forces keep operating and launch costs high through 2027 and add a loss-making services node.

Industry and Value Chain

❌ D4616 questions
C-2455C-2564C-2648C-2752C-2842C-2936C-3146C-8454C-3257C-3346C-3437C-3534C-3642C-3762C-3846C-3940

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The industry structure is a set of per-organ near-monopolies behind regulatory and switching-cost barriers, with a demand side that is pulling machine perfusion into use for reasons outside Xvivo's control; that supports the lung consumables engine completely and the European liver and heart franchises substantially. The same structure is contested wherever the sell-side's 2027 to 2029 numbers need Xvivo to win: in United States heart against an entrenched, aggressive, logistics-owning incumbent; in United States liver and kidney against funded, cheaper or dominant rivals; and now in lung from a customer that has built its own platform. Competitor attitude has turned sharper over 18 months, the device-layer barrier has fallen through 510(k) predicates, the route to market through Xvivo's own services is failing, and suppliers are a drag until 2027. The sector's precedents say approvals create the market a year later, worries clear through multiple expansion at range lows, and capitalised-cost margins are the sector's characteristic false friend; Xvivo fits all three. The net for the model: volumes supported, price flat to down outside lung fluids, margins contested, and the competitive picture argues for an EBITDA margin that settles in the mid-to-high 20s rather than above 30 percent within the four-year horizon unless United States heart share arrives at TransMedics' expense.

C-24

Market rank and distance from number one or two

C55

Question asked Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

Judgement. Xvivo is number one where it has been for two decades and a challenger everywhere the price now pays for. Lung: Perfadex Plus holds about 90 percent of static lung preservation globally with patent cover to 2035 and is used by over 90 percent of lung programmes; the XPS with STEEN is the only established warm EVLP platform, although United States EVLP penetration is only 10 to 20 percent of transplants. European liver: Liver Assist has over 50 percent share of European liver perfusion (company, Q3 2026) at about 25 percent penetration of core markets; Italy runs 20 to 25 percent machine perfusion penetration via the Avionord service model. United States heart: not top two; TransMedics has over 1,000 United States heart cases and the national logistics network; Paragonix holds the static container segment. United States liver: not top two; TransMedics holds 40 to 75 percent of United States liver perfusion and OrganOx is taking share on cost; Bridge to Life is cleared. United States kidney: Organ Recovery Systems' LifePort holds about 95 percent. By revenue Xvivo (812 in 2025) is about one-seventh of TransMedics (USD 605 million) and a fraction of Terumo and Getinge. The distance to number one in the United States contested organs is large in installed base and logistics; it is small in clinical evidence (heart) and price (all organs).

C-25

Market concentration and steepness

C64

Question asked How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

Judgement. Highly concentrated and steep in every organ, which protects incumbents, including Xvivo in lung, and raises the bar for its entries elsewhere. The top five hold well over 30 percent in each organ market; in practice the top one to three hold nearly all of each: Perfadex about 90 percent of static lung preservation, LifePort about 95 percent of United States kidney perfusion, TransMedics 40 to 75 percent of United States liver perfusion plus 21 percent of all United States transplants through its National OCS Program, Liver Assist over 50 percent of European liver perfusion. The tail is generic fluids, homemade circuits and small entrants. The distribution is steeper than five-to-one between the top five and the next five. For the model: share gains come from displacing a dominant incumbent organ by organ, so adoption curves should be modelled centre by centre, not as a diffuse share drift.

C-26

Share stability and new entrants' gains

❌ D48

Question asked How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

Judgement. Stable for two decades in lung fluids, volatile and fast-moving in the perfusion device layer. New entrants have taken meaningful share in three to five years: TransMedics went from niche to 21 percent of United States transplants with an owned aviation fleet; OrganOx grew about 50 percent a year and now wins 95 percent of normothermic liver cases at some centres on cost; Paragonix grew 65 percent organic and went to five organs; Bridge to Life went from clearance in January 2026 to repeat orders, master service agreements with centres and OPOs, NICE funding and a USD 110 million raise by August 2026. Xvivo's own lung share is stable in fluids and now contested on the platform by its largest customer and by Traferox. For the model: share in a given organ can move 10 to 20 points in two years where a cheaper adequate device appears; share in lung fluids does not move.

C-27

Consolidation toward the largest and toward the most advantaged

C52

Question asked To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

Judgement. Share consolidates toward the most advantaged platform per organ, and the advantage that wins is economics plus logistics rather than size or incumbency: the largest player by revenue (TransMedics) is losing liver share to the cheaper OrganOx and faces pricing pushback in heart, while the largest by installed base in lung fluids (Xvivo) keeps its share because nobody has an equivalent product. Xvivo is among the advantaged in lung (product, evidence, hubs) and in heart on paper (a third of TransMedics' price; the only randomised trial linked to survival; a simpler cold workflow), and is not yet among the advantaged in United States liver and kidney, where cheaper or entrenched alternatives exist. The direction of consolidation therefore favours Xvivo's heart entry if it executes commercially, and disfavours its abdominal pricing.

C-28

Competitors' attitude: margin or volume first; benign or loss-tolerant; prices and volume

❌ D42

Question asked What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

Judgement. Margin-first at the top of each organ market and volume-first among the entrants, with no loss-making price war in Xvivo's core. TransMedics prices at USD 80,000 to 100,000 a case and protects that price, but invests in a fleet that pulled its gross margin to about 58 percent; OrganOx under Terumo runs a 15 percent margin with a 20 percent target, so it is volume-first within a margin frame; Bridge to Life prices to disrupt at USD 15,000 to 20,000; Paragonix and Getinge are margin-conscious consolidators. Prices do not fall when volume falls in lung fluids (2023 pricing held 84 percent margins through inflation; cold-solution prices are stable), but Xvivo's own abdominal price fell with the emerging-market kidney mix in 2026 and NICE funding for a cheaper rival pressures European liver price. The attitude is not benign: TransMedics publicly attacks cold perfusion and launched CHOPS as a blocking product; surgeons are poached. For the model: no price war, but no pricing tailwind outside lung fluids, and a structurally lower price point in abdominal than in thoracic.

C-29

Stability of competitor attitude; O'Leary instability; shark-attack risk

❌ D36

Question asked How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

Judgement. The attitude has become less stable and more aggressive over the last 18 months to three years, and one actor shows the reactive, situation-dependent instability the question names. TransMedics is that actor: a founder-led, "notoriously aggressive" company that absorbed a short-seller attack (January 2025, -49 percent in three months), a 51.6 percent EPS miss and a 25 percent fall in May 2026, and responds with product blocks (CHOPS) and public clinical attacks on rivals; its behaviour after an FDA approval for Xvivo's heart is unpredictable. The consolidators (Terumo, Getinge) are predictable and well-capitalised. The shark-attack risk is concentrated in liver, where Bridge to Life's price point and funding could crush category pricing if it senses weakness at Xvivo's United States entry; it is low in lung, where nobody else has the fluid. The trend across the period: two acquisitions by giants, one low-cost clearance, one customer going in-house, one incumbent entering cold storage, all since September 2024.

C-31

How supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile

❌ D46

Question asked Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

Judgement. The structure supports Xvivo where the case's proven engine is and contests it where the price's expected value is. Supportive: per-organ concentration, PMA and EU MDR barriers that shut out sub-scale entrants, USD 500,000 hardware and USD 40,000-per-run switching costs, a clinical-evidence adoption loop at academic centres, and a demand side that is pulling perfusion into use. Fragile: the barrier that protected the device layer has been lowered by 510(k) predicates for hypothermic perfusion (which cut both ways: faster for Xvivo's liver, faster for its rivals), the consolidators have distribution Xvivo cannot match, the largest customer is becoming a supplier, and the industry's economics are moving toward the cheapest adequate system. The support is therefore robust in lung fluids, conditional in heart (the structure lets a better-evidenced, cheaper entrant win share, if it can supply the logistics), and fragile in abdominal.

C-84

Precedents and rules of thumb for outperformance in this sector; fit

C54

Question asked What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

Judgement. The sector's history, as the Sector and Industry Digests and the peer files record it, says four things about how companies like Xvivo outperform and underperform, and Xvivo fits three of them in a way the Consumer should use.

  1. An approval or a payment decision creates the market for a small maker with one new consumable, and revenue lags the approval by about a year. Bonesupport's SOLARIO trial and Medicare payment change produced +55.8 percent over three months and 31 percent United States growth before the payment change; Xvivo's heart CE mark and French reimbursement are the same event type, with a twelve-month lag to meaningful revenue (RedEye's 87 to 182 for 2026 to 2027). The fit is strong; the lesson is to model the revenue a year after the approval, not at it.
  2. At the bottom of its range a franchise's multiple can rise without an earnings upgrade as each worry clears, as long as the business underneath is intact; a results beat alone does not lift the share price. European medtech typically rebounds through multiple expansion after a major sell-off (Morgan Stanley); Xvivo is at the 1st percentile of its ten-year P/E range and the 6th percentile of its five-year 24-month-forward range, with the CE mark cleared and the PMA accepted, so it fits the "worries clearing" pattern; the sector note rates that story "big" for reimbursed franchises and "early" for consumables becoming the standard of care. The lesson from Alcon (price fell after a beat and raise) is that the first beat is not rewarded; the first beat-and-raise is. Xvivo has no guidance to raise, which weakens the fit.
  3. The most likely failure in healthcare is a reassuring headline margin built on capitalised costs, under-investment or one-off income. The Industry Digest names Xvivo (152.8 of capitalised development, 19 percent of sales) alongside Coloplast, GVS and Eckert & Ziegler. Xvivo fits this underperformance pattern too, and the Q3 EPS (1.42 with FX gains, lease recognition and customer inventory) is an instance.
  4. Outperformance within the cohort came from penetration growth plus margin delivery; underperformance from one-customer dependence and regulatory slippage. Xvivo's own history: 2023 to 2024 outperformance on EVLP growth and margin expansion (EBITDA margin 13 to 21 percent; revenue +44 and +38 percent); 2025 underperformance (-64 percent in the year) on one customer's destocking and a six-to-twelve-month CE delay. Ambu's turnaround (EBIT margin 2.7 to 13 percent, re-rated) shows the sector pays for margin delivery once growth is believed; Vitrolife (SEK 9.7 billion of impairments; CEO departure; trough multiple) shows what the sector does to capital-allocation failure and serial target misses.

Fit, summarised. Xvivo fits the "approval creates the market" and "worries clearing at a range low" outperformance patterns and the "capitalised-cost false friend" and "single customer, slipped date" underperformance patterns, simultaneously. The empirical base rate the change-forces report cites is also relevant: three-plus well-funded competitors tend to compress EBITDA margins below 20 percent within five years, which argues against the 30 percent target in abdominal-heavy mixes.

C-32

Customer demand: support for the thesis, and fragility

C57

Question asked How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

Judgement. Customer demand supports the thesis strongly in lung and in European heart and is fragile in three specific ways. Support: United States EVLP disposables +183 percent in Q3 (+92 percent year to date); three OPO hubs activated with "an important platform for expansion beyond the largest customer"; heart 22 to 24 a quarter under derogation with Australia at 40 to 50 percent penetration and France reimbursed; kidney adoption at elite centres (Mayo, Northwestern) and +72 percent in Q2 before the component constraint; clinicians prefer the cheaper, simpler cold workflow and a Vanderbilt cardiologist expects Xvivo to "transform" heart preservation. Fragility: (1) concentration, one customer at over 10 percent of revenue which has now insourced its platform; (2) cost, the USD 40,000 run and flat reimbursement cap penetration at 20 to 30 percent of transplants on current economics and hospitals prefer physician-led processes to company-driven services; (3) surgeon conservatism and limited long-term head-to-head heart data against TransMedics, which makes adoption centre by centre. Abdominal demand is positive but moderating (12 percent in Q3) and Services demand is the clearest failure (-28 percent; contracts slow to convert). For the model: lung demand ex the largest customer growing 25 to 35 percent; heart building progressively; abdominal mid-teens to 20 percent after the constraint lifts; services flat at best through mid-2027.

C-33

Route to market and intermediaries: help or hindrance, and fragility

❌ D46

Question asked To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

Judgement. The route to market is being rebuilt around intermediaries (OPOs, Perfusion Solution Inc's 175 perfusionists, Lung Bioengineering as a centralised EVLP provider, Avionord-style service bundles in Italy) and the rebuild is helping in lung, hindering in services, and untested in heart. Helping: the hub model bypasses hospital capital and staffing limits and is the mechanism behind the lung recovery; the French reimbursement and the eight existing heart centres give a direct channel. Hindering: Xvivo's own organ-recovery service, the route it chose to control United States adoption, lost East Coast accounts to physician-led programmes hiring internal teams, carries expanded surgical capacity ahead of volume, and converts contracts slowly; TransMedics' one-call concierge model remains the route hospitals prefer where budgets allow. Fragility: the largest intermediary customer is now a competitor; the OPO transition in late 2026 and January 2027 can disrupt the hub route for two quarters; distributor markets (9 percent of sales) add lumpiness; European heart depends on country registrations and reimbursement negotiations outside France. For the model: route-to-market cost (selling expenses 37 percent of sales in 2025) stays high through 2027.

C-34

Existing competitors' behaviour: help or hurt, fragility of position

❌ D37

Question asked How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

Judgement. Existing competitors hurt Xvivo in every United States organ it is entering and help it only by overpricing. TransMedics owns the United States heart and liver workflow (OCS, National OCS Program, 22 aircraft, NOP Connect portal and scheduling), attacks cold perfusion clinically, extends into cold storage (CHOPS) and into Xvivo's digital gap; its USD 80,000 to 100,000 price is the opening Xvivo's third-of-the-price proposition exploits, and some centres now restrict OCS to complex cases. OrganOx under Terumo wins liver on local economics and is funded to scale; Bridge to Life is cheaper still and newly capitalised; Organ Recovery Systems holds United States kidney. Paragonix (Getinge) holds static containers across five organs. In lung, the incumbents are Xvivo itself and, now, its own customer. Fragility: in lung the position is strong on product and weak on concentration; in heart it is strong on evidence and price and weak on logistics and installed base; in abdominal it is weak on price and distribution. The competitors' behaviour does not threaten the lung cash engine; it threatens the margin the sell-side expects from heart and abdominal in the United States.

C-35

New entrants by type and threat level

❌ D34

Question asked To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

Judgement. All three entrant types are present, and the orthogonal one is the most dangerous because it is a customer. Adjacent entrants: Terumo (via OrganOx) and Getinge (via Paragonix, now with KidneyVault) bring hospital procurement relationships and distribution; Strata Critical Medical bundles air and ground transport, recovery, NRP and perfusion staffing and says it competes directly with device makers that bundle services; threat high to abdominal pricing and to the services unit. Standard entrants: Bridge to Life's VitaSmart (cleared, funded, NICE-backed) and generic fluids; threat high in liver, low in lung. Orthogonal entrants: United Therapeutics/Lung Bioengineering's own EVLP platform (the largest customer), Traferox's TorEx and X°Port, hospital-built ECMO circuits, BMI OrganBank's room-temperature kidney platform (preclinical); threat high and immediate from the customer, medium from Traferox, low from the rest inside the horizon. The entry barriers that remain high are the fluids' formulations and the PMA for high-risk dynamic devices; the barrier that has fallen is the 510(k) predicate for hypothermic devices.

C-36

Substitutes and alternatives; how changing

❌ D42

Question asked To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

Judgement. The primary substitute is still free static cold storage, used by 90 percent of United States centres and adequate for standard-risk organs, and it is being eroded slowly by the DCD mix shift rather than by product. The secondary substitutes are multiplying and getting cheaper: regulated cold transport without assessment (Traferox X°Port, Paragonix containers, TransMedics CHOPS), normothermic regional perfusion in the donor (a complement that can reduce the need for ex-vivo assessment in some DCD cases and which Xvivo now also sells), and homemade perfusion circuits at large academic centres. The direction of change is toward more clinically adequate, lower-cost alternatives for the standard case, which keeps Xvivo's premium products confined to the marginal-organ and long-distance cases where their value is clearest. For the model this is the mechanism behind the penetration ceiling: perfusion wins the marginal 20 to 30 percent of organs first and the standard-risk majority only if its price falls.

C-37

Regulators and political actors: help or hurt, fragility

C62

Question asked What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

Judgement. Regulators and political actors are helping, decisively in the last six months, and the help is fragile only in timing and payment. Helping: the CE mark; French reimbursement; FDA PMA acceptance with Breakthrough designation and an expanded Continued Access Protocol with CMS cost recovery; Health Canada approvals; the OPO rule upheld in court; the 2019 Advancing American Kidney Health Order; CPT codes for lung and heart perfusion; a 510(k) predicate for liver; NICE funding for the category (even if for a rival). Hurting or fragile: FDA review length (RedEye's twelve months, late 2027 to early 2028; the TransMedics OCS Heart precedent took years), no advisory panel scheduled, no post-approval Medicare coverage mechanism beyond management's intention to pursue RAPID, reimbursement outside France unresolved, private insurers that still call perfusion experimental in places, tariffs on European-made disposables, and an OPO transition that will disrupt before it helps. For the model: Europe heart revenue from Q4 2026, United States heart revenue not before 2028, United States liver not before 2028, and no dedicated United States payment code assumed.

C-38

On whom disruption forces act, how, and the fragility they leave

❌ D46

Question asked On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

Judgement. Disruption is acting on four actors and leaves Xvivo most fragile where it is most concentrated. On demand: the OPO rule disrupts the customer base (nearly half of OPOs face decertification; agreements expire January 2027), which lifts long-run adoption and creates two quarters of contracting chaos; large OPOs may internalise support functions as they consolidate, which can bypass Xvivo's services even as it raises device demand. On competition: the customer-as-entrant in lung, and cheap hypothermic devices in liver, disrupt Xvivo's pricing assumption. On regulators: the 510(k) predicate disrupts the PMA barrier, in Xvivo's favour for liver and against it as a barrier. On suppliers: the manufacturing transfer and tenfold capacity project disrupt Xvivo's own supply for two to three quarters. On the company itself: a staged European heart launch and an approval-gated United States launch mean the growth that replaces the lung customer's decline arrives on regulators' dates, not Xvivo's. The resulting fragility is a 2027 in which lung concentration unwinds while the replacement revenue is timed by others.

C-39

Suppliers: support or risk

❌ D40

Question asked Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

Judgement. A source of risk now and a planned support later. Risk: fully outsourced, single-source disposable kits, a critical Dextran 40 input, no volume discounts, cost savings that take 12 to 18 months, a Q4 2026 clean-room validation and a Netherlands-to-Sweden transfer that has already constrained kidney and liver growth and caused inventory write-downs, undisclosed supplier concentration and contract terms, and outsourced cross-border logistics exposed to freight and tariffs. Support: Western-only geography (no exposure to conflict zones), structured supply agreements with co-investment, a quality system that audits suppliers, the resolved 2025 XPS hardware shortage, and a capacity project that removes the historical delivery bottleneck for heart, liver and kidney disposables ahead of their launches. The Consumer should model supply as a margin drag in Q4 2026 and H1 2027 and a modest gross-margin support from 2028.
