Company - Competitive advantage

C527 questions
C-4157C-4234C-4374C-4461C-4544C-4650C-4747

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The competitive advantage is deep and widening in one organ and shallow or unbuilt in the rest. The lung consumables franchise has the sector's strongest combination of proprietary formulation, clinical evidence, switching costs and an installed base that compounds, and it earns top-decile disposables margins; the heart system enters with the best clinical evidence in its category and a price a third of the incumbent's, which is a real relative advantage on paper and an untested one in the United States market that matters. Scale is absent, pricing power is applied only in lung and is being given back in abdominal, scale economies are shared by positioning rather than principle, and the gravitational pull that improves outputs each period runs in lung alone. The addressable market is large enough for 20 percent-plus growth for the whole horizon. For the model: hold thoracic product gross margins at 80 to 85 percent, treat abdominal gross margin recovery as an execution outcome capped in the low-to-mid 60s through 2027, assume no pricing tailwind outside the compassionate-to-commercial heart step, and let the group gross margin drift with mix rather than recover to 74 percent.

C-41

Sustainable relative advantages or moats, by type, strength and direction

C57

Question asked Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

Judgement. Xvivo holds one deep, widening moat (clinical evidence plus proprietary fluids in lung and, prospectively, heart), two real but narrowing ones (regulatory barriers; production know-how), and lacks the two that decide United States commercial outcomes (scale; a service and logistics network). The Consumer should treat the lung consumables franchise as durable at 80 to 85 percent gross margins through the horizon and treat every other segment's margin as earned by execution rather than protected by structure.

  • Customer-related (strong in lung, widening; weak in services, narrowing). Perfadex is habit for over 90 percent of lung programmes with a twenty-year track record; the XPS requires USD 500,000 of capital, USD 40,000 kits per run and specialised training; each EVLP run adds to the evidence base; FlowHawk sits in six of the ten largest United States programmes; hubs add spokes. In heart the moat-to-be is the evidence: NIHP2019's 76 percent reduction in severe primary graft dysfunction (38 percent in severe complications) and 92 versus 86 percent one-year survival, the first randomised heart trial linked to survival, published in The Lancet and the European Heart Journal; PRESERVE's 92.1 percent 30-day and 91.4 percent one-year results. In services the moat is absent: accounts churned to in-house teams.
  • Production-related (medium, narrowing). STEEN's patented extracellular composition and Perfadex Plus's pre-buffered THAM formulation (patent cover to 2035) are non-replicable; but manufacturing is outsourced, so the moat is in the recipe not the plant, and the 510(k) predicate shows hypothermic devices are replicable.
  • Scale-based (absent). 207 employees, 812 of revenue; no volume discounts; rivals are Terumo, Getinge and a USD 605 million TransMedics.
  • Regulatory or political (strong, narrowing at the device layer). PMA trials cost tens of millions and years; EU MDR shut out sub-scale players; Breakthrough designation; the moat narrows where the FDA accepts 510(k) equivalence for cold devices.
  • Culture or knowledge (strong in science, not in commercial). Two decades of perfusion chemistry and clinical data; the fluids enabled the first pig-to-human transplants; a patient-centric Swedish culture that surgeons prefer; no evidence of an execution culture that out-competes TransMedics.
C-42

Scale relative to competitors, and what it confers

❌ D34

Question asked How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

Judgement. Small, and the scale confers little beyond the installed base in lung. Revenue 812 (2025) against TransMedics USD 605 million growing 37 percent, Terumo's Organ Technologies at ¥6.2 billion a quarter, Getinge's Acute Care Therapies; 207 employees; average daily traded value about USD 2.8 million; a sales force being rebuilt region by region. What scale confers elsewhere in the sector (an owned aviation fleet, a national hub network, a digital command centre, hospital procurement relationships across a broad portfolio) Xvivo rents through partners (Perfusion Solution Inc, five aviation companies, OPOs) rather than owns, which keeps capital intensity down and limits control. The tenfold capacity project is the attempt to build production scale; management says it will not bring purchasing scale. Relative scale is high against Bridge to Life and Traferox and low against everyone else that matters. For the model: no scale-driven cost advantage in the horizon; selling and administrative expenses leverage comes from revenue growth on a fixed base, not from scale economies in purchasing.

C-43

Realistic addressable market and mid-term growth rate it allows

C74

Question asked How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

Judgement. Large relative to the company and sufficient for 20 percent-plus growth for the mid term, with the caveat that the realistic share of it is cost-capped. The addressable organ preservation and machine perfusion market is projected at USD 2.9 billion by 2033 at about 7 percent a year, with current overall penetration about 4 percent; the liver and kidney preservation market alone is USD 1.9 billion growing over 10 percent (Morgan Stanley); about 170,000 transplants a year against 1.7 to 2 million in need. Xvivo's revenue is about USD 85 million, so even a cost-capped 20 to 30 percent perfusion penetration of today's transplants is several times its current size, and heart and liver are larger organ markets than lung. Australia at 40 to 50 percent heart penetration and Italy at 20 to 25 percent abdominal penetration show what a supported market reaches. The realistic addressable market therefore allows 20 to 25 percent revenue growth a year for four years without heroic share assumptions, and the constraint is route-to-market cost and approval timing, not market size.

C-44

Surplus customer utility versus alternatives; leading or not inferior

C61

Question asked How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

Judgement. Leading in lung, demonstrably not inferior in heart on the evidence available, and not inferior but not superior in liver; the inferiority that cannot yet be disproved is in real-time organ assessment, where TransMedics' warm platform can and Xvivo's cold platform cannot.

  • Lung. The XPS and STEEN are described by experts as unrivalled; Perfadex is the standard of care; utility is high for marginal lungs (lungs rejected by dozens of clinics become transplantable) and low for standard-risk lungs where ice works.
  • Heart. The clinical data are the sector's best (76 percent PGD risk reduction; 92 versus 86 percent survival; six additional lives per 100 transplants); surgeons describe "comparable outcomes, simpler workflow and superior economics" at a third of the price; European teams report reduced stress; preservation extended beyond the three-to-four-hour window enables daytime surgery. The caveats: the earlier European cold-heart trial did not meet its endpoints, which Jefferies reports created scepticism; a surgical-research director argues the ice comparator inflated the benefit; a Mass General Brigham surgeon notes very limited long-term head-to-head data; TransMedics argues cold transport causes ischaemic damage. Inferiority is not disproved on multi-year outcomes; it is not demonstrated either.
  • Liver. Liver Assist leads Europe with over 100 publications and evidence of lower graft loss and biliary complications; in the United States, OrganOx wins normothermic cases on cost and control and Bridge to Life matches the hypothermic approach at a lower price, so utility is adequate rather than leading.

For the model: assume heart share gains in Europe on evidence and price; assume United States heart share gains are gradual and conditional on post-launch registry data; assume no utility premium in abdominal pricing.

C-45

Demonstrable, applied pricing power with minimal utility erosion

❌ D44

Question asked To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

Judgement. Pricing power exists and has been applied in one place, lung consumables, and has not been applied, or has been given back, everywhere else. Evidence of application: pricing actions countered 2023's input inflation and held disposable gross margins at 84 percent; Q1 2026 price increases offset tariffs and the weaker dollar; thoracic gross margin was 85 percent in 2025 and 89 percent in Q3 2025. Evidence against: abdominal gross margin fell from 68 percent (Q2 2025) to 54 to 57 percent in H1 2026 on mix and "temporary price pressure", recovering only to 61 percent in Q3; cold-preservation solution prices are expected flat to slightly down with three suppliers at a third each; hospital boards reject expensive capital requests and private payers deny coverage; STEEN at about USD 7,000 a litre is "the limit" of what administrators accept and large centres build homemade circuits to avoid it; the largest customer cites cost as a reason to insource. Pricing power in services is weak (bundled competitors; labour costs not passable). The one unapplied but real lever is converting compassionate-use heart sales to commercial pricing. For the model: real price minus 1 to 0 percent in abdominal, 0 to plus 1 percent in lung, a one-off step in heart.

C-46

Sharing scale economies with customers

C50

Question asked To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

Judgement. De facto yes, stated no. The hub model shares the economics of a USD 500,000 machine and a trained team across many spokes so that smaller centres access EVLP without buying an XPS; the heart and multi-organ portfolio is priced at about a third of TransMedics' per-case cost; the 2026 price increases were framed as cost offsets rather than value capture; the company's communication is patient-centric ("nobody should die waiting") rather than price-led. There is no stated principle of passing scale savings to customers, the capacity project's savings are promised to the abdominal gross margin (70 percent target) rather than to price, and the consumables are among the most expensive items in the procedure. The sharing is therefore a positioning against a higher-priced incumbent, not a culture, and it will be tested when the United States heart launch forces a choice between share and margin.

C-47

Gravitational forces improving advantage and outputs with each period

❌ D47

Question asked To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

Judgement. In lung, yes: every EVLP run adds clinical data, every hub adds spokes and consumption, every FlowHawk installation adds workflow lock-in, and the installed base compounds without heroic effort; this is why lung disposables could grow 56, 69 and 183 percent in successive quarters once the 2025 hardware and destocking disruptions passed. At group level, not yet: the financial outputs are improving by effort (a cost programme; a field-force restructuring; a manufacturing transfer) and are being held back by the segments where Xvivo is the entrant (abdominal, United States heart) and the one where it has no structural advantage (services). The mechanical operating leverage is visible in single quarters (Q4 2025 at 25 percent; Q3 2026 revenue +34 percent and EBITDA +45 percent) and is offset by discretionary launch spending through 2027. For the model: assume mechanical margin improvement from lung volume and mix conversion in heart; do not assume mechanical improvement from scale in abdominal or services.

Company - Economic strength

❌ D4611 questions
C-4954C-5045C-5125C-5234C-5349C-5428C-7560C-7674C-7750C-7857C-7947

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The economic strength is that of a high-gross-margin consumables business carrying three investments: a launch programme (heart, liver, kidney), a service build and a capacity project. The strong outputs are gross margin (70 to 74 percent group; 80 to 85 percent thoracic products), revenue growth (20 percent-plus underlying) and demonstrated operating leverage when spending pauses; the weak outputs are returns on capital near 4 percent, free cash flow near zero for three years, an EBIT margin of about 11 percent, and an earnings base that is flattered at EBITDA by capitalised development and distorted at EPS by currency, tax and a coming amortisation step. Financial gearing is absent and the balance sheet is comfortable but will be drawn down. The scope for improvement is large and concentrated in three bridges (abdominal cost, services utilisation, heart mix), each of which is dated and none of which has started to show in a full year's numbers. For the model: build EBITDA from segment gross margins and an operating-cost base that grows at two-thirds the rate of revenue, then build EBIT with an explicit amortisation schedule (SEK 39 a year from the CE mark plus the PMA and liver assets on approval), then net income with a 21 to 24 percent tax rate and an unhedged currency line, and treat consensus margin expansion as the upper half of the range rather than its centre.

C-49

EPS growth, revenue growth, durability

C54

Question asked What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

Judgement. The company is generating very high reported EPS growth off a collapsed base and about 20 percent underlying revenue growth, and the two must be modelled separately because the EPS line carries non-recurring items in both directions. The revenue growth is durable at a high-teens to low-twenties rate for the horizon; the EPS growth rate is not durable in its current form because the base is distorted.

Revenue. FY2023 +44 percent, FY2024 +38 percent, FY2025 -1 percent (+3 percent organic), nine months 2026 +25 percent reported (733), Q3 +34 percent (+32 percent organic, about 24 percent ex the SEK 19 timing). Consensus +22 / +22 / +33 / +33 percent for FY2026 to FY2029. Durability: supported by the structural drivers and the heart launches; dented in 2027 by the largest customer's step-down and harder comparatives; the +33 percent in FY2028 requires United States heart revenue, which depends on a decision in late 2027 or early 2028.

EPS. FY2024 brokers'-basis EPS 5.74, FY2025 1.03 (-82 percent, on a 34.7 percent tax rate and a 66.6 currency loss), nine months 2026 reported EPS 3.08 (1.15 + 0.52 + 1.42, with Q3 including about 0.22 of FX gains on cash). Consensus 3.38 / 6.08 / 9.47 / 14.80 (+228 / +80 / +56 / +56 percent). Because nine-month reported EPS already exceeds 3.0 and Q4 is seasonally the strongest quarter, FY2026 consensus EPS is likely to rise mechanically after Q3; the FY2027 growth rate from that higher base then falls, and the FY2027 figure itself carries the new SEK 39 annual CE-mark amortisation (about 1.0 of EPS after tax) that the EBITDA-to-EBIT gap in consensus (110 in FY2027 against 86 in FY2026) may or may not fully include.

Durability, stated plainly. Revenue growth of 20 percent a year for three to four years is the durable core. EPS growth is a function of (a) that growth, (b) an EBITDA margin that must rise from 20 to 27 percent for consensus FY2027, (c) amortisation and currency lines that have each moved EPS by more than the operating lines in one of the last three years. The framework's own over-extrapolation warning applies: any EPS compound rate above 40 percent a year from a depressed base reverts when a launch slips.

C-50

Scope to deploy capital at high returns for three to five years; implication for fair multiple

❌ D45

Question asked How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

Judgement. The scope to deploy is large and already being used; the return on it is unproven and the history says low, so the fair multiple cannot yet rest on it. Deployment: consensus capital expenditure (fixed and intangible) of 210 / 226 / 240 / 245 for FY2026 to FY2029, 21 falling to 15 percent of revenue, against peers at 0 to 8 percent; nine-month 2026 investment of 171; 800.7 of capitalised development on the balance sheet at end-2025 plus 131.7 in nine months 2026; a tenfold capacity project; a United States commercial build. Return: returns on capital have fluctuated near 4 percent against typical double digits for established medtech, depressed by capitalised development and acquisition goodwill; the return on the heart programme will be known only after FDA approval and United States adoption; the return on the services build has been negative so far. The mid- and long-term potential is real (a large under-penetrated market where the company has the evidence lead) and the fair multiple should price it as an option with a dated test (FDA decision late 2027 to early 2028; first full European heart year 2027) rather than as a demonstrated reinvestment engine. For the model: capital intensity stays above 15 percent of revenue through FY2028; free cash flow is near zero until then.

C-51

Returns on capital: post-tax on new invested capital, on capital employed, on tangible capital

❌ D25

Question asked How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

Judgement. Low on every measure that can be computed, and not disclosed on the one that would flatter it. Return on capital employed has run near 4 percent historically; FY2025 adjusted EBIT of 93 on shareholders' equity of roughly 2,100 (consensus book value per share 70.67 for FY2026 times 31.5 million shares implies about 2,200) gives about 4 percent pre-tax, about 3 percent post-tax; FY2024's 116 of adjusted EBIT gave about 5 percent. Return on new invested capital is not demonstrable: the capital invested since 2020 (Organ Assist, STAR, Avionord, FlowHawk, 800 of development) has produced an abdominal segment at 61 percent gross margin, a loss-making services unit, and a heart product that has not yet earned commercial revenue in its main market. Return on tangible capital is not given; the intangible base (capitalised development plus goodwill) is most of the capital, so the tangible return is far higher (the lung consumables business on its own would be a high-return business) but is not in evidence. Consensus implies returns rising to about 13 percent on equity by FY2028 (net income 271 on equity 2,691) and about 14 percent by FY2029, which is the first point at which the group would clear a double-digit return. For the model: returns on capital are an output of the margin leg, not a constraint on it.

C-52

Conversion of net income into free cash flow; working capital

❌ D34

Question asked How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

Judgement. Conversion is poor and will stay poor until the capitalised regulatory spending ends; working capital, by contrast, has been fixed and is now a modest source of cash. Free cash flow on FactSet's definition (operating cash flow less fixed-asset capital expenditure, before intangibles) was 7.3 in FY2025 (29 percent of reported net income); on the fuller definition including intangibles the company burned cash in 2025 and was close to breakeven in 2026 (Q1 +10.7, Q2 -4, Q3 about +4 on RedEye's measure; total cash flow about +1 in Q3 after 49 of investing). Operating cash flow has inflected: 101 in FY2025, 182 in nine months 2026 (65.3, 63.5, 53.0). Consensus free cash flow is -99 in FY2026, 0 in FY2027, 103 in FY2028 and 216 in FY2029, against net income of 103 / 175 / 271 / 465, so conversion runs negative, zero, about 40 percent and about 45 percent. Working capital: net working capital fell from 242 (29.8 percent of trailing revenue) at Q4 2025 to 53.7 (5.6 percent) at Q3 2026 as the 2025 inventory build for the heart launch unwound; receivables are stable at about 13 percent of sales (about 47 days); the risk is a renewed inventory build for the United States heart launch in 2027 and channel inventory at customers (the Q3 build by the largest customer). For the model: use operating cash flow as the earnings-quality test; assume free cash flow turns positive only when capitalised development falls below about 100 a year, which is a 2028 event on the current programme.

C-53

Gross and operating margins and trend

❌ D49

Question asked How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?

Judgement. Gross margin is high but drifting down on mix; the EBITDA margin is flat at about 20 percent with single quarters above it; the EBIT margin is low and about to carry new amortisation. Gross margin (company basis): 75 percent FY2024, 74 percent FY2025, 71 percent H1 2026, 70 percent Q3 2026 (thoracic 80 percent from 89 percent on machine mix; abdominal 61 percent; services -19 percent). FactSet's standardised gross margin is about 8 points lower on every period (65.7 percent FY2025; 61.7 percent Q3 2026) and should not be mixed with the company's. EBITDA margin (company basis): 13 percent FY2023, 21 percent FY2024, 20 percent FY2025, 21 / 19 / 21 percent in the three quarters of 2026 (nine months 20 percent), with Q4 2025's 25 percent as the peak and management's own warning that it was not a baseline. Adjusted EBIT margin: 14.1 percent FY2024, 11.5 percent FY2025, 13 / 9 / 11 percent in 2026. The trend in gross margin is down and the trend in EBITDA margin is flat; the sell-side's path (consensus EBITDA margin 20.7 / 26.8 / 28.9 / 34.1 percent; EBIT 12.1 / 17.7 / 20.7 / 26.6 percent) requires both to reverse. The two margin bridges to monitor are the abdominal gross margin (61 percent toward 70 percent) and the services gross margin (-19 percent toward breakeven); together they are worth about four to five points of group EBITDA margin at FY2027 revenue.

C-54

Cleanliness of the earnings base

❌ D28

Question asked How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

Judgement. Not clean, and the uncleanliness is of the kind that flatters EBITDA and distorts EPS in both directions, so the Consumer must model below the EBITDA line explicitly rather than apply a drop-through ratio.

  • Capitalised development. 152.8 in FY2025 (19 percent of sales; 159.2 on the annual report's broader measure), 131.7 in nine months 2026, against amortisation of 20.1 in 2025; 800.7 of deferred development at end-2025. Expensed R&D is 16 percent of sales on top. If the capitalised amount were expensed, FY2025 EBITDA of about 161 would be near zero; the company's 20 percent EBITDA margin is therefore an accounting choice sanctioned by IFRS and the Industry Digest names it as the sector's characteristic false friend. The offsetting fact is that the spending is finite and tied to trials that end with approvals.
  • Amortisation to come. The company expects SEK 39 a year of CE-mark amortisation over ten years from August 2026; the heart PMA and liver assets will add to it on approval. Consensus EBITDA-to-EBIT gaps (86 / 110 / 132 / 162) rise but are not reconciled to this by any broker on file; the Consumer should build D&A bottom-up.
  • Adjustments. Small: -2.6 of FlowHawk integration costs in 2025; the FY2025 brokers'-basis EPS (1.03) exceeds reported (0.80) mainly through the currency item.
  • Currency in financial items. -66.6 in 2025, +32.7 in 2024, about +7.1 in Q3 2026 alone; unhedged.
  • Tax. 2.9 percent (2023), 13.9 percent (2024), 34.7 percent (2025); the underlying Swedish rate is 20.6 percent and consensus implies about 19 to 24 percent; United States profits would push it up.
  • Revenue recognition. 8.5 of initial finance-lease recognition on XPS placements in Q3; machine timing distorts quarterly growth.
  • One-offs historically. The 17 PrimECC write-down (Q4 2023), 22 of STAR integration (2023), inventory write-downs (Q4 2025, Q3 2026).

Net: reported EBITDA overstates cash earnings by roughly the capitalised amount less maintenance-level development; reported EPS is volatile on currency and tax; adjusted EPS on the brokers' basis is the least bad metric and still needs an explicit amortisation step.

C-75

Fixed-cost operating leverage: sensitivity and range of outcomes

C60

Question asked How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

Judgement. The operating leverage is material and demonstrated, and it cuts both ways: with a gross margin of 70 to 74 percent and an operating-cost base (selling about 37 percent of sales, administration about 10 percent, expensed R&D about 16 percent in 2025) that grows slower than revenue when launch spending pauses, each point of revenue growth above the cost base's growth adds roughly 0.7 points of EBITDA, and a shortfall subtracts the same. Evidence: trailing-twelve-month EBITDA margin rose from 5 percent (Q2 2022) to 18 percent (Q2 2024) on revenue doubling; Q4 2025 reached 25 percent when spending paused; Q3 2026 revenue +34 percent produced EBITDA +45 percent (35.9 to 52.0) despite a five-point gross-margin fall; conversely Q2 2025's 15 percent revenue decline took the EBITDA margin to 13 percent and Q1 2026's 7 of launch costs took three points off. The sensitivity DNB Carnegie publishes is about 3 percent of EPS per point of revenue growth and 2 percent per 50 basis points of EBIT margin. The range of outcomes this creates for FY2027: on consensus revenue of 1,210, an EBITDA margin anywhere from 22 percent (launch costs persist, services still negative, abdominal at 61 percent) to 28 percent (management's bridge lands) is defensible, which is EBITDA of 265 to 340 and EPS of roughly 4.5 to 6.5 on the same share count and tax, before the amortisation and currency lines widen it further. The leverage is also partly discretionary: management confirmed at Q3 that investment continues, so the operating leverage shows up only when management chooses to let it.

C-76

Financial leverage or gearing: amplification and comfort

C74

Question asked How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

Judgement. There is no financial gearing to speak of and the balance sheet is comfortable; the amplifier of EPS and risk at Xvivo is currency, not debt. Net cash of about 193 on FactSet's definition (313 cash; 121 of debt, of which about 85 is financial debt on the FactSet capital-structure snapshot and the rest leases); net debt to EBITDA -1.0x; equity ratio 87 percent; net interest expense 4.6 in FY2025; a EUR 20 million revolver drawn EUR 8 million, maturing January 2028; no covenants of note; the board can issue up to 10 percent without a vote. The comfort has limits: consensus free cash flow is -99 in FY2026 and zero in FY2027, so cash drifts down toward 200 to 250 before it rebuilds, the revolver matures inside the horizon, and a prolonged United States launch delay with continued services losses would bring an equity raise into view by 2028, which the risks report flags. For the model: interest at or near zero; financial items dominated by unhedged currency revaluation of about 205 of monetary assets; no deleveraging contribution to EPS.

C-77

How much of each major cost is cuttable

C50

Question asked How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

Judgement. A meaningful share of the cost base is discretionary in principle and strategic in practice, so the cuttable amount is larger than the amount management will cut. By line (FY2025 shares of sales, company basis): cost of goods 26 percent, of which the services node is labour and surgical capacity that could be cut back to volume (the H2 2025 programme and the six-month halt on new service contracts show management will do this) but is now framed as launch infrastructure; selling 37 percent, of which the United States field build (10 to 13 heart-launch hires; hub teams) is discretionary and would be cut only if the launch failed; administration 10 percent, already down from 12 percent and with modest further scope; expensed R&D 16 percent plus capitalised development 19 percent, of which the trial-related portion falls away mechanically with approvals rather than by decision. The 2025 programme produced a 25 percent EBITDA quarter, which is the evidence that about four to five points of margin can be released within two quarters by pausing spending. Peer precedent: Ambu's turnaround came one-third from gross margin and two-thirds from operating-expense leverage. For the model: a downside case in which launches slip should include a cost response worth three to four points of EBITDA margin within two quarters; the base case should assume management does not use it.

C-78

Scope to improve outputs beyond cost-cutting; precedents; few or many; strategy fit

C57

Question asked Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

Judgement. The scope is large, concentrated in three big areas, supported by peer precedent in two of them, and fully congruent with the stated strategy; the risk is timing rather than existence. (1) Abdominal gross margin from 61 percent toward 70 percent through the Swedish consolidation and a process learning curve (one to 1.5 years; worth about two points of group gross margin at FY2027 mix); precedent: the company's own abdominal disposables reached 69 to 70 percent in 2023 before the kidney and emerging-market mix shifted. (2) Services from -19 percent gross margin to the industry's 18 to 33 percent range as recovery volumes and NRP contracts fill the surgical capacity (worth two to three points of group gross margin); precedent: TransMedics' service revenue grew 29 percent at a 27 percent product-versus-service margin split, which shows volume fills the node but it stays thin. (3) Heart from compassionate to commercial pricing and from 22 a quarter to consensus 181 in FY2027 at thoracic-grade margins, which is the single largest mix improvement available. Many small items also exist (sourcing redesigns, back-office scaling, field-force reallocation, FlowHawk integration). Peer precedents for the group-level outcome: Ambu lifted its EBIT margin from 2.7 to 13 percent in three years on a razor-blade model once growth was believed, and Vitrolife's 29 percent EBITDA margin shows where a mature consumables franchise settles; TransMedics' 11 percent EBIT margin shows what a service-heavy model earns. The sell-side's 34 percent EBITDA margin for FY2029 is above every precedent on file for a company with a services node.

C-79

Business perimeter: breadth, change, predictability

❌ D47

Question asked How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

Judgement. The perimeter is narrow by count and widening by activity, and the widening lowers predictability. Three segments, four organs, operations in four countries (Sweden, the Netherlands, Italy, the United States) with direct sales in North America, Europe, Australia and China and distributors elsewhere; revenue 53 percent United States, 38 percent EMEA, 9 percent other; thoracic 70 percent of Q3 sales and concentrated in the Americas (69 percent), abdominal concentrated in EMEA (80 percent). The change is that the activities inside the perimeter have multiplied: product sales, organ-recovery services, NRP services, software, hub operations, and two regulatory regimes running three approval programmes at once. Predictability: the segment disclosure is good (sales, organic growth and gross margin by segment; EVLP disposable growth; heart sales; hub count), but the drivers are quarterly or event-driven, there is no numerical guidance, and a one-customer concentration in the largest segment means the group can swing 15 points of growth on one account. For the model: a narrow perimeter to forecast at the revenue line, a wide one at the margin line.

Company - Management

❌ D4911 questions
C-5556C-5657C-5750C-57b45C-5854C-5940C-6055C-6147C-6244C-6356C-6438

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Management is a careful, honest, scientifically excellent steward with a demonstrated weakness in United States commercial execution, which is the dimension the next three years of the investment case depend on. The chief executive and team have delivered scale, clinical proof, regulatory milestones, cost discipline and clean governance; they have not delivered a working United States services unit, a stable people organisation during scaling, or a single regulatory date on time before the two that landed this year, and the finance director's redeployment to fix the services problem has left the CFO seat shared for eight months. Communication is clear on operations and silent on the economics of the new models. Capital allocation is reinvestment without demonstrated returns. For the model: give full weight to management's operational milestones and clinical claims, apply a lag of two to four quarters to its commercial dates, assume operating expenses rise with the launch programme regardless of near-term margins, and do not model a cost response unless launches slip.

C-55

Size of ambition or vision, relative to peers

C56

Question asked How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

Judgement. The stated ambition is large (a market opportunity "100 times larger" by converting static storage; "standard of care for all donor hearts", including DBD, DCD, extended-criteria and paediatric; "preferred partner throughout transplantation"; "2027 as a springboard to 2028"; volume growth at twice the market's rate written into incentives) and the behaviour behind it is measured rather than relentless: former colleagues describe the chief executive as thoughtful, deliberate and cautious, United States clinicians call the approach to their market too slow, and the leadership report's "force" marker is only partial. Relative to peers the ambition is below TransMedics' (a founder who built an owned airline to win) and comparable to Ambu's or Bonesupport's (a leadership goal with a dated financial target), and above Vitrolife's. The gap between ambition in words and in pace is the thing the Consumer should carry: the company sets the targets of an aggressive grower and executes at the pace of a careful one, which argues for modelling the company's own dates with a lag.

C-56

Quality of strategy and strategic priorities

C57

Question asked How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

Judgement. The strategy is smart in shape, bold in its central bet and dynamic in its responses, and it has one priority whose economics are unproven. Smart: an all-organ portfolio that no competitor matches; asset-light partnerships (perfusionists, aviation, OPOs) instead of an owned fleet; the pivot from a 215-patient PMA trial to a 510(k) when a predicate appeared; exporting the Italian service-bundle model; a staged European heart rollout prioritising funded centres; supply-chain scale as a named capability. Bold: the heart system is the largest entry in the company's history against an entrenched incumbent, and the clinical programme was run to completion five months early. Dynamic: the H2 2025 cost programme, the summer 2026 field-force restructuring, the six-month halt on new service contracts to fix logistics. The unproven priority is the United States services unit as launch infrastructure: it widens the moat in theory (control of the recovery workflow) and has lost money and accounts in practice. Mid-term moat widening is early (FlowHawk integration; hubs) and the strategy can capitalise on a range of environments because transplant demand is non-cyclical and the balance sheet is unlevered. For the model: the strategy supports the revenue leg fully and the margin leg conditionally.

C-57

Quality of the chief executive, by actions

C50

Question asked How great is the chief executive, judged by actions rather than conjecture (an enabler, not an ossifier)?

Judgement. A disciplined, competent internal operator rather than a great one, judged by what he has done. For: ran the company through the founder-era chief executive's sudden death in 2022 without losing the strategy; doubled revenue from 415 (2022) to 822 (2024); completed the United States heart trial five months early and met every endpoint; delivered the CE mark and the PMA filing; restored two cash-positive quarters and a record margin with the 2025 cost programme; communicates honestly (announced the delay proactively; warned against extrapolating the 25 percent quarter; admits limited visibility); restored sell-side confidence. Against: no prior executive record before Xvivo; former directors say he lacks the founder's visionary pull and was late to scale United States services; United States clinicians call the approach too slow; the 2025 destocking was "significantly misjudged" and produced a 30 percent one-day fall; every regulatory date has slipped; the employee net promoter score halved on his watch; the leadership report's agency marker is weak and its capital-allocation marker weak. He is an enabler of the clinical and financial programme and has not yet shown he can out-execute a United States competitor commercially.

C-57b

Priority on hiring; quality of the leadership team

❌ D45

Question asked How high a priority is hiring well for the chief executive, and how great is the leadership team the chief executive has built (an enabler)?

Judgement. Hiring is treated as a priority in words and the team is long-tenured and stable, but the two most important hires of the last two years are an unfilled seat and a replaced head. The team: Deputy CEO and COO Lena Hagman (ex-Getinge, a credible successor), CMO Andreas Wallinder (PhD, surgical credibility), R&D head Charlotte Walldal (ex-Essity), Johan Holmström (EMEA and rest of world), Jaya Tiwari (North American clinical and regulatory), Mark Reade (North America, two decades in devices, since July 2024), Kristoffer Nordström (CFO and now strategy and services). Minimal disruption in 18 months, long average tenure. Against: the North American commercial leadership "underperformed and was let go"; the CFO seat has no named successor after eight months of search; headcount grew to 207 with no functional breakdown and no turnover disclosure; the company admits dependence on key personnel; surgeons and perfusionists are being recruited in a market where TransMedics pays more and poaches. The chief executive's stated hiring philosophy (seasoned United States medtech commercial leaders; partner rather than hire for surgical capacity) is sensible; its proof is the next two years of United States launch execution.

C-58

How well resourced and invested the business is

C54

Question asked How well resourced and invested is the whole business (a buffer; an ant colony: built yesterday for resilience)?

Judgement. Well invested in the things that take years (clinical evidence, product, regulatory assets, manufacturing capacity) and under-invested until recently in the thing the thesis now needs (United States commercial and service capacity), which is now being built ahead of demand at a margin cost. Evidence of resilience built yesterday: 800.7 of capitalised development; the Lancet and European Heart Journal trials; a tenfold capacity project; the all-organ portfolio; a partner network of 175 perfusionists and five aviation companies; net cash. Evidence of the gap: services lost accounts for lack of regional logistics; the field force was restructured in 2026; 10 to 13 United States commercial hires are only now being made for a launch targeted a year away. The current build (207 employees, up 7 percent in a year; surgical roster doubled; three hubs) is an ant-colony investment in the right places and its productivity is not yet evidenced. For the model: operating expenses grow at least in line with revenue through 2027.

C-59

Holism and congruence of culture

❌ D40

Question asked How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

Judgement. The culture is coherent in values and incongruent with the market it now has to win. Coherent: Swedish, trust-based, patient-centric, collaborative, scientifically rigorous; surgeons prefer it to TransMedics' aggression; the code of conduct, whistleblower record and governance scaffolding are clean; incentives were extended to perfusion volume. Incongruent: the employee net promoter score fell from 51 to 27 and commitment from 4.16 to 4.04 in 2025 (still inside the Swedish benchmark zone of 10 to 30, but a 47 percent fall during scaling); surgeons were lost to rivals; a former director calls United States services execution "terrible because of poor commercial focus"; the counter-view on file is that a consensus-driven model prevents rapid course-correction against a hyper-aggressive United States competitor. Goals (standard of care in all organs), strategy (service-led adoption) and execution (a service unit that has shrunk for six quarters) are not yet symbiotic in the Lifco or Ryanair sense. For the model: no cultural flywheel to assume; the execution risk is organisational, not technological.

C-60

Clarity and helpfulness of communication

C55

Question asked How clear, straightforward and helpful is the company's communication, including on the strategy and the driver transmission mechanism?

Judgement. Clear and honest on what is reported, and silent on the numbers a modeller most needs. Helpful: quarterly disclosure of segment sales, organic growth and gross margin, EVLP disposable growth, heart sales, hub count, FlowHawk growth, capitalised development and cash flow; proactive disclosure of the CE delay, the Q3 timing items (the 19 of lease and inventory effects) and the largest customer's transition; "exceptionally clear" financial presentations; the company publishes consensus before results. Unhelpful: no numerical guidance in a launch year; 2027 targets stated without a bridge; hub economics withheld until six months of data exist; the size and timing of the largest customer's decline not quantified; FlowHawk's fall from +48 percent to 0 percent not reconciled; partnership terms undisclosed; D&A guidance limited to the CE-mark amortisation. The driver transmission from plan to operations to revenue and gross margin is clear; from there to EPS it is opaque. The sell-side therefore models the heart line unaided, which is why consensus is wide (FY2028 revenue 1,501 to 1,726 across four brokers).

C-61

Record of acting, not talking; delivery whatever the environment

❌ D47

Question asked How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?

Judgement. The record is one of action in science, cost and regulation and of persistent shortfall in United States commercial delivery, with delivery against the sell-side that is good at the top line and poor at timing. Acts: the cost programme, the perfusionist partnership, three hubs, the field-force restructuring, the finance director's redeployment, the liver pivot, the trial completion, the CE mark, the PMA filing, the AGM board refresh. Delivery: revenue +98 percent 2022 to 2024; sales beats in four of the last five quarters; EBITDA beat in Q3 2026 (+14 percent against consensus) and Q4 2025 (+31 percent), misses in Q1 and Q2 2026 (-6 percent each); FY2025 in line on EBITDA and 1 percent light on sales. Shortfalls: services down for six quarters with contracts "taking longer than originally anticipated"; every regulatory date slipped before the two that landed; the Q2 2025 destocking misjudged. In the tough 2025 environment (one customer, currency, a delay) the company reported -1 percent revenue and an 82 percent EPS fall, which is not outperformance in a tougher environment; it was an honest tracker of its own setbacks. Mid-term, the record justifies trusting the clinical and regulatory programme and discounting the commercial dates.

C-62

Outlier standards; out-executing competitors

❌ D44

Question asked To what extent does the company hold outlier standards, driving higher, faster and better, with a record of out-executing competitors?

Judgement. Outlier in science and in product gross margin; not outlier in commercial execution. The clinical evidence (a 76 percent reduction in severe primary graft dysfunction; the first randomised heart trial linked to survival; 92 versus 86 percent one-year survival; PRESERVE enrolled five months early with unprecedented centre interest) is the best in its category and exceeds the incumbent's; thoracic disposable gross margins of 84 to 89 percent are top decile among specialised disposables makers. Commercially, TransMedics out-executed Xvivo in the United States for a decade (over 1,000 heart cases before Xvivo's filing; 21 percent of United States transplants), OrganOx is winning liver on service and cost, and Xvivo's services unit lost accounts to physician-led programmes. The standard the company drives is "higher and better" in the laboratory and "later and slower" in the field.

C-63

Shareholders first; prudence of governance; evidence

C56

Question asked Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

Judgement. The evidence shows a company that is honest with shareholders and prudent in its scaffolding, with an alignment gap rather than an alignment failure. Shareholders first: chief executive pay fell to 6.01 million in 2025 from 7.38 million on missed targets (variable pay -47 percent); the 2022/2025 warrant programme expired unexercised when targets were missed; long-term plans need 8 to 12 percent annual TSR and 12 percent perfusion-volume growth to vest; dilution from all plans is at most 1.35 percent; no dividend because reinvestment at this stage is the right call; the delay was announced proactively; the Q3 timing items were disclosed unprompted. Prudent governance not strictly required: a single share class; a seven-member board, six independent; two new independent directors with United States commercial experience; an unqualified audit with goodwill as a key audit matter; zero whistleblower cases 2022 to 2025; a rolling-forecast system; a business-continuity review; a tendered audit in 2025. The gaps: the chairman is dependent on the 14.27 percent holder Bure and chairs the remuneration committee; KPMG has audited since 2013; there is no internal audit function; director and executive equity is thin (chairman 8,000 shares; CFO 3,700; chief executive 53,523 plus 39,000 rights, 0.19 percent); the variable cash cap was raised to 60 percent of base for the chief executive and 45 percent for others; there is no clawback disclosure of the United States kind; no open-market insider buying. Why it reads as prudent on balance: nothing in the record is self-serving, the pay system punished the misses, and the governance red flags are structural (block-holder, tenure) rather than behavioural.

C-64

Enlightened capital allocation; dynamic repurchaser

❌ D38

Question asked To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

Judgement. An operator-generator that reinvests everything, not an allocator, and the record of returns on what it has reinvested is weak. Reinvestment: 32 to 35 percent of revenue into R&D in 2024 to 2025, capitalised development of 152.8 (2025) and 131.7 (nine months 2026), a 50 capacity project, consensus capital expenditure of 210 to 245 a year. Acquisitions: Organ Assist (2020) created the abdominal segment and worked; STAR Teams (2021, up to USD 26.36 million) took until July 2023 to integrate at 22 of one-off cost and created the group's loss-making unit; Avionord (2022, up to EUR 12 million) built a profitable Italian service model; FlowHawk (2024, 57) is growing 25 percent year to date and 0 percent in Q3. Returns: about 4 percent on capital; the heart programme's return is unknown until approval. Repurchases: none, ever; the 2026 authorisation is housekeeping. The leadership report's capital-allocation discipline marker is "weak". The right behaviour for a launch-stage business is to reinvest, and the company has not wasted capital on a Vitrolife scale; but there is no evidence of allocation at a high return on new invested capital and no sign of dynamic repurchasing.

Risks

❌ D374 questions
C-8063C-8134C-8230C-8335

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The risk profile is that of a strong engine with an unconstrained downside: the core growth engine is not decelerating and the balance sheet is net cash, so survival is not at issue, but the case carries three fundamental risks of the sudden, large shape (an FDA setback on heart; the largest customer's step-down; a liver pathway refusal), a currency exposure that is an outlier even among unhedged Nordic exporters, and a perception profile (small, illiquid, shorted, unanimously rated, read across from TransMedics, listed in a tightening Sweden) that amplifies any fundamental disappointment into a 25 to 45 percent drawdown to a fair trough multiple. The risks report's winning hypothesis, the false friend in which a revenue beat coincides with earnings-quality erosion, was partly borne out by Q3 (timing-assisted sales, inventory write-downs, negative services margin, FX-assisted EPS) and partly refuted (operating cash flow 53; EBITDA +45 percent). For the model: run the base case with the United States heart launch a year later than the company's target and the lung customer stepping down through 2027, run a downside with an FDA data request, and size the position for a drawdown of a third that the record shows is ordinary for this stock.

C-80

Deceleration of the core growth engine over the last twelve months

C63

Question asked To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

Judgement. The core growth engine has not decelerated; it has accelerated from a trough, and the input force behind it (DCD mix, OPO pressure, hub adoption) is not slowing. The question for the model is what the engine's normal rate is once the trough rebound is netted. Revenue growth over the last twelve months went from -0.6 percent (Q4 2025) to +10.2, +34.0, +33.5 percent; organic 23, 36, 32 percent; United States EVLP disposables +56, +69, +183 percent; the company's 2022 to 2025 normal was about 25 percent a year. Underlying Q3 growth after removing the 19 of timing was about 24 percent, and the two-year stacked rate is about 13 percent a year, so the engine is running at or slightly above its normal rate on an underlying basis, not far above it. The one deceleration inside the engine is at the component level: kidney slowed to +19 percent from +72 percent on a component replacement until early 2027, European liver is maturing, services fell 28 percent and FlowHawk went to zero. The input force is strengthening (court-upheld OPO rule; competitor growth 37 to 65 percent). The known future deceleration, the largest customer's step-down, is a 2027 event and should be modelled as such rather than read into the last twelve months.

C-81

Fundamental risks: sudden, high-magnitude, low-probability; plausibility and size

❌ D34

Question asked What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

Judgement. Five risks of this shape exist; none is probable; two are large enough to break the case.

  1. An FDA request for further heart data or a new trial, or a rejection. Plausibility medium (the TransMedics OCS Heart precedent took years; no panel is scheduled; the earlier European cold-heart trial failed its endpoints; PRESERVE is single-arm). Size: a year or more of United States heart revenue (consensus heart 336 in FY2028 of which the United States part is most of the growth) and continued capitalisation and opex; the risks report's own trigger is any hold or delay over six months. Case-breaking for the 2028 numbers, not for the company.
  2. Full loss of the largest lung customer. Plausibility medium-to-high for a partial step-down (confirmed), low for a complete exit within the horizon (STEEN stays in the platform). Size: up to 132 of 2025 revenue, 16 percent of the group, at thoracic margins; even the 5 to 6 percent of 2027 revenue DNB Carnegie sizes is a quarter of a year's growth.
  3. FDA refusal of 510(k) comparability for Liver Assist. Plausibility medium (the Q-sub outcome is not yet disclosed). Size: a 2029 United States liver launch instead of early 2028, plus the cost of a 215-patient trial.
  4. A dual currency shock. A simultaneous 30 percent fall in USD and EUR would remove about 220 of revenue and 79 of EBITDA, turning operating cash flow negative; plausibility low at that size, but a 10 percent move (-45 of sales, -2 points of margin) is ordinary.
  5. A supply or quality event: a clean-room validation failure in the Q4 2026 transfer, a sterile-disposable recall (CooperSurgical's media recall cost USD 271.6 million), a single-source kit supplier outage. Plausibility low; size a quarter or two of a segment's sales. Also: a multi-year outcome study showing cold heart perfusion inferior to warm (the earnings review's own "what would change my mind"), and OPO decertification chaos freezing United States contracting for two quarters (plausibility medium, size modest).
C-82

Perception- or sentiment-driven sudden risks, including sector events

❌ D30

Question asked What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

Judgement. The perception risks are unusually large for the company's quality because the stock is small, thinly traded, heavily shorted, unanimously rated Buy and read across from a volatile United States bellwether. Channels: (1) TransMedics' results days and any further short-seller report of the Scorpion type (January 2025 took TransMedics down 49 percent in three months and Xvivo's multiple contracted in sympathy; TransMedics fell over 25 percent on its May 2026 miss); (2) a European or United States medtech de-rating leg (United States medtech was down 25 percent year to date in June 2026; fourteen of seventeen dashboard peers sit in the bottom decile of their own valuation history); (3) crowding, with short interest at 8 to 9.5 percent against under 3 percent for Swedish medtech peers and a 100 percent Buy consensus that has "nowhere to go but down" on a slip; (4) liquidity, with about USD 2.8 million traded a day on a 7.1 billion SEK capitalisation and a 14.27 percent holder whose chairman representative makes any placing a signal; (5) the Swedish rates regime, where a Riksbank hike in November 2026 into one of the most rate-sensitive developed economies lowers Swedish small-cap appetite and strengthens the krona; (6) the company's own binary dates, where the record shows -30 percent on a results day (Q2 2025), -25 percent on the CE delay, -10 percent on 14 July 2026, and a 64 percent fall across 2025. The symmetric channel is a squeeze on good regulatory news. The weekly environment note's Fragility tilt (Double Underweight) names exactly this profile.

C-83

How far the shares could fall to a fair trough multiple

❌ D35

Question asked How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

Judgement. From 223.4, a fair trough is 25 to 45 percent below, depending on whose earnings the trough multiple is applied to, and the stock has visited that zone within the last twelve months (52-week low 160.1; October 2025 low about 163).

The arithmetic. The shares trade at 27.06x consensus 24-month-forward EPS (the 6th percentile of the five-year range of 24.75x to 243.05x; the two-year median is 32.73x) and at 25.5x on the dashboard's P/E (1st percentile of ten years). The implied 24-month-forward consensus EPS is about 8.3. A fair trough for a 20 percent grower whose growth is doubted is the growth end of the mature medtech consumables set, 20 to 23x (Straumann 21.5x; the set median far lower), and the stock's own five-year low is about 25x.

  • On consensus 24-month-forward EPS of about 8.3: 25x gives about 207 (-7 percent); 20x gives about 166 (-26 percent).
  • On the memo's own path (24-month-forward EPS about 6.4 at September 2026, which Q3 does not change materially because the beat was timing-assisted): 25x gives about 160 (-28 percent); 20x gives about 128 (-43 percent).
  • Published anchors: RedEye's bear case 145 (-35 percent), its base 385 and bull 785; the risks report's severe scenario of 35 to 50 percent value destruction; DNB Carnegie's 25 percent discount to TransMedics in its bear scenario.

What would take it there. A multi-quarter FDA delay, a sharper-than-expected lung customer step-down coinciding with services losses, or a sector de-rating leg; the multiple is company-driven (the record attributes about 15 percent of volatility to sector compression), so the trigger would mostly be the company's own news. The strategic-buyer floor (11 to 20x sales paid for peers; Xvivo at about 6.9x FY2026 sales) argues the 128 case is unlikely to persist, not that it cannot print.

Back to contents

Guidance

❌ D426 questions
C-6847C-7044C-7134C-7240C-8544C-7344

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The company guides by operational milestone and multi-year ambition rather than by number, and that combination is the least helpful for a modeller: the operational blocks are specific and mostly dated, the financial targets are a stretch that every broker already discounts, and the qualitative commentary, which has been the accurate part of management's communication, is more cautious than consensus for the next twelve months on services, kidney, European liver, the United States launch date and the lung customer. There is no guidance to be conservative, to be raised or to be rebased, so the sell-side cycle will be driven by reported quarters and regulatory events rather than by company steers. For the model: take the company's milestones with a two-to-four-quarter lag on commercial dates, take its qualitative caution at face value, treat the 2027 margin targets as a ceiling, and build the EBITDA-to-EPS bridge from first principles because the company will not provide it.

C-68

Company guidance versus consensus for this year and next, and why

❌ D47

Question asked To what extent is company guidance above or below sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

Judgement. There is no numerical guidance to compare, so the question resolves into the relationship between the company's targets and steers and the consensus: for FY2026 the company's only steer (July 2026: EBITDA in line with consensus) is now below what Q3 implies, and for FY2027 the company's targets (EBITDA margin above 30 percent; EBIT above 20 percent) sit above consensus (26.8 percent EBITDA; 17.7 percent EBIT; DNB Carnegie 26.6 and 17.2 percent). The reasons: the company sets targets as ambition and the sell-side discounts them for the services losses, the abdominal mix and launch costs; the company has never given numerical annual guidance and declines to quantify hub economics or the heart ramp. On revenue, management's qualitative framing (a controlled European heart rollout; United States launch "targeted" for end-2027 subject to approval; kidney growth resuming early 2027; services contracts slow) is more cautious than consensus on timing and consistent with it on direction. For the model: treat the 2027 margin targets as the top of the range and the FY2026 "in line with consensus" steer as superseded by a Q3 that beat EBITDA consensus by 14 percent.

C-70

Record, nature and behaviour in upgrading guidance

❌ D44

Question asked Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

Judgement. The company has no record of upgrading guidance because it gives none; its behaviour with targets is to set them at a capital-markets-style horizon (2027), reaffirm them through setbacks and warn against extrapolating good quarters. Instances: the 2023 to 2027 strategy was kept intact through the 2022 succession; the 2027 margin targets were reaffirmed on 14 July 2026 after a 6 percent EBITDA miss and were not raised after the Q3 beat; the chief executive cautioned that the 25 percent Q4 2025 margin was not a baseline and guided to 20 percent; management attributed part of the Q3 2026 margin to seasonality. The archetype is an honest tracker that lowers expectations qualitatively when it must (the 2025 delay; "2025 was not pleasing") and never raises them numerically. For the Consumer this means the sell-side, not the company, will drive any upgrade cycle, and the first beat-and-raise the sector rewards cannot come from a company that does not guide.

C-71

Conservatism of guidance for the next one to three years against the past, adjusted for change forces

❌ D34

Question asked How conservative is current guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five years, adjusted for the difference in input change forces versus the past?

Judgement. Not conservative. The 2027 targets require an EBITDA margin above 30 percent and an EBIT margin above 20 percent against a history of 13, 21 and 20 percent EBITDA and 6, 14 and 11.5 percent adjusted EBIT (FY2023 to FY2025), a nine-month 2026 EBITDA margin of 20 percent, and a 70 percent abdominal gross margin against 61 percent today; they were set before the services unit turned negative, before the largest customer's platform approval and before the CE-mark amortisation was known. Adjusted for the change forces, the targets become harder, not easier: the external demand force is stronger than when they were set (which helps revenue), but competition, mix, amortisation and launch costs are all worse (which hurts margin). A conservative target would be one the sell-side already exceeds; here every broker on file sits below it. The company's qualitative steers, by contrast, are conservative in tone (limited visibility; controlled rollout; investment continues). For the model: the targets are a stretch case with perhaps one-in-three odds on the company's date and better odds a year later.

C-72

Specificity of guidance: building blocks or vague

❌ D40

Question asked How specific is the guidance: built from stated building blocks, or vague about how results come about?

Judgement. The operational building blocks are specific and the financial bridge is absent. Specific: four to five hubs; 175 perfusionists; tenfold capacity by Q4 2026; eight then about 30 heart centres in named countries; a PMA received 28 September 2026 with launch targeted end-2027; a liver 510(k) in H1 2027 for early 2028; 10 to 13 United States commercial hires; SEK 39 a year of CE-mark amortisation; kidney growth resuming early 2027. Absent: any numerical annual guidance; any bridge from the blocks to the 2027 margins; hub procedure volumes, utilisation or revenue; the size and timing of the largest customer's decline; services contract conversion timing; partnership economics; the amortisation profile of the 800.7 of capitalised development beyond the CE-mark asset. The transmission is therefore traceable from plan to operations and from operations to segment revenue and gross margin, and vague from there to EPS. The Consumer must build the bridge the company has not published.

C-85

Informal or qualitative commentary versus formal guidance and consensus

❌ D44

Question asked To what extent is informal/qualitative company commentary above or below formal guidance and sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

Judgement. The qualitative commentary is more cautious than consensus on timing and margin in the next twelve months and more ambitious than consensus beyond that, and the Consumer should weight the near-term caution because it has been the more accurate half of management's communication. Near term, below consensus in spirit: "many" service contracts taking longer than anticipated; kidney constrained until early 2027; European liver growth moderating; the Q3 margin partly seasonal; investment in commercial capabilities, infrastructure and supply chain continuing; the United States heart launch now "targeted" and "subject to review and approval"; sales to the largest customer to decline. Longer term, above consensus in spirit: standard of care for all donor hearts; 2027 a springboard to 2028; the 10x value of a perfusion run; a market 100 times larger; the 2027 margin targets retained. Consensus (26.8 percent EBITDA in FY2027; heart 181; lung 647) sits between the two, nearer the ambition on margin and nearer the caution on heart timing. The pattern across the last two years is that management's qualitative caution on commercial pace proved right and its regulatory optimism proved early, so informal commentary should be read as a leading indicator for the near-year numbers.

C-73

Other material or uncommon aspects of guidance or investor relations

❌ D44

Question asked What else is material or uncommon about the company's guidance or investor relations?

Judgement. Six things are uncommon enough to carry. (1) No numerical guidance at all in a year with three simultaneous launches, which is rarer than it sounds for a Nordic small cap and shifts the forecasting burden entirely to four brokers. (2) The company publishes a consensus compilation before results (19 October 2026), which makes beats and misses visible and well defined. (3) Coverage is thin and concentrated: DNB Carnegie, RedEye (whose analyst discloses owning the shares), Pareto and Stifel, plus a restricted broker; only one post-results note was accessible for Q3, so the sell-side response to Q3 is not yet observable. (4) The CFO, who is the investor-relations principal, is also running strategy and the services turnaround and has no named successor. (5) Segment and operating-metric disclosure is better than most peers' (gross margin by segment; EVLP disposable growth; heart sales; hub count), which lets the case be tracked quarterly even without guidance. (6) The company discloses capitalised development and now the CE-mark amortisation explicitly, which is helpful, but does not reconcile reported EBITDA to a cash-earnings view, which the Industry Digest flags as the sector's characteristic trap. Also notable: Bure, the largest holder, reports on Xvivo's trading in its own interim reports, which is an unusual second channel of disclosure.
