Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Xvivo Perfusion AB (XVIVO-SE), a Gothenburg-listed organ-preservation and machine-perfusion company selling proprietary preservation fluids (Perfadex Plus, STEEN Solution), perfusion machines (XPS for lung, Heart Assist Transport, Liver Assist, Kidney Assist Transport) and single-use kits, plus United States organ-recovery, normothermic regional perfusion and FlowHawk workflow-software services; reported in three segments, Thoracic (lung and heart), Abdominal (liver and kidney) and Services

Request: T-P1

Peers: TransMedics Group (TMDX-US), the only direct listed machine-perfusion peer, with a vertically integrated service and logistics model whose margin path shows what a services-heavy perfusion business earns; Terumo Corporation (4543-JP) for its Organ Technologies segment (OrganOx), the best-funded liver-perfusion rival, which reports segment revenue and margin; Getinge (GETI.B-SE) for Paragonix, a static-container and multi-organ competitor inside a large medtech cost base; Ambu (AMBU.B-DK), a Nordic single-use consumables razor-and-blade business whose 2022 to 2025 margin turnaround is the sector's cleanest precedent for operating leverage on a disposables base; Bonesupport (BONEX-SE), a Nordic single-consumable company scaling a United States launch at very high gross margins; Vitrolife (VITR-SE), a Nordic consumables-and-equipment franchise now cutting costs after serial target misses; private competitors Bridge to Life (VitaSmart, hypothermic liver perfusion, cleared by the FDA in January 2026, raised USD 110 million in August 2026) and Organ Recovery Systems (LifePort, the United States kidney-perfusion incumbent) for pricing and cost behaviour in cold perfusion Inputs: What is the split of fixed and variable costs across cost of goods, selling (37 percent of sales in 2025 per the 2025 annual report), administration (10 percent) and research and development (16 percent expensed; 152.8 million Swedish kronor capitalised in 2025, 131.7 million in the first nine months of 2026), and how much of the selling line is the United States field force, surgical roster and hub teams added in 2025 and 2026 (headcount 207 at September 2026 per the Q3 2026 interim report)? How did margins move with volume in the company's own up-cycle (trailing EBITDA margin from 5 percent in Q2 2022 to 18 percent in Q2 2024; a record 25 percent adjusted EBITDA margin in Q4 2025 after the H2 2025 cost programme) and in its down-cycle (Q2 2025 revenue -15 percent and EBITDA margin 13 percent), and what did the Q3 2026 quarter (revenue +34 percent, EBITDA +45 percent, gross margin 70 percent) show about incremental margins once the 19 million Swedish kronor of timing-related thoracic revenue is removed? What did the H2 2025 cost and cash programme actually deliver, which lines it cut, and how much of it reversed as 2026 launch spending resumed? Which cost lines in the United States organ-recovery services unit (gross margin -19 percent in Q3 2026 against +37 percent a year earlier, per the Q3 2026 presentation) are fixed surgical capacity, what utilisation level returns it to the industry's 18 to 33 percent gross-margin range, and what precedents exist for cutting or exiting such a unit? How feasible is the company's stated path from a 61 percent abdominal gross margin (Q3 2026) to its 70 percent 2027 target through the Netherlands-to-Sweden manufacturing consolidation and the tenfold disposable-capacity project completing in Q4 2026, given management's statement (Q2 2026 call) that it receives no volume-based supplier discounts and that unit-cost savings take one to 1.5 years? What amortisation will the 800.7 million Swedish kronor of capitalised development at end-2025, the 39 million Swedish kronor a year of CE-mark amortisation the company has disclosed, and the heart PMA and liver regulatory assets produce, and over what periods? For TransMedics, Terumo's Organ Technologies and Ambu: how did gross and operating margins move with volume through their launch and scale-up phases, with figures and dates, and what cost programmes did they announce and deliver? Focus: How much of Xvivo's cost base is genuinely fixed so that 20 percent-plus revenue growth converts to the EBITDA margin above 30 percent that management targets for 2027, against a loss-making services node, a manufacturing transfer and three simultaneous launches, and whether any peer with a services or logistics leg has reached a 30 percent EBITDA margin.

Request: T-P2

Peers: TransMedics Group (TMDX-US), the price leader at USD 80,000 to 100,000 per case whose customers increasingly resist its pricing; Terumo Corporation (4543-JP) for OrganOx, priced at roughly USD 30,000 to 45,000 per case and taking liver share on cost; Getinge (GETI.B-SE) for Paragonix static containers, the low-cost incumbent alternative in heart and lung transport; Ambu (AMBU.B-DK), whose 2024 repricing of legacy contracts and subsequent volume losses are the sector's clearest recent precedent for pricing power that was spent; Vitrolife (VITR-SE), which holds a 20 to 30 percent price premium in culture media and faces consolidated clinic buyers; private competitors Bridge to Life (VitaSmart at about USD 15,000 to 20,000 per case; NICE-recommended in the United Kingdom), Organ Recovery Systems and Global Transplant Solutions (which, with Bridge to Life, each hold roughly a third of the United States preservation-solution market per an October 2026 expert interview), and Traferox Technologies (X°Port cleared by the FDA in 2025; TorEx pending) Operating conditions: A United States hospital-budget squeeze with flat organ-acquisition reimbursement and widespread staffing shortages; the transition of European heart sales from compassionate-use and derogation pricing (7 percent of H1 2026 revenue per DNB Carnegie, August 2026) to commercial pricing after the 11 August 2026 CE mark and French national reimbursement of 18 May 2026; the entry of a cheaper hypothermic liver device (Bridge to Life) into Xvivo's European liver franchise, where Xvivo reports over 50 percent share; the largest customer (Lung Bioengineering, a United Therapeutics subsidiary, 132 million Swedish kronor of 2025 revenue per the 2025 annual report) moving to its own approved EVLP platform while continuing to buy STEEN Solution and components; a strengthening Swedish krona against the dollar and euro with United States tariffs on European-made disposables; and the United States heart launch targeted for the end of 2027 against TransMedics' installed base Inputs: What price increases has Xvivo achieved and held in lung consumables (pricing actions preserved an 84 percent disposable gross margin through 2023 inflation per the 2023 annual report; price rises to offset tariffs and the weaker dollar in Q1 2026 per the Q1 2026 report), and what price has it given back in abdominal (abdominal gross margin from 68 percent in Q2 2025 to 57 percent in Q2 2026 on "temporary price pressure in key markets" and a lower-priced kidney mix in emerging markets, per the Q2 2026 interim report)? What do transplant centres and OPOs pay per run for STEEN Solution (an expert cites about USD 21,000 per three-litre run), XPS kits (about USD 40,000 per run) and Perfadex, how do those prices compare with TransMedics, OrganOx, Bridge to Life and Paragonix per case, and at what price points do centres build their own circuits or insource? How are Xvivo's contracts structured (per-procedure bundles, device amortisation inside consumable pricing, finance leases on XPS placements as recognised in Q3 2026, OPO hub agreements, the Perfusion Solution Inc partnership) and what pass-through, indexation or currency clauses do they carry? What pricing will commercial European heart sales command relative to compassionate-use pricing, country by country, and how do the Netherlands, Belgium, Austria, the United Kingdom, Germany and Italy reimburse machine perfusion? What price will STEEN and components fetch from Lung Bioengineering once it runs its own platform? What do surgeons, OPO executives, hospital procurement and the sell-side say now about Xvivo's pricing versus TransMedics' in heart ahead of a United States launch, and about hypothermic liver pricing after Bridge to Life's clearance and NICE recommendation? For Ambu and Vitrolife, what happened to volumes and share after their price actions, with dates and figures? Focus: Whether Xvivo can hold real prices in lung fluids and set commercial heart prices in Europe and the United States at a sustained premium to cold storage without losing adoption, and whether abdominal pricing has further to fall as funded low-cost rivals and consolidated buyers converge on the European liver franchise.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: The investment case rests on two loops whose direction is contested in the evidence: a reinforcing loop in lung (each EVLP run and hub adds evidence, installed base and consumption, which drew United States EVLP disposable growth of 183 percent in Q3 2026) against a balancing loop in which the largest customer, premium consumable pricing and company-run services push large centres to insource, which the Lung Bioengineering platform approval and the services unit's six quarters of decline have now made visible. Peers: TransMedics Group (TMDX-US), whose National OCS Program is the sector's clearest reinforcing loop (service density drives case volume drives logistics efficiency) and whose gross margin fell to about 58 percent funding it; Terumo Corporation (4543-JP) for OrganOx, which is scaling a rental-and-consumables loop in liver; Ambu (AMBU.B-DK), a razor-and-blade installed-base loop in single-use endoscopy now meeting low-cost Chinese entry; Vitrolife (VITR-SE), where clinic consolidation turned a validation lock-in loop into a buyer-power balancing loop; private peers Bridge to Life and Strata Critical Medical (a listed logistics and recovery provider, STRA-US, that competes with device makers bundling services) for the balancing loops in liver pricing and in third-party recovery services Inputs: Does the OPO hub-and-spoke model create a reinforcing loop in lung (more hubs, more spokes, more runs, more evidence, more hubs), what procedure volumes and consumable pull-through have the three activated hubs produced (undisclosed by the company), and what stopped the Oklahoma OPO's EVLP programme? Does customer insourcing form a balancing loop (premium pricing and company-run services push the largest centres and United Therapeutics to build their own platforms, which cuts Xvivo's volume leverage), and how far could it spread beyond Lung Bioengineering to UPMC, Mass General and other centres reported to build homemade circuits? Does the European heart launch create a reinforcing loop through the 15-clinic trial network (about 25 percent of European heart centres per DNB Carnegie's 2023 initiation) and Australia's 40 to 50 percent penetration, and what balancing loop do country reimbursement delays and surgeon conservatism impose? Does the services unit create a reinforcing loop with product adoption (surgical capacity supports the United States heart launch and lifts XPS utilisation) or a balancing loop (negative margins consume cash and hospital distrust of company-driven services slows adoption), and what did TransMedics' and Strata's experience show? Does capitalised regulatory spending form a loop in which reported EBITDA funds continued capitalisation, and what happens to the loop when approvals end it? Does the DCD donor shift and OPO decertification pressure reinforce perfusion adoption across all suppliers, or does OPO consolidation let the survivors internalise perfusion and bypass device-makers' services? Focus: Which of the lung reinforcing loop and the customer-insourcing balancing loop dominates lung revenue over the next 18 months to four years, and whether the services unit is a loop that supports the heart launch or a drain that the loop cannot repay.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: The earnings-per-share bridge from EBITDA is the least documented part of this case: the company gives no numerical guidance, it has disclosed 39 million Swedish kronor a year of CE-mark amortisation (Q3 2026 interim report) on top of 800.7 million Swedish kronor of capitalised development at end-2025 (2025 annual report), the reported tax rate has run from 2.9 percent to 34.7 percent in three years, and financial items carry unhedged currency revaluation. Peers: TransMedics Group (TMDX-US), for how a perfusion company's amortisation, tax and share count behaved through its heart and liver launches; Terumo Corporation (4543-JP) for OrganOx segment margins and amortisation after acquisition; Ambu (AMBU.B-DK) and Vitrolife (VITR-SE), Nordic medtech peers with capitalised development policies (Vitrolife capitalised 63 million Swedish kronor in 2025 amortised over five to 20 years) and unhedged or lightly hedged currency exposure; Bonesupport (BONEX-SE), for the tax and cost profile of a Nordic company scaling United States revenue; Getinge (GETI.B-SE) for Paragonix purchase-accounting amortisation Inputs: The amortisation schedule for Xvivo's capitalised development (800.7 million Swedish kronor at end-2025; amortisation 20.1 million in 2025 against 152.8 million capitalised; 131.7 million capitalised in the first nine months of 2026; 39 million a year over ten years from the CE-mark asset per the Q3 2026 interim report): what is capitalised for the heart PMA and the liver programme, when does each begin to amortise, over what life, and when does capitalisation fall as the regulatory programmes end? Depreciation on the tenfold capacity project and Swedish production facilities. The tax rate: the Swedish statutory rate, the drivers of the 34.7 percent reported rate in 2025 (a 66.6 million Swedish kronor currency loss in financial items per the 2025 annual report), the effect of United States profits as the heart launches, deferred tax assets and the intercompany structure (intercompany revenues were 58 percent of parent transactions in 2025). Financial items: the size of unhedged monetary assets (about 205 million Swedish kronor per the 2025 annual report), the currency mix of revenue (about 55 percent USD, 35 percent EUR) and costs, the absence of any hedging policy, and the sensitivity management has published (a 10 percent dollar move is about 45 million Swedish kronor of sales and about two points of EBITDA margin). Interest on the EUR 20 million revolving facility (EUR 8 million drawn; maturing January 2028) and lease liabilities. The share count: 31.5 million shares, the board's 10 percent issuance authority, dilution of up to 1.35 percent from incentive programmes, and the probability and size of an equity raise if launches slip. One-offs: inventory write-downs (Q4 2025 and Q3 2026), finance-lease recognition (8.5 million Swedish kronor in Q3 2026), FlowHawk integration costs, and the risk of impairment of the services unit or FlowHawk goodwill. What DNB Carnegie, RedEye, Pareto and Stifel assume for each of these lines in their published 2026 to 2028 models, and how far apart they are. Focus: How much of the EBITDA growth the sell-side models for 2027 and 2028 reaches earnings per share after amortisation of the regulatory assets, a normalising but rising tax rate and unhedged currency, and whether the depreciation-and-amortisation and tax lines in consensus are consistent with the company's own disclosures.

Layer 2

Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

9 questions in 1 group · 12,020 words · written 23 October 2026, 13:48 to 14:07 UK, Fable, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
Xvivo (XVIVO-SE): second set of questions, the operating and financial model for the next 18 months to four years

Written 23 October 2026, the day after the Q3 2026 report. All money is SEK millions unless stated; margins are on the company's own presentation unless marked FactSet; "consensus" is the FactSet median of 22 October 2026 (latest estimate date 6 October 2026, so it predates the Q3 report). Where I disagree with the first set I say so in the answer concerned.

Orientation for the Consumer, in brief

  • The model I arrive at is a revenue line near but below consensus to FY2027, materially below it from FY2028 because I date the United States heart launch a year after the company's target, and an EBITDA margin that rises from 20 percent to about 30 percent over four years rather than reaching the company's 30 percent target in 2027. The largest single difference from consensus is not revenue or margin but the lines below EBITDA: amortisation of regulatory assets, a tax rate that normalises from about zero in 2026, and currency gains that do not recur. Together they make FY2027 adjusted EPS roughly flat on FY2026 (about 3.8 in each year) against consensus of 3.38 and 6.08, and FY2028 EPS about 6.1 against 9.47.
  • Cash is the opposite story. Because working capital released about 190 in the first nine months of 2026 and capitalised development falls once the regulatory programmes end, free cash flow is positive from FY2027 on my figures (about +21 in FY2027, +123 in FY2028) against consensus of 0 and 103, and the balance sheet needs no equity. Earnings quality improves as reported earnings disappoint: the Consumer should expect the sell-side to cut EPS and the company to build cash at the same time.
  • The five judgements the model rests on, in order of importance: (1) the lung consumables engine excluding the largest customer grows 25 to 30 percent a year and the largest customer's purchases fall by roughly 65 in 2027, so lung revenue grows only mid-single digits in 2027 and about 18 percent in 2028; (2) European heart revenue builds to about 155 in 2027 and 275 in 2028, a two-quarter lag behind the sell-side; (3) operating costs grow 13 percent in 2027 and about 20 percent a year in 2028 and 2029 as the United States launches are built, so operating leverage shows mainly from 2028; (4) depreciation and amortisation reaches 120 in 2027 and 185 in 2029, 10 to 25 above the consensus gap; (5) the tax rate is about 5 percent in FY2026 (nine-month actual near zero) and 20 to 23 percent thereafter.

Financial forecasts

C529 questions
P-0158P-0256P-0350P-0445P-0555P-0642P-0838P-0955P-1056

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The financial forecasts the foundational components support are those of a 23 percent-a-year revenue grower whose EBITDA margin rises from 20 percent to about 30 percent over four years, whose earnings per share stall for one year (FY2027) while the 2026 gifts of a zero tax rate and currency gains are repaid by a full year of regulatory amortisation and a normal tax charge, and whose free cash flow turns positive in 2027 and compounds thereafter as the capitalisation cycle ends. The operating leverage is large and demonstrated but switched off by management's choice to keep investing through three launches, so it appears at EBITDA from 2027 and at EBIT only from 2028; the financial leverage is nil and the real amplifier is an unhedged krona worth about 1.0 of EPS per 10 percent move; the efficiency bridges (services utilisation, abdominal unit cost) are worth four to five points of margin and arrive in 2028 to 2029 rather than 2027; price contributes one point of growth in 2027 through the heart conversion and nothing thereafter; the reinforcing loops in lung and heart dominate the balancing loops from 2028 but are tied with them in 2027, the year in which the largest customer's step-down lands; the record says to lean revenue slightly below consensus, margins two to four points below, and dates two to four quarters late; and the items below operating profit are where the model departs furthest from consensus, taking FY2027 and FY2028 EPS to 3.8 and 6.1 against 6.08 and 9.47. The net for the Consumer: the next 18 months are a transition from an inflated reported growth peak to a lower and more honest earnings base, in which the business improves on every operating and cash measure while reported EPS disappoints the sell-side; the forecasts for 18 months to four years are positive for the business and negative against expectations, and the single event that decides which the market weighs is the FDA's handling of the heart PMA through 2027. For the investment case the forecasts are moderately positive on fundamentals and a headwind on estimates, which argues for a case built on cash, balance-sheet strength and a strategic floor rather than on earnings momentum.

P-01

Fixed-cost operating leverage: how it shapes the main line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years

C58

Question asked After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

Judgement. The operating leverage is large in structure, demonstrated in the record, and currently switched off by choice; it will reappear in two steps, a modest one in 2027 as timing items and the manufacturing transfer wash out, and a large one from 2028 as the heart and liver launches add revenue at thoracic-grade gross margins onto a cost base that management will, by then, have mostly built. The Consumer should model it as an incremental EBITDA margin of about 45 percent on added revenue from 2027 onwards, which is what the company delivered in its last clean growth year (2024), rather than the 25 percent reported incremental margin of Q3 2026 or the 0 to 15 percent underlying incremental margin of that quarter once the timing items are removed. The resulting EBITDA margin path is 20 percent (FY2026), 24 (FY2027), 27 (FY2028), 29.5 (FY2029) and 31.5 (FY2030), against a last-four-year average of about 16 percent and a consensus path of 20.7, 26.8, 28.9 and 34.1 percent.

What the structure is. Gross margin runs at 70 to 74 percent (company basis) with thoracic products at 80 to 85 percent, and the operating-cost base excluding depreciation was about 440 in 2025 (54 percent of sales) and runs at about 500 in 2026 (Q3 operating costs of 150 less about 24 of depreciation and amortisation, annualised). The cost base is mostly people (207 heads; selling costs 37 percent of sales), and it is fixed in the sense that it does not fall with volume (Q2 2025: revenue -15 percent, EBITDA margin 13 percent) but it is not fixed in the sense of being capped: it grew 14 percent in 2026 on the United States field-force build, hub teams and launch preparation. So the leverage exists mechanically and is being spent on discretion, which is why a 20 percent margin has persisted from 2024 through 2026 on revenue that rose from 822 to about 990.

What the record shows, as incremental margins. FY2023 to FY2024: revenue +224, EBITDA (brokers' basis) +98, an incremental margin of 44 percent with the cost base growing about 25 percent. FY2024 to FY2025: revenue -10, EBITDA -18, so the base absorbed none of the shortfall. Q3 2026 as reported: revenue +64 year on year, EBITDA +16, a 25 percent incremental margin; but about 19 of the revenue was timing (8.5 of finance-lease recognition at nearly full margin and about 10 of customer inventory at thoracic margin), worth about 17 of EBITDA, and inventory write-downs took about 8 off gross profit. Removing both, underlying Q3 EBITDA was about 43 on revenue of about 234, an 18 percent margin and an underlying incremental margin of roughly 15 percent on the year-earlier quarter. That is the honest reading of 2026: the cost base is absorbing almost all of the growth, which is what management said it would do ("investment continues") and is the reason the first set's two statements about operating costs (growing "at two-thirds the rate of revenue" in the economic-strength group; "at least in line with revenue through 2027" in the management group) both have evidence behind them: the first describes 2024, the second describes 2026.

How it shapes the lines, by period.

  • Q4 2026 to Q4 2027 (the next five quarters). I model operating costs excluding depreciation growing about 13 percent in 2027 (to about 560) against revenue growth of about 17 percent, and gross margin recovering from about 70 to about 72 percent as the Q3 write-downs do not recur, heart converts from compassionate to commercial pricing and abdominal recovers to about 63 percent. That gives EBITDA of about 278 on revenue of 1,160, a 24 percent margin and an incremental margin on the year of about 47 percent. Quarterly, the margin runs 20 percent in Q4 2026 (seasonal revenue strength offset by year-end costs and the first full quarter of launch hiring), 21 in Q1 2027, 23.5 in Q2, 24.5 in Q3 (summer activity lowers costs, as in 2026) and 26.5 in Q4 2027. The three things that would put the 2027 margin at 21 to 22 percent rather than 24: a repeat of the Q3 2026 pattern in which hiring absorbs the growth, a slower abdominal recovery because the Netherlands-to-Sweden transfer runs late, and the largest customer stepping down faster than the 65 I assume. The things that would put it at 26 to 27: management pausing spend as it did in H2 2025 (which produced four to five points in two quarters), or European heart arriving at the sell-side's 181 rather than my 155.
  • 2028 to 2030. The leverage becomes large because the added revenue is mostly heart and liver consumables at 80 percent-plus gross margin, while the cost base, even growing about 20 percent a year through the United States launches, falls from 50 percent of sales to about 44 percent by 2030. EBITDA margin reaches 27 percent in 2028 and about 30 percent in 2029, which is when the company's "2027" target is met on my model. Against the last four to six years (EBITDA margin 8, 12, 22, 20 percent in FY2022 to FY2025 on FactSet's operating basis), the model's 2028 to 2030 is a different company at the margin line; against consensus it is one to four points lower in every year, and the difference is almost entirely my assumption that management keeps investing through the United States launches.

The EBIT line carries less of the leverage than EBITDA. Depreciation and amortisation rises from about 85 (FY2026, excluding acquired-intangible amortisation) to 120, 150, 185 and 205, so the EBIT margin path is 11.5, 13.6, 16.8, 19.5 and 22.4 percent: the company's "EBIT above 20 percent by 2027" is a 2029 to 2030 event. This is the single most important consequence of the operating leverage question for the Consumer: the leverage that the gross margin and the cost base create between 2027 and 2030 is split roughly 80/20 between EBITDA and the amortisation of the assets that made the launches possible, so EBITDA growth of 3.6x over FY2025 to FY2030 becomes EBIT growth of about 5.4x from a much lower base and EPS growth of about 3.2x from the 2026 base.

Peers, as a check. Ambu lifted its EBIT margin from 2.7 to 13 percent in two years on a disposables base once growth was believed, and targets about 20 percent by FY2027/28; Terumo's Organ Technologies (OrganOx) moved from 13 to 21 percent adjusted margin in a year of 50 percent growth, then back to 15 percent on production optimisation costs; TransMedics, the only perfusion company with a services and logistics leg, expects to approach a 30 percent operating margin only by 2028 after a decade of launches. Xvivo's product gross margin is 20 points above TransMedics', which is why 30 percent EBITDA is attainable for Xvivo with a services node, but the peer set says it takes a launch cycle to get there, not a year.

P-02

Financial leverage: how it shapes earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years

C56

Question asked After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement. There is no debt leverage to amplify anything, and on my cash-flow path there will be none within the horizon: net cash of about 193 (FactSet definition, leases in debt; about 230 excluding leases) becomes about 170 at end-2026 (a Q4 working-capital rebuild and about 50 of investing), about 185 at end-2027, about 305 at end-2028 (the revolver's repayment in January 2028 reduces cash and debt equally) and about 545 at end-2029. The amplifier at Xvivo is currency, and it is large: a 10 percent move in USD/SEK changes EBITDA by about 16 (two margin points) and, through the revaluation of 250 to 300 of mostly foreign-currency cash, net financial items by 25 to 30, so a 10 percent krona move in one year is worth roughly 1.0 of adjusted EPS, a quarter of my FY2027 figure. The Consumer should carry financial items as a line with a central estimate near zero and a cone of plus or minus 30 a year, and should treat the consensus macro path (a stronger krona) as a modest headwind to reported EPS in 2027 that my inputs include as -8.

The balance sheet path, line by line (my base case). Operating cash flow of about 214 in FY2026 (182 in nine months, lifted by the release of about 190 of working capital as the 2025 inventory build unwound), 221 in FY2027, 304 in FY2028, 429 in FY2029 and 563 in FY2030. Capital spending including intangibles of about 221, 185, 165, 170 and 175: the fall from 2026 comes from the tenfold capacity project completing in Q4 2026 and capitalised development falling from about 175 a year to about 100 to 130 as the heart PMA moves from trial to review and the liver programme moves from a 215-patient trial to a 510(k). Lease payments of about 15 a year. Free cash flow of about -21, +21, +123, +242 and +370. The EUR 20 million revolver (EUR 8 million, about 90 SEK, drawn) matures in January 2028 and is repaid from cash on my path. No dividend, no buyback, no acquisition. Incentive-plan dilution of about 0.1 million shares a year (1.35 percent maximum across all plans).

What financial leverage does to EPS: nothing, and that is itself a finding. Consensus net income rises from 103 (FY2026) to 465 (FY2029) with net financial items near zero and a flat share count; so does mine, from 120 to 278. Interest income on 300 to 600 of cash at 2 to 3 percent is 6 to 15 a year and interest on the revolver and leases is about 4, so the net interest line is immaterial in every year. There is no deleveraging contribution, no buyback contribution and, on my path, no dilution contribution. The first set's C-76 judgement (comfortable balance sheet; cash drifting down toward 200 to 250 before rebuilding; an equity raise in view by 2028 if the United States launch slips) is right in direction but, after Q3, too cautious on the cash trough: the working-capital release means the trough is already behind the company (cash 313 at Q3 2026 against 280 a year earlier) and the balance sheet would absorb a one-year FDA delay without a raise, because the delay would also delay the launch spending.

Where the leverage is: currency, sized. Sales are about 55 percent USD and 35 percent EUR against a largely SEK and EUR cost base, with no hedging policy or instruments. Management's own sensitivity: a 10 percent USD move is about 45 of sales and about two points of EBITDA margin; the annual report's all-currency figure is 13 of operating income per 10 percent krona move (which I read as the net after the United States cost base is netted, so the EBITDA sensitivity to the dollar alone is nearer 16). Financial items carried +32.7 of currency in 2024, -66.6 in 2025 and +19.2 in the first nine months of 2026 on cash alone. The macro path on file (Intesa Sanpaolo: USD/SEK 9.15 by end-2026 and 8.67 by late 2027 from 9.70 in mid-2026; a Riksbank hike likely in November 2026) implies a 5 to 10 percent krona strengthening against the dollar across the next 18 months, which would take 2 to 4 percent off reported revenue growth, about one to two points off the EBITDA margin relative to constant currency, and 15 to 25 off financial items through cash revaluation. My inputs carry roughly half of that: revenue growth at reported rates about two points below constant-currency rates in 2027, and net financial items of -8 in 2027 and -5 in 2028. A dollar that holds at 9.5 to 9.7 would add about 0.5 to FY2027 EPS; a dollar at 8.5 would take about 0.7 off.

The equity-raise question, answered. The board holds authority to issue up to 10 percent (3.15 million shares). I put the probability of its use for cash inside the horizon at under 10 percent, and only in a path where the FDA requests a new heart trial (a two-year delay) while services losses continue and management keeps the launch organisation intact; even that path keeps cash above about 150 on my figures, so a raise would be a choice to fund a new trial rather than a necessity. The authorisation's stated purpose is acquisitions; a bolt-on funded by shares is the more likely use, and I model none.

P-03

Cost elimination and efficiency: how much reaches the main line items, and when

C50

Question asked After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

Judgement. About five to six points of EBITDA margin are available from three identified efficiency bridges by 2029 (services from negative gross margin to about 15 percent; abdominal gross margin from 61 to the high 60s; the end of regulatory capitalisation, which reaches free cash flow rather than EBITDA), and I model about four of them arriving, back-end loaded: roughly one point in 2027, two more in 2028 and the rest in 2029. None of it is "cost elimination" in the sense of a programme that removes cost; all of it is utilisation and unit cost, which depend on volume arriving. The one true cost-cutting lever, the H2 2025-style pause that releases three to four points in two quarters, exists and should be held in a downside case only; in the base case management does not use it, and it should not be modelled as upside because using it would mean the launches had failed.

Bridge 1: the services unit (worth 1.5 to 2 points of group margin). Services revenue of about 60 in FY2026 at roughly zero gross margin for the year (nine months +4 percent, Q3 -19 percent) means the unit costs the group about 10 a year at the Q3 run-rate, against a gross profit of about 25 at the 2024 level (88 of revenue at about 30 percent). The cost is fixed surgical capacity hired for the United States heart launch and for OPO contracts that "are taking longer than originally anticipated" to convert; per-case charging replaced the old daily-availability model in 2024, so the unit's margin is a utilisation problem, not a pricing one. TransMedics' service gross margin (27 to 35 percent in 2026 on 66 million dollars a quarter) is the ceiling for a well-run node of this kind; Strata's asset-light model shows a third party can do it 40 percent cheaper, which caps the price. I model services revenue flat at about 62 in 2027 with gross margin at zero, then 72 at 8 percent in 2028 and 90 at 15 percent in 2029 as the United States heart launch gives the surgical roster the volume it was hired for. The swing from -10 to +14 of gross profit is about 24, two points of margin at 2027 revenue, arriving mostly in 2028 and 2029. If management instead cut the roster back to volume (as it halted new service contracts for six months in 2025), the swing would come in 2027 but would remove the launch infrastructure the heart plan depends on; I do not model it.

Bridge 2: abdominal unit cost (worth 1.5 to 2.5 points). Abdominal gross margin was 64 percent in 2025, 57 percent for nine months 2026 (mix toward lower-priced kidney, emerging-market pricing, the Netherlands-to-Sweden transfer and write-downs) and 61 percent in Q3. The company's 70 percent target for 2027 rests on the tenfold disposable-capacity project (about 50 of capital, completing Q4 2026) and consolidation to Sweden; management told investors it receives no volume discounts and that unit-cost savings take one to 1.5 years, which dates the benefit to H2 2027 at the earliest and mostly 2028. Abdominal disposables reached 69 to 70 percent in 2023 before the mix shifted, so the level is achievable; the mix (kidney growing faster than liver; Bridge to Life and OrganOx pricing in liver) argues it is achievable later and perhaps at 67 to 68 rather than 70. I model 59 percent for FY2026 (Q4 recovering to about 62), 63 for 2027, 66 for 2028, 68 for 2029 and 69 for 2030. At 2027 abdominal revenue of about 318, each point is about 3.2 of gross profit, so the path adds about 13 in 2027 (1.1 points of group margin), a further 12 in 2028 and 10 in 2029.

Bridge 3: the end of capitalisation (worth nothing to EBITDA, 50 to 100 a year to free cash flow, and a negative to EBIT). Capitalised development of 152.8 in 2025 and 131.7 in nine months 2026 is the sector's "false friend": it produces the 20 percent EBITDA margin and becomes amortisation. Its ending is an efficiency in cash terms only. I model intangible investment falling from about 175 in 2026 to about 130 in 2027 (heart PMA in review; CAP study with CMS cost recovery; liver 510(k) and a United States clinical evaluation; kidney United States work) and about 100 a year from 2028. The counterpart is that amortisation of the regulatory assets rises (see P-08), so the "saving" improves free cash flow by 50 to 75 a year from 2027 and reduces EBIT at the same time. The Consumer must not double count: the EBITDA margin path in P-01 already assumes expensed R&D stays at about 15 to 16 percent of sales; if the company expensed what it now capitalises, the EBITDA margin would be 10 to 12 points lower in every year and EBIT unchanged.

Smaller items. Administration has already fallen from 12 to 10 percent of sales and I model it at about 8 percent by 2030 (0.5 point a year of leverage, included in P-01). FlowHawk integration costs (2.3 in 2025) are immaterial. Back-office scaling, the field-force reallocation to the South and West and sourcing redesigns are real but unquantified and I give them no separate credit.

When it reaches the lines, summarised. 2027: about one point of group gross margin from abdominal and the absence of write-downs, no services contribution, no net cost reduction; the margin rise from 20 to 24 percent in P-01 is two-thirds operating leverage and one-third these bridges. 2028: two further points (abdominal, services starting). 2029 to 2030: one to two further points, with services reaching mid-teens gross margin. Free cash flow: the capitalisation wind-down is the second-largest contributor (after EBITDA growth) to free cash flow moving from about -21 in 2026 to +123 in 2028.

P-04

Pricing power: how price contributes to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years

❌ D45

Question asked After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement. Price contributes about one point to group revenue growth in 2027 and nothing thereafter, and about half a point to gross margin in 2027 and nothing thereafter; the whole of the model's growth is volume and mix. The one positive price event, the conversion of European heart sales from compassionate-use and derogation pricing to commercial pricing after the August 2026 CE mark, is a step rather than a trend and is already partly in the Q3 and Q4 2026 run-rate; the one negative price trend, abdominal, is slow and is offset in the margin by unit cost rather than by price. Lung fluid pricing is flat in real terms and defended, which is a large positive for the margin's stability and no positive for its growth. I disagree with nothing in the first set's C-17 and C-45; this answer sizes what they described.

Lung consumables: nominal rises, real zero, held. Perfadex Plus holds about 90 percent of static lung preservation with patent cover to 2035, and no one else has STEEN or the XPS kit. The record is of price being used to defend margin: 84 percent disposable gross margin held through 2023 inflation; an approximately 5 percent United States price increase in November 2025 to offset tariffs and the weaker dollar; European contracts slower to reprice because of tenders. Medicare reimburses organ-acquisition costs on a reasonable-cost pass-through basis, which insulates the purchasing decision from hospital budget pressure for Medicare patients and is the structural reason the price holds; the counter-evidence (STEEN at about 7,000 dollars a litre described as "the limit"; UPMC and Mass General building their own circuits; the largest customer citing cost as a reason to insource) sets a ceiling rather than a decline. For the model: nominal price plus 2 to 4 percent a year in the United States when costs or tariffs rise, zero in Europe, real price zero; no contribution to margin expansion from lung price; a thoracic product gross margin of 82 to 85 percent held through the horizon except where machine placements dilute the mix (as in Q3 2026, 80 percent).

The largest customer: a price cut by another name. Lung Bioengineering's move to its own approved platform while continuing to buy STEEN and components is, in revenue terms, a volume loss (XPS kits and services) and, in price terms, a lower value per run. The components that remain are the highest-margin items, so the gross-profit loss is smaller than the revenue loss: of the roughly 65 of revenue I remove in 2027, I remove about 50 of gross profit. The contractual price of STEEN to the customer after the transition is not disclosed; the risk is a volume-tied discount, which would be the first real price concession in lung fluids. I do not model one.

Heart: the one real price lever, sized. Compassionate-use and derogation sales were 7 percent of H1 2026 revenue (about 33) and heart sales ran at 22 to 24 a quarter in Q2 and Q3 2026 under discounted trial and derogation pricing (management confirmed in 2023 that trial pricing was discounted). Jefferies puts the commercial cost of a Heart Assist run at about 40,000 dollars against about 85,000 for TransMedics' OCS; France has national reimbursement since May 2026; the Netherlands, Belgium, Austria and the United Kingdom are the Tier 2 markets with funding pathways being negotiated. The step from derogation to commercial pricing on the existing eight-centre volume is worth, on my estimate, 20 to 40 percent per case, that is roughly 20 to 30 of 2027 revenue at nearly full margin if the existing volume holds; the rest of my 2027 heart revenue of 155 (from about 90 in 2026) is new centres. Price in heart after the step: flat to slightly down as Tier 2 and Tier 3 countries negotiate tariffs, and set against two cheaper alternatives (Paragonix SherpaPak static containers, which Getinge says hold over 50 percent of United States heart and lung transport, and ice) rather than against TransMedics. In the United States from 2029, Xvivo's price is positioned at about half of TransMedics' per case and I assume it stays there; the margin on that price is still thoracic-grade because the product cost is low, so the United States launch is accretive to gross margin despite being priced to take share.

Abdominal: price down, offset by cost. The evidence is one-directional: abdominal gross margin from 68 percent (Q2 2025) to 54 to 57 percent (H1 2026) on "temporary price pressure in key markets" and a lower-priced kidney mix; Bridge to Life's VitaSmart at 15,000 to 20,000 dollars a case with NICE funding in the United Kingdom and a 110 million dollar raise to scale; OrganOx winning liver cases on cost at a third of TransMedics' price; Organ Recovery Systems holding about 95 percent of United States kidney perfusion. I model real abdominal price of -1 to -2 percent a year through the horizon, so the recovery of abdominal gross margin to the high 60s (P-03) is a unit-cost and mix outcome that price works against, and the 70 percent target is reached, if at all, in 2030. A faster price decline (VitaSmart taking European liver share in 2027) would hold abdominal gross margin at 60 to 62 and cost the group about a point of margin.

Services and FlowHawk: no pricing power. Per-case service pricing competes with Strata's 40 percent-cheaper local recovery model and with physician-led in-house teams; FlowHawk is small and its growth fell to zero in Q3. Both are priced to win adoption.

Net for the model. Price adds about 10 to 12 to 2027 revenue growth (the heart step net of abdominal and the lower value per run at the largest customer), nothing thereafter; price adds about half a point to 2027 gross margin and nothing thereafter; price is a stabiliser of the thoracic margin and a drag on the abdominal one. Peers confirm the shape: Ambu's 2024 repricing of legacy contracts lifted gross margin to 59.5 percent and then cost it volume (Anaesthesia and Patient Monitoring organic growth down to 1.6 percent by Q3 2025/26); Vitrolife held a 20 to 30 percent premium in culture media on clinical risk aversion and then lost high-volume clinics to Cooper on contract pricing. Xvivo's lung franchise is more defended than either (no generic equivalent), and its abdominal franchise is less defended than either (cheaper adequate rivals already cleared and funded).

P-05

Reinforcing or balancing feedback loops: how they shape the main line items over the next 18 months to four years

C55

Question asked After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement. Six loops are at work and they sort cleanly by line item. Two reinforcing loops drive the revenue line (the lung hub-and-spoke loop; the heart clinical-network loop) and they dominate their balancing counterparts (customer insourcing; reimbursement delay and surgeon conservatism) over the horizon as a whole but not in 2027, which is the one year in which the balancing loops are as large as the reinforcing ones in lung and the reinforcing loop in heart has not yet compounded. Two loops drive the margin line: the services loop is balancing now (negative gross margin consuming cash and slowing adoption at centres that distrust company-run services) and becomes mildly reinforcing only when the United States heart launch gives the roster volume, which on my dating is 2029; the abdominal pricing loop is balancing throughout. One loop, capitalisation, drives the gap between EBITDA and EBIT and between EBITDA and cash, and it reverses direction as approvals arrive: the reported margin gets worse while cash gets better. The sixth, the DCD and OPO-reform loop, is reinforcing for the industry's device volume and ambiguous for Xvivo's services. For the model, the net effect is a revenue line whose growth dips in 2027 (about 17 percent) and re-accelerates in 2028 to 2029 (about 27 and 25 percent), a gross margin that drifts up from 70 to 75 percent on mix, an EBITDA margin that lags revenue by a year, and an EBIT margin that lags EBITDA by a further year.

Loop 1, lung hub-and-spoke (reinforcing; dominant over the horizon, tied in 2027). More hubs mean more spokes, more EVLP runs, more consumables, more evidence and more hubs. Evidence it is running: three hubs activated against a four-to-five target; United States EVLP disposables +56, +69 and +183 percent in the three quarters of 2026 (+92 percent year to date); the first hub performed about 20 EVLP procedures in H1 2026; OneLegacy opened the first Western United States EVLP centre in July 2026; UPMC's utilisation rose from 5 to 10 percent of cases to far higher once Xvivo supplied on-site specialists. What stops it: the Oklahoma OPO bought an XPS and abandoned the programme for want of coordinators and volume, and the company discloses no hub throughput. The balancing loop against it is insourcing: premium pricing and company-run services pushed the largest customer (about 132 of 2025 revenue, about 150 in 2026 including the Q3 inventory build) to build and get approved its own platform; large academic centres (UPMC, Mass General, Bergamo) build ECMO-based circuits to avoid the 40,000-dollar kit. Sizing the two against each other for lung revenue: ex-customer, ex-timing lung revenue of about 425 in 2026 growing 25 to 30 percent (hubs, DCD mix, OPO pressure) adds 105 to 130 in 2027; the customer's step-down removes about 65 and the Q3 timing items remove 19. Net lung growth in 2027 about 5 to 8 percent (I use 625 against 580), then about 18 percent in 2028 once the step-down is in the base. The insourcing loop does not spread further on the evidence: circuit-builders still buy STEEN and Perfadex, no second centre has sought platform approval, and United Therapeutics' platform is designed for its own centralised facilities rather than for sale. The reinforcing loop therefore wins from 2028; in 2027 the two cancel.

Loop 2, heart clinical network (reinforcing; timed by a balancing loop). Fifteen European trial sites (about 25 percent of European heart centres) act as reference centres; the NIHP2019 and PRESERVE results (76 percent reduction in severe primary graft dysfunction; 92 versus 86 percent one-year survival; 92.1 percent 30-day success) convert conservative surgeons; Australia reached 40 to 50 percent penetration of brain-death heart transplants under a special-access scheme; derogation sales rose from 9 to 24 to 22 a quarter before the CE mark. The balancing loop is country reimbursement (France done; the Netherlands, Belgium, Austria and the United Kingdom negotiating; Germany without DCD and without a pathway) and clinical habit (a Northwest England surgeon still uses ice because it is "not very expensive compared to the Xvivo box"). The sector's rule, revenue a year after approval, dates the compounding to H2 2027. I therefore model heart at about 90 in 2026, 155 in 2027 (quarterly about 30, 33, 42, 50), 275 in 2028 and 420 in 2029 (of which about 90 United States), against the sell-side's 92.5, 181 and 336. The reinforcing loop is real and the first set's C-16 describes it well; I date it two quarters later than the sell-side because every date in this programme has slipped and because Tier 2 reimbursement is a negotiation, not a formality.

Loop 3, services (balancing now; conditionally reinforcing from 2029). The intended loop: surgical capacity supports the heart launch and lifts XPS utilisation, which pulls through consumables, which funds more capacity. The observed loop: capacity hired ahead of volume produces a -19 percent gross margin, which consumes about 10 a year of gross profit, while East Coast accounts churned to physician-led teams and contracts convert slowly, and hospital distrust of company-driven services (a pulmonary physician: commercial service models do not necessarily benefit hospital cost structures) slows the adoption the capacity was meant to speed. TransMedics shows the loop can be made to run (26 percent of United States thoracic and liver transplants through its National OCS Program) at the price of a 58 percent group gross margin and an owned fleet; Strata shows a third party can offer the service cheaper and technology-agnostic. For the model: services gross margin about zero in 2027, 8 percent in 2028, 15 percent in 2029; services revenue flat in 2027 and growing only with NRP and the United States heart launch thereafter. The loop is a drain through 2027 that the lung loop can repay (lung gross profit of about 500 in 2027 against a services drag of about 10), and whether it becomes a support depends on the United States heart approval, which is the same binary as the rest of the case.

Loop 4, abdominal pricing (balancing throughout). Each cheaper adequate device (OrganOx at a third of TransMedics' price; VitaSmart at a half of OrganOx's; NICE funding for the category; Terumo's plan to triple OrganOx revenue by FY2027) lowers the price a centre will pay for the next one, and each lost centre lowers Xvivo's volume leverage in abdominal manufacturing. Against it, the Italian pay-per-procedure model (Avionord; 20 to 25 percent penetration; 15 of 22 centres) is a small reinforcing loop in Xvivo's favour that the company intends to export. For the model: abdominal revenue growth of about 21 percent in 2027 and 24 to 27 percent in 2028 to 2029 (kidney unconstrained from early 2027; United States liver from early 2028 on the 510(k); United States kidney broader launch in 2027), gross margin recovering on cost not price (P-03), and no abdominal pricing contribution to group margin.

Loop 5, capitalisation (self-reinforcing while trials run; reverses on approval). Reported EBITDA (about 20 percent) funds continued capitalisation (about 175 a year), which keeps EBITDA at 20 percent, which is the margin the sell-side models from. Operating cash flow of 182 in nine months 2026 was almost entirely absorbed by investing of 171. The loop breaks in Xvivo's favour in cash and against it in reported profit: the CE mark converted a balance-sheet asset into 39 a year of amortisation from August 2026; the heart PMA (on approval, which I date to late 2028) and the liver 510(k) (early 2028) will add, on my estimate, about 35 and about 10 a year; capitalisation itself falls to about 100 a year by 2028. The lines affected are depreciation and amortisation (85, 120, 150, 185, 205 from FY2026 to FY2030), EBIT (lagging EBITDA by a widening absolute gap), and free cash flow (turning positive in 2027 and reaching about 240 in 2029). The first set's C-54 and the Industry Digest's "false friend" warning are confirmed by the Q3 numbers (write-downs, timing-assisted sales, FX-assisted EPS); the model's answer is to carry an explicit amortisation schedule and to test earnings quality on operating cash flow less investing, which was about +1 in nine months 2026 and should be about +20 in 2027 and +120 in 2028.

Loop 6, DCD donors and OPO reform (reinforcing for industry volume; ambiguous for Xvivo's services). DCD donors exceeded brain-death donors in the United States in 2025 and grow over 7 percent a year; DCD organs need assessment; the CMS tier rule (upheld 28 September 2026) decertifies Tier 3 OPOs from late 2026 and expires agreements in January 2027; underperformers must pursue marginal organs. Competitor growth of 37 to 65 percent across TransMedics, OrganOx and Paragonix is the proof that the loop runs for every supplier. The ambiguity is consolidation: surviving large OPOs have the space and scale to run in-house perfusion suites (a former Xvivo general manager; a Mid-America Transplant director reports voluntary mergers), which raises device demand and bypasses the services unit; and consolidated buyers demand discounts (the IVF clinic precedent that broke Vitrolife's lock-in). For the model: the loop supports lung device and consumable growth of 25 to 30 percent ex the largest customer, creates two quarters of contracting friction around January 2027 (which is part of why I phase Q1 2027 growth at only 14 percent), and gives services no help.

The two questions the research asked, answered. Which loop dominates lung revenue: the hub loop, from 2028; in 2027 the insourcing loop neutralises it, so lung grows mid-single digits for one year and the group's growth that year comes from heart and abdominal. Is services a loop that supports the heart launch or a drain the loop cannot repay: a drain through 2027 that the lung franchise repays several times over, and a support only if and when the United States heart is approved; it is not large enough to break the case and not productive enough to help it inside 18 months.

P-06

Track record of delivery: how much weight the forecasts should carry, and in which direction to lean

❌ D42

Question asked After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

Judgement. Weight the revenue forecasts moderately and lean them slightly below consensus to FY2027 and clearly below from FY2028; weight the margin forecasts lightly and lean them down by two to four points in FY2027 and FY2028; weight any date lightly and lean it late by two to four quarters; and give the FY2028 and FY2029 consensus figures, which rest on four and two brokers and on an FDA decision that has not been scheduled, the weight of a scenario rather than a forecast. The record supports exactly this asymmetry: the company has beaten sales consensus in four of the last five quarters and missed EBITDA consensus in two of the last four, has delivered every clinical milestone and no commercial date on time before 2026, and has guided qualitatively in a way that proved right on commercial pace and early on regulatory timing. My own forecasts lean the same way the record points: revenue 1 percent below consensus in FY2026, 4 percent below in FY2027 and 8 percent below in FY2028; EBITDA 3, 14 and 14 percent below; EPS above consensus in FY2026 (tax and currency), 37 percent below in FY2027 and 36 percent below in FY2028.

The record, by line and by horizon.

  • Revenue. Beats of +4, +4, +2 and +6 percent against consensus in the four quarters to Q3 2026; FY2025 1 percent light after a Q2 2025 miss of 15 percent that produced a 30 percent one-day fall. Five years of revenue met or beat the sell-side's direction in every year but 2025. The beats come in growth phases with easy comparatives; the Q3 2026 beat was about one-third timing. Lean: near consensus in the near term, below it when comparatives harden (from Q1 2027) and when a customer or a regulator sets the date.
  • Margins. EBITDA beats of +31 percent (Q4 2025) and +14 percent (Q3 2026); misses of -6 percent in Q1 and Q2 2026; quarterly EBITDA margins have ranged 13 to 25 percent over seven quarters on a flat annual average of 20. The sell-side cut FY2027 EBITDA by 6 percent and EPS by 13 percent over twelve months while holding revenue, and the reasons were operating costs and gross margin. Lean: down, because management has told investors twice (January 2026; October 2026) not to extrapolate a strong quarter and that investment continues, and because the two bridges the margin needs (abdominal unit cost; services utilisation) have not started to show in a full year.
  • Dates. The heart CE mark slipped six to twelve months on a component change; the kidney United States launch slipped from 2025 to 2026 to 2027 on product adjustments and a component replacement; the liver United States route changed from a 2029 PMA to a 2028 510(k) (an acceleration, but a change); the United States heart launch moved from "end-2027" to "targeted for end-2027, subject to FDA review and approval", and RedEye now models early 2028. The two dates that landed (CE mark in August; PMA receipt in September 2026) landed late against the original plan. Lean: late, by two to four quarters on commercial dates, which is why I place the United States heart launch in early 2029 against the company's end-2027 and RedEye's early 2028.
  • Guidance. None numerical, so no record of raising or missing it; the 2027 targets (EBITDA margin above 30 percent, EBIT above 20 percent, abdominal gross margin 70 percent) were set before the services unit turned negative, before the customer's platform approval and before the CE-mark amortisation was known, and every broker on file sits below them. Lean: treat the targets as a 2029 to 2030 outcome, which is where my model puts them (29.5 percent EBITDA and 19.5 percent EBIT in 2029; 31.5 and 22.4 in 2030).
  • Qualitative commentary. The accurate half of management's communication: "many" service contracts slower than planned (true for six quarters); kidney constrained until early 2027; European liver moderating; the Q3 margin partly seasonal; the largest customer to decline. Lean: take it at face value for the near year, which I do.

Why the asymmetry exists, so the Consumer can judge whether it persists. The company's revenue is driven by forces it does not control (DCD mix, OPO reform, hospital staffing) that are strengthening, and by launches whose clinical content it controls completely and whose commercial content it controls only partly; its costs are driven by a management that reinvests by conviction. So the top line surprises up when demand inflects and the profit line disappoints when management spends the surprise, and dates slip because regulators and component suppliers, not Xvivo, set them. That pattern holds until the launches are done, which is 2029 on my dating. The one thing that would change the lean on margin is a change in management's stated priority (a permanent CFO who sets a margin floor, or a capital-markets day that bridges the 2027 targets); neither is in prospect.

Weight, stated as a usable rule. For the next 18 months: revenue within plus or minus 5 percent of my figures with the skew to the upside in Q4 2026 (heart conversion, Q4 seasonality) and to the downside in H1 2027 (customer step-down, OPO transition, kidney); EBITDA margin within a cone of 21 to 27 percent for FY2027 around my 24; EPS within 3.0 to 4.8 for FY2027 around my 3.8, with currency the widest contributor. Beyond 18 months, the FDA's handling of the heart PMA splits the cone into two paths that differ by about 150 of FY2028 revenue and about 2.5 of FY2028 EPS, and no forecast should be read without stating which path it assumes; mine assumes the later one.

P-08

Below-operating-profit items and share count: how they shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years

❌ D38

Question asked After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

Judgement. The lines below operating profit are where my model departs most from consensus and from the company's own framing, and they move EPS in opposite directions across the two years that matter most: they flatter FY2026 (a tax rate near zero for nine months and +19 of currency gains on cash lift adjusted EPS to about 3.8 against consensus 3.38) and they depress FY2027 and beyond (a full year of CE-mark amortisation, a tax rate normalising to 20 percent, no currency gain and a modest krona headwind), so that FY2027 adjusted EPS is about 3.8 again, flat on FY2026, against consensus 6.08. Net income follows EPS because the share count is flat. Free cash flow moves the other way: positive from 2027 on my figures and above consensus in every year, because the items that depress EPS (amortisation; deferred-tax unwind) are non-cash and the items that lift cash (working-capital release; lower capitalisation) are not in earnings. Versus the last four to six years, in which EPS swung between 0.8 and 5.7 on tax, currency and write-downs while free cash flow after intangibles was negative or zero, the next four years give a steadier EPS path that compounds from a lower base than consensus (3.8, 3.8, 6.1, 8.7, 12.2) and a free cash flow that turns positive for the first time in the company's growth phase.

Depreciation and amortisation: the line consensus has lightest. History: total depreciation and amortisation 67 (FY2024) and 70 (FY2025), of which 39 was property, plant and equipment (including right-of-use assets), 20 was capitalised development and the balance acquired intangibles. The run-rate rose to 24 in Q3 2026 (nine months about 63) and includes 3.2 of CE-mark amortisation for one month. From Q4 2026 the CE-mark asset adds 9.75 a quarter (39 a year over ten years), the amortisation of other completed development projects runs at about 25 a year and rising, depreciation of the tenfold capacity project and the Swedish facility adds about 5 a year from 2027, and machines placed under operating leases depreciate as PPE. My schedule, excluding amortisation of acquired intangibles (about 11 a year, which adjusted earnings exclude): FY2026 about 85 (total about 94), FY2027 120, FY2028 150 (liver 510(k) asset of roughly 100 amortising from early 2028 at about 10 a year; first months of the heart PMA asset if approved late 2028), FY2029 185 (heart PMA asset of roughly 350 at about 35 a year for a full year), FY2030 205. Consensus EBITDA-to-EBIT gaps are 86, 110, 132 and 162: mine are 10 to 25 higher from 2027, which is the first set's "15 to 30 above" narrowed after seeing the Q3 run-rate. DNB Carnegie models depreciation at 8.3 to 8.8 percent of sales for 2026 to 2028; mine is 8.5, 10.3 and 10.2 percent. Vitrolife (five to 20 years) and Ambu (five to 15 years) amortise capitalised development over similar lives; TransMedics expensed its trials and so carries no comparable charge, which is one reason its EBIT margin understates its cash margin and Xvivo's overstates it.

Tax: from nothing to normal. The Swedish statutory rate is 20.6 percent. The reported rate was 2.9 percent (2023), 13.9 (2024) and 34.7 (2025, on a pre-tax profit of 38.6 depressed by the 66.6 currency loss, with 14.2 of non-deductible items). In the first nine months of 2026, net income of 97 against pre-tax profit of about 95 (EBIT about 80 plus net financial items about +16) implies a tax charge near zero, which DNB Carnegie attributes to "positive tax effects" in Q3; deferred tax assets fell from 32 to 21 in 2025 as Swedish loss carry-forwards were used, so the 2026 benefit most likely reflects United States losses now being recognised against a profitable outlook. I model 5 percent for FY2026 (Q4 at about 20 percent), 20 percent for FY2027, 21 for FY2028, 22 for FY2029 and 23 for FY2030, the rise reflecting United States profits (federal plus state about 25 to 27 percent) as the heart and liver launches move the profit mix. DNB Carnegie models a paid rate of about 15 percent through 2028 inside a 24 percent long-run assumption; RedEye uses 21 percent; consensus implies about 24, 19, 19 and 18 percent, which is too low from 2028 on the profit-mix logic and too high for 2026. The transfer-pricing structure (intercompany revenue 58 percent of the parent's transactions) gives management scope to keep the group rate near 21 percent; the risk of a retrospective adjustment is real but undated and unmodelled. Bonesupport's experience (a 229 tax credit on recognising deferred tax assets in 2023, then a 39 charge in 2024) is the Nordic precedent for the pattern: a one-year gift, then a normal rate.

Net financial items: currency, not interest. Net interest expense was 4.6 in 2025 (revolver at EURIBOR plus 1.25 percent on about 90 drawn; 1.3 of lease interest; a 0.44 percent commitment fee) and is offset by interest income on 300-plus of cash, so the underlying line is within plus or minus 3. Currency on cash: +32.7 (2024), -66.6 (2025), +19.2 (nine months 2026), of which +7.1 in Q3 alone (about 0.22 of the 1.42 reported EPS). I model +12 for FY2026 (the nine-month gain less a Q4 loss on a strengthening krona and interest), -8 for FY2027, -5 for FY2028, -3 for FY2029 and zero for FY2030, with the warning in P-02 that the cone is plus or minus 30 a year. DNB Carnegie models +27, -2 and -5 for 2026 to 2028; RedEye models nothing. The absence of hedging is a policy choice management has defended as unnecessary given United States costs offset United States revenue; it does not offset the revaluation of cash, which is where the volatility sits.

One-offs: small, two-directional, and to be excluded from the base. Inventory write-downs (Q4 2025; about 8 in Q3 2026, which took thoracic gross margin from an underlying 85 to a reported 80 percent); 8.5 of finance-lease recognition in Q3 2026 (a pull-forward, not a one-off in accounting terms, but a timing item that will recur unevenly as machines are placed); FlowHawk integration costs of 2.3 in 2025; the 2023 PrimECC write-down of 17 and 22 of STAR integration. Prospective one-offs with a real probability: impairment of FlowHawk goodwill (57) if cross-selling fails (growth fell to zero in Q3 2026 from 48 percent in Q2), and impairment of services-related goodwill from STAR Teams if the unit does not recover; goodwill and capitalised development are 54 percent of total assets and goodwill is the auditor's key audit matter. I model no impairment; a FlowHawk or services write-down of 50 to 100 is a 2027 to 2028 risk to reported, not adjusted, EPS and to the equity ratio, not to cash.

Share count: flat. 31.5 million shares; incentive plans dilute by at most 1.35 percent in total (SORP 2026 by at most 0.5 percent), so about 0.1 million a year; the 10 percent issuance authority is unused and, on my cash path, stays unused. TransMedics' convertible (460 million dollars, 4.9 million potential shares) and 19 percent equity overhang are the contrast; Xvivo's path has no dilution leg and no buyback leg.

The bridge from EBITDA to EPS, my base case against consensus.

On a "clean" FY2026 (tax at 20 percent, no currency gain), EPS would be about 2.9, so the FY2027 figure of 3.8 is about 30 percent growth on clean earnings and flat on reported; the Consumer should expect the market to read it as flat.

Free cash flow, defined as operating cash flow less capital spending including intangibles less lease payments. History: FactSet free cash flow before intangibles of 3 (2023), 41 (2024) and 7 (2025); after intangibles, negative in every year of the growth phase (net investing of 162, 244 and 258 against operating cash flow of 46, 111 and 101). The turn: operating cash flow of 182 in nine months 2026 (working capital released about 190 as inventory unwound from 242 at Q4 2025 to 54 at Q3 2026) against investing of 171, so free cash flow was about +1 for nine months and total cash flow +21 after currency. My path: FY2026 -21 (a Q4 working-capital rebuild and about 50 of investing), FY2027 +21, FY2028 +123, FY2029 +242, FY2030 +370, against consensus of -99, 0, 103 and 216 on FactSet's definition. Net income to free cash flow conversion goes from negative (2025) to about 17 percent (2027), 64 percent (2028), 87 percent (2029) and 95 percent (2030): the characteristic shape of a company whose capitalised investment ends. The two cash risks: a renewed inventory build for the United States heart launch (I carry working capital rising from 7 to 10 percent of revenue by 2028, about 80 of cash over two years) and channel inventory at the largest customer (the Q3 build of 10 reverses in Q4 2026 or Q1 2027).

P-09

Net likely impact on the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years

C55

Question asked After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

Judgement. Against the last four to six years, the next four give slower but far less volatile revenue growth, a margin that rises for the first time over a full cycle rather than in single quarters, an EPS that compounds from a base that is lower than consensus believes, and a cash flow that turns positive for the first time since the company began its launch programme. The net impact is positive on every line except reported EPS in FY2027, which is flat, and except against consensus, which the model undercuts on EBITDA by about 14 percent and on EPS by about 36 percent in FY2027 and FY2028. The lines that improve most versus history are free cash flow and the EBIT margin from 2028; the line that improves least is EPS over the next 18 months, because 2026's below-the-line gifts (tax, currency) are repaid in 2027 by amortisation and a normal tax charge. The Consumer should read the model as "a better business, a lower earnings base, and a more honest earnings-to-cash relationship than the sell-side's".

Line by line, history against model.

Line FY2021 to FY2025 (history) FY2026 to FY2030 (model) Net impact and why
Revenue 258 to 812; growth +44, +61, +44, +38, -1 percent; a 33 percent compound rate with one -1 percent year and one -15 percent quarter 993, 1,160, 1,475, 1,845, 2,250; growth +22, +17, +27, +25, +22 percent; a 23 percent compound rate Slower, steadier, with a trough in 2027 (customer step-down, hardening comparatives, OPO transition) and re-acceleration on launches from 2028; one-customer swings replaced by regulator-dated steps

The three findings that join the lines up.

  1. The margin expansion is real but late, and the EPS it produces is later still. The leverage of a 72 to 75 percent gross margin against a cost base growing slower than revenue appears at EBITDA from 2027 and at EBIT from 2028; amortisation of about 390 of regulatory assets (CE mark, PMA, 510(k)) over ten years takes 40 to 85 a year of it; tax normalisation takes a further 20 percent. That is why my FY2027 EBITDA is 14 percent below consensus but my FY2027 EPS is 37 percent below: the gap compounds down the account. The Consumer should test any upside scenario against the same compounding: 20 of extra EBITDA is about 0.5 of EPS, not 0.63.
  2. The cash story is better than the earnings story, which reverses the history. From 2021 to 2025 the company reported rising earnings (EPS 0.76 to 5.74) while burning cash on acquisitions and capitalisation; from 2026 to 2030 it reports earnings that disappoint the sell-side while free cash flow goes from about -21 to about +370 and net cash rises more than fivefold from its end-2026 level. This matters for the fourth set: the market's narrative is an EPS narrative, and the first thing that will change is the number it is built on.
  3. The variance in the outcome is concentrated in two items and one date. The largest customer's step-down (I assume -65 in 2027; the range is -40 to -100) and the European heart ramp (I assume 155 in 2027; the range is 120 to 200) together span about 100 of FY2027 revenue and about 1.5 of EPS; the FDA's decision date on the heart PMA spans about 150 of FY2028 revenue and about 2.5 of EPS. Currency adds plus or minus 1.0 of EPS in any year on a 10 percent move. Everything else (abdominal margin, services, administration, tax rate, interest) is worth less than 0.5 of EPS a year each.

Where I differ from the first set, and why. The first set put FY2027 EPS at "roughly 4.5 to 5.5" and FY2028 "20 to 30 percent below consensus"; I am at 3.8 and 36 percent below. The difference is not the operating view (my FY2027 EBITDA margin of 24 percent is inside their "low-to-mid 20s") but three below-the-line items the first set identified and did not fully carry: amortisation at 120 rather than consensus-plus-15, a tax rate of 20 percent rather than 21 to 24 applied to a FY2026 base that is itself inflated by a near-zero rate, and a currency headwind rather than zero. I also place the United States heart launch in early 2029 rather than "a year later than the company's target" (which would be end-2028), the difference being the two quarters between approval and first revenue. On revenue to FY2027 the first set said "near consensus"; I am 4 percent below, because I size the customer step-down at 65 (DNB Carnegie's 5 to 6 percent of 2027 revenue) and the heart ramp two quarters behind the sell-side. Each is a judgement of degree rather than direction, and the first set's direction holds on every line.

P-10

The base case for the key operational drivers and the key financial line items over the next 18 months to four years

C56

Question asked Pulling together all the above answers, in all sets so far, what is your carefully considered base case for the key operational drivers and the key financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement. The base case is a company that grows revenue about 23 percent a year for four years on the structural adoption of machine perfusion, led by lung consumables through hubs and by European heart from 2027, with the United States heart and liver launches adding from 2028 to 2029; whose EBITDA margin rises from 20 to about 30 percent by 2029 because launch costs peak in 2028 while the added revenue carries 80 percent-plus gross margins; whose EBIT margin reaches the company's 20 percent target in 2029 to 2030 after absorbing amortisation of the regulatory assets; whose adjusted EPS is about 3.8 in both FY2026 and FY2027, 6.1 in FY2028, 8.7 in FY2029 and 12.2 in FY2030; and whose free cash flow turns positive in 2027 and reaches about 240 in 2029 without any equity. Against consensus the case is 1 to 8 percent lower on revenue, 3 to 14 percent lower on EBITDA, and 36 percent lower on EPS in FY2027 and FY2028, with the gap closing to about 18 percent on FY2029 EPS if the United States heart is approved by late 2028. The next 18 months are a transition: reported growth decelerates from the inflated Q3 2026 peak to the low-to-mid teens in H1 2027, EPS stalls, and the quality of the number improves; the case is made or lost on the three quarters from Q4 2026 to Q2 2027 for European heart and the lung customer, and on the FDA in the twelve months after that.
