Q1 2028: +22 percent (335); 24.5 percent. The shape is a deceleration into H1 2027 as the customer step-down, the OPO transition and hardening comparatives coincide, then re-acceleration from H2 2027 as European heart compounds, kidney is unconstrained and the step-down is in the base.

What the base case assumes that could be wrong, ranked by effect on FY2027 EPS. (1) The cost base grows 13 percent in 2027: if it grows 17 percent, as in 2026, EBITDA margin is 22 percent and EPS about 3.2. (2) The largest customer falls by 65: at -100, lung is flat and EPS about 3.3; at -40, EPS about 4.1. (3) European heart at 155: at the sell-side's 181, EPS about 4.3; at 120, about 3.3. (4) The dollar at about 9.0 to 9.2 on average in 2027: at 9.6, EPS about 4.3; at 8.5, about 3.1. (5) Tax at 20 percent: at 15 percent (DNB Carnegie's paid rate), EPS about 4.0. For FY2028 the ranking changes: the FDA date dominates (approval in H1 2028 with a launch in H2 adds about 100 of revenue and 1.5 of EPS; a request for more data takes about 50 of revenue and 1.0 of EPS away and prolongs capitalisation).

What would make me change the base case within six months. Up: Q4 2026 heart revenue above 35 with two or more Tier 2 reimbursement decisions; a disclosed hub throughput that implies more than 150 EVLP runs a year across the hubs; a Q-sub outcome that confirms the liver 510(k) without a United States clinical study; an FDA advisory-panel date in H1 2027. Down: Q4 2026 or Q1 2027 lung revenue below 140 excluding timing (the step-down arriving faster); services gross margin still negative in Q1 2027 with no contract conversions; operating costs above 140 a quarter excluding depreciation; a component or clean-room failure in the Q4 2026 transfer; an FDA request for additional heart data.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Lung (the cash engine). United States EVLP disposables grew 183 percent in Q3 2026 and 92 percent year to date against a destocked 2025 base; three OPO hubs are activated against a target of four to five. I model lung revenue excluding the largest customer and excluding the Q3 2026 timing items (8.5 of finance-lease recognition; about 10 of customer inventory build) growing 25 to 30 percent in 2027 and about 20 to 25 percent in 2028, driven by the DCD donor mix, the CMS OPO rule upheld on 28 September 2026 and hub activations. The largest customer (Lung Bioengineering, 132 of 2025 revenue, about 150 in 2026) moves to its own approved platform while continuing to buy STEEN Solution and components: I model its revenue at about 85 in 2027 and about 80 thereafter, a step-down of about 65 (DNB Carnegie's 5 to 6 percent of 2027 revenue). Net lung growth: +22 percent in 2026, +8 percent in 2027, +18 percent in 2028, +14 percent in 2029, +13 percent in 2030.
  • Heart. CE mark 11 August 2026; French national reimbursement 18 May 2026; derogation sales of 22 to 24 a quarter in Q2 and Q3 2026 convert to commercial pricing from Q4 2026; Tier 2 markets (the Netherlands, Austria, the United Kingdom, Belgium) obtain reimbursement during 2027; about 25 commercial centres by end-2027 and about 40 by end-2028. United States: PMA received by the FDA on 28 September 2026; I assume approval in late 2028 and launch in Q1 2029, one year after the company's "targeted end-2027", with Continued Access Protocol sales only before then. Heart revenue: 90, 155, 275, 420 (United States about 90), 580 (United States about 190).
  • Liver and kidney. Liver: European growth moderating to about 12 percent a year; a 510(k) submission in H1 2027 using Bridge to Life's VitaSmart as predicate and a United States launch in early 2028 (United States liver about 10, 40 and 80 in 2028 to 2030). Kidney: constrained by a core-component replacement until early 2027, then about 30 percent a year with the broader United States launch in 2027. Abdominal gross margin recovers from 59 percent (2026) to 63, 66, 68 and 69 percent on the Netherlands-to-Sweden manufacturing consolidation and the tenfold capacity project completing in Q4 2026, with savings lagging one to 1.5 years; real abdominal price assumed -1 to -2 percent a year.
  • Services. Flat at about 62 in 2027 at zero gross margin (nine months 2026 +4 percent; Q3 -19 percent) as OPO and NRP contracts convert slowly; growing with the United States heart launch from 2028 at 8 percent, then 15 and 20 percent gross margin.
  • Operating costs. About 496 excluding depreciation in 2026 (Q3 run-rate about 125 a quarter), growing 13 percent in 2027 (10 to 13 United States commercial hires; launch preparation; wage inflation), about 22 percent in 2028 and 2029 (United States heart, liver and kidney launches and the services roster) and 18 percent in 2030. Operating costs fall from 50 percent of revenue in 2026 to 44 percent in 2030.
  • Depreciation and amortisation. Q3 2026 run-rate about 24 a quarter including one month (3.2) of CE-mark amortisation; the CE-mark asset adds 39 a year over ten years from August 2026; other capitalised development amortises at about 25 a year rising; the capacity project and Swedish facility add about 5 a year from 2027; the liver 510(k) asset (about 100) amortises from early 2028 at about 10 a year; the heart PMA asset (about 350) from late 2028 at about 35 a year. Acquired-intangible amortisation (about 11 a year, FlowHawk and earlier acquisitions) is excluded from the input as adjusted earnings exclude it.
  • Net financial items. Net interest within plus or minus 3 (revolver EUR 8 million at EURIBOR plus 1.25 percent, offset by interest on 300-plus of cash); currency revaluation of foreign-currency cash is unhedged and was +19.2 in nine months 2026. FY2026 +12 (nine-month gain less a Q4 loss on a stronger krona); thereafter a small drag on the consensus macro path (USD/SEK 9.15 by end-2026, 8.67 by late 2027 per Intesa Sanpaolo). Cone plus or minus 30 a year.
  • Tax. Nine-month 2026 tax charge near zero (positive tax effects; United States loss recognition), so 5 percent for FY2026 with Q4 at about 20 percent; Swedish statutory rate 20.6 percent; rising to 23 percent by 2030 as United States profits grow.
  • Cash flow. Working capital released about 190 in nine months 2026 (net working capital from 242 at Q4 2025 to 54 at Q3 2026, 5.6 percent of trailing revenue); I rebuild it to 7 percent at end-2026 and 10 percent by 2028 for the United States heart launch inventory. Capital spending: nine months 2026 171 (138 intangibles; 31 property, plant and equipment); FY2026 about 221; falling as the capacity project completes and capitalised development falls from about 175 a year to about 100 by 2028. Lease payments about 15 a year (lease liabilities 41 at end-2025). No dividend, buyback or acquisition; incentive-plan dilution at most 1.35 percent across all plans, so about 0.1 million shares a year. The EUR 20 million revolver matures in January 2028 and is repaid from cash.

The company's guidance, and how your inputs compare with it

The company gives no numerical annual guidance. Its targets, reaffirmed in July 2026 and not restated at Q3: an EBITDA margin above 30 percent and an EBIT margin above 20 percent by 2027, and a 70 percent abdominal gross margin by 2027. Its only quantitative steer in 2026 was that FY2026 EBITDA would land in line with consensus (July 2026). Its milestones: a United States heart launch "targeted" for the end of 2027 subject to FDA review; a liver 510(k) submission in H1 2027 for an early-2028 launch; kidney growth resuming in early 2027; four to five OPO hubs; tenfold disposable capacity by Q4 2026; CE-mark amortisation of 39 a year.

My inputs against that: EBITDA margin 24 percent in 2027 (target above 30; reached in 2029 to 2030 on my path); EBIT margin 13.6 percent in 2027 (target above 20; reached in 2029 to 2030); abdominal gross margin 63 percent in 2027 (target 70; 69 in 2030); United States heart launch in Q1 2029 against the company's end-2027 (a two-to-four-quarter lag on commercial dates, as the record of slipped dates argues); liver 510(k) and kidney dates taken as the company states them. FY2026 EBITDA of 199 is 3 percent below the pre-results consensus of 206 that management pointed to in July, because the Q3 beat was about one-third timing and Q4 carries launch hiring.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  • Revenue: identical (company reported revenue equals FactSet revenue and the brokers' basis; FY2025 812 on all three).
  • Gross margin: the company reports 74 percent for FY2025 and 70 percent for Q3 2026; FactSet's standardised gross margin is about 8 points lower (65.7 percent FY2025; 61.7 percent Q3 2026) because it reclassifies some costs into cost of goods. My inputs and Part 4 use the company's basis; the only consensus gross-profit line (713 for FY2026, one broker) is on the company's basis.
  • EBITDA: the company reports adjusted EBITDA (excluding items affecting comparability, for example 2.6 of FlowHawk integration costs in 2025); FactSet's operating EBITDA was 163 for FY2025 and the brokers' adjusted basis 161 (19.8 percent). My FY2026 EBITDA of 199 is on the company's adjusted basis, which matches the brokers' basis to within about 1 percent.
  • EBIT and depreciation: consensus EBIT is the brokers' adjusted EBIT; FactSet's EBITDA-to-EBIT gap (86, 110, 132, 162 for FY2026 to FY2029) includes all depreciation and amortisation. My depreciation input excludes acquired-intangible amortisation of about 11 a year, so my total gap is 96, 131, 161, 196 and 216 against the consensus gap; on a like-for-like total basis I am 10 to 25 above consensus from 2027.
  • EPS: consensus is the brokers' adjusted EPS (FY2025 actual 1.03 against reported 0.80, the difference mainly the currency item and acquired-intangible amortisation); my cross-check EPS is adjusted EPS on the same basis (excluding acquired-intangible amortisation, including currency in financial items) over 31.5 million shares rising 0.1 million a year. Reported EPS would be about 0.28 a year lower from the acquired amortisation.
  • Free cash flow: FactSet's consensus free cash flow is operating cash flow less capital expenditure (-99, 0, 103, 216 for FY2026 to FY2029); mine deducts capital spending including intangibles and lease payments. On FactSet's definition (before leases) my figures would be -7, +36, +139, +259 and +388.
  • Net cash: FactSet includes leases in debt (debt 121; net cash 193 at Q3 2026); excluding leases net cash is about 230.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 3 and Layer 4

Research requests for the next set

Company: Xvivo Perfusion AB (XVIVO-SE), a Gothenburg-listed organ-preservation and machine-perfusion company selling proprietary preservation fluids (Perfadex Plus, STEEN Solution), perfusion machines (XPS for lung, Heart Assist Transport, Liver Assist, Kidney Assist Transport) and single-use kits, plus United States organ-recovery, normothermic regional perfusion and FlowHawk workflow-software services; reported in three segments, Thoracic (lung and heart; about 70 percent of Q3 2026 sales), Abdominal (liver and kidney; about 25 percent) and Services (about 5 percent)

Request: T-D1

Peers: TransMedics Group (TMDX-US), the only direct listed machine-perfusion peer, which launched OCS Heart and OCS Liver in the United States through its National OCS Program and whose EBITDA and operating margin path through those launches is the closest precedent for a perfusion company scaling a heart product against an incumbent; Layer 3 and Layer 4

Layer 3: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months
and Layer 4: Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

10 questions in 4 groups · 16,520 words · written 24 October 2026, 10:21 to 10:44 UK, Fable, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
Xvivo (XVIVO-SE): third and fourth sets of questions, the predictability of the forecasts and the gap to guidance, consensus and the market narrative

Written 24 October 2026, two days after the Q3 2026 report. All money is SEK millions unless stated; margins are on the company's own presentation unless marked FactSet. "Consensus" means the FactSet median of 22 October 2026 (latest estimate date 6 October 2026, so it predates the Q3 report). "Post-Q3" means the two reviews published on 23 October 2026 by DNB Carnegie and RedEye, the only post-results notes on file; Pareto and Stifel have not been seen since July and 6 October respectively. "Our model" is the Financial Analyst's model after update 1 and "our EPS" is the adjusted earnings per share it calculates (3.47 / 3.82 / 6.07 / 8.75 / 12.18 for FY2026 to FY2030 before the input changes I make below; about 3.75 for FY2026 after them). Where I disagree with the first or second set I say so in the answer concerned.

Orientation for the Consumer, in brief

  • The forecasts are congruent in direction across all three sets and their centre of gravity has not moved: a 20 to 23 percent revenue grower whose EBITDA margin rises slowly, whose earnings per share stall in FY2027 and whose free cash flow turns positive in 2027. Two factual premises in the second set are wrong (a near-zero nine-month tax rate; a 190 working-capital release) and I correct the three inputs they fed. The corrections leave FY2026 EPS at about 3.75 (the cross-check's 3.81 was right by the accident of two offsetting errors) and lower the free cash flow path by 10 to 40 a year without changing its sign.
  • Predictability is moderate at the revenue line (FY2027 within about plus or minus 6 percent of 1,162), low at the EBITDA line (21 to 27 percent margin around 24) and poor at the earnings line (FY2027 EPS 2.1 to 5.6 around 3.8), and the share price trades on the earnings line and on binary regulatory news. The drivers are concentrated: three for 2027 (European heart, lung net of the largest customer, operating-cost growth) and one regulator's date for 2028.
  • The gap to the market is unfavourable for the next six to twelve months: the company's 2027 targets will be missed by a wide margin and are likely to be re-dated at or soon after the Q4 2026 report on 28 January 2027; consensus FY2027 EPS (about 5.8 post-Q3 on the FactSet mean) is about 50 percent above our 3.8 and will be cut in steps through 2027; consensus FY2028 (9.47, being raised toward 10.5 as RedEye shifts United States heart revenue into 2028) rests on an FDA date we place a year later. The sell-side knows every one of our dynamics in words; it has not carried the below-EBITDA ones into its numbers, and retail investors have not carried any of them.
  • The one gap that runs in the case's favour is cash: the two brokers who model it show free cash flow of about -97 (DNB Carnegie) and near zero for 2026 to 2027, whereas on our corrected inputs it is about -10 and +15, with the balance sheet self-funding every launch. A second, smaller, favourable gap is that nobody on the sell-side has sized a faster FDA decision as upside to a 2028 base that we set conservatively.
  • The most important single finding for the memo's valuation topics: the dashboard's valuation rating of A and the "1st percentile of ten years" reading rest on consensus EPS. On our EPS the shares trade at about 40 times 24-month-forward earnings (the model's own 39.9x), which is the middle of the stock's five-year range (median 44.8x; 25th percentile 33.8x), not its floor. The case is therefore a multiple-and-delivery case, not a "cheap on earnings" case, and the fair trough is lower than the first set computed.

The Financial Analyst's cross-check gaps, explained, and the inputs I change

The one-page summary flags three gaps between the second set's own cross-check and the model's figures. Each has a specific cause; two expose wrong inputs, one is arithmetic.

Gap 1: FY2026 earnings per share, the Analyst's 3.81 against the model's 3.47. Both start from the same pre-tax profit of 125.7 (EBITA 113.7 plus net financial items of 12). The model's net income of 109.3 implies a tax charge of 16.4, an effective rate of 13 percent; the cross-check applied the 5 percent input to the whole year (tax of about 6). The model is the nearer to the truth, because the second set's premise that "the nine-month tax charge was near zero" is wrong: the Q3 report shows a nine-month tax charge of 17.2 on a pre-tax profit of 114.4, a 15 percent rate (quarterly charges of 6.5, 16.5 and a credit of 5.8 in Q3). The Analyst generalised the Q3 credit, and computed pre-tax profit as about 95 (EBIT 80 plus financial items 16) when the reported figure was 114. That second error is the other half of the story: nine-month net financial items were about +34 on the company's adjusted EBIT (currency gains on cash, interest income and other financial income; DNB Carnegie reports Q3 alone at +11), not the +16 the second set assumed, so the +12 full-year input is too low by about 15. The two errors offset in the cross-check, which is why 3.81 is close to the right answer for the wrong reasons. I change two inputs: net financial items for FY2026 from +12 to +27 (nine-month actual of about +34 less a Q4 drag of about 7 from a strengthening krona and net interest; DNB Carnegie's post-Q3 figure is +27), and the FY2026 tax rate from 5 to 16 percent (nine months at 15 percent; Q4 at about 20 percent on a pre-tax profit of about 26). Pre-tax profit becomes 140.7, net income 118.2 and EPS about 3.75, against the pre-results consensus of 3.38 and DNB Carnegie's post-Q3 3.90 at a 15.1 percent rate. The 2027 to 2030 tax inputs (20 to 23 percent) and financial-item inputs (-8, -5, -3, 0) I leave unchanged: the deferred tax assets are nearly used (12.7 of loss carry-forwards at end-2025 from 24.9), the Swedish rate is 20.6 percent and United States losses stay unrecognised until the United States turns profitable, which on our dating is 2029.

Gap 2: FY2026 free cash flow, the Analyst's -21 against the model's 131.7. The gap of 153 is almost exactly a working-capital release the model computes and the Analyst did not: the model starts from FactSet's end-2025 net working capital of 242 (29.8 percent of revenue) and moves to the 7 percent input (69.5 on FY2026 revenue), a release of 172.5; its operating cash flow is 198.7 plus 12 less 16.4 plus 172.5, or 366.8, and 366.8 less 221 of capital spending and 14 of leases is 131.7. The Analyst's -21 instead builds from the company's reported nine-month operating cash flow of 182 plus a Q4 estimate of about 32. The company's figure is the one to trust, and it shows the second set's "190 of working capital released in nine months" is an artefact of FactSet's quarterly working-capital series, not a cash event: nine-month EBITDA was about 142 and operating cash flow 182, so the whole of working capital, cash tax and other items contributed about +40, not +190 (the Q3 report itself cites an inventory reduction of only 21.6). The FactSet series is unreliable throughout (it shows negative net working capital of -38 and -18 in H1 2024, which is impossible for a company carrying 20 percent-plus of sales in sterile inventory, then 178 at end-2024 and 242 at end-2025). The economic reality is a working-capital-heavy business (receivables about 13 percent of sales, inventories 20 to 25 percent, payables about 8 to 10 percent, so about 20 to 30 percent net), not the 5.6 percent the first and second sets read off the series. I disagree with the first set's C-52 ("working capital has been fixed and is now a modest source of cash") and with the second set's P-02 and P-08 on the mechanism; I agree with their conclusion (self-funded; no equity raise) because it rests on EBITDA, not on working capital. I change the working-capital input to 22 percent of revenue at end-2026 (a release of about 23 from FactSet's 242 base, consistent with the real nine-month release of about 40 less a Q4 rebuild for seasonally strong sales and heart launch stock), then 21, 20, 20 and 19 percent (modest efficiency as the Swedish consolidation and tenfold capacity project settle, offset by United States heart inventory from 2028). On these inputs the model's free cash flow becomes about -8 in FY2026, +15 in FY2027, +110 in FY2028, +202 in FY2029 and +341 in FY2030, against the second set's -21, +21, +123, +242 and +370. The sign and the story survive; the level is 10 to 40 a year lower.

Gap 3: FY2027 free cash flow, the Analyst's 21 against the model's 5.7. This is arithmetic, not an input. On the second set's inputs the model's FY2027 operating cash flow is 206 (EBITDA 279, financial items -8, cash tax 30, working-capital build 35) and the Analyst's cross-check wrote 221; the 15 difference is the cross-check treating part of the 2027 tax charge as non-cash. The model is right to pay it in full, since the Swedish loss carry-forwards are nearly exhausted and the United States losses are not recognised. No input changes for this reason; on my revised working-capital inputs the FY2027 figure is about +15 (a build of 26 rather than 35).

A model-basis point that is not an input. The summary shows year-end net cash of -24.2 for FY2026 and -18.5 for FY2027. The company reported net cash of 193 at 30 September 2026 on FactSet's definition (313 of cash; 121 of debt including leases) and about 230 excluding leases; with free cash flow near zero in Q4, the end-2026 figure should be about 180 to 200. The gap of about 200 is in the model's opening balance or its debt definition, not in any forecast input, and the Financial Analyst should check it before the memo's return calculation uses net cash. The forecast inputs I change are set out in inputs-changed.md.

Layer 3: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months

Risks

❌ D427 questions
D-0155D-0252D-0335D-0430D-0558D-0645D-0742

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The predictability of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months is moderate for the business and low for the security. The sets are congruent in direction and their forecast's shape (revenue growth near 17 percent in 2027 re-accelerating to 27 percent in 2028; an EBITDA margin rising from 20 to 24 percent; earnings per share flat at about 3.8; free cash flow turning positive) has survived five independent lines of analysis and the correction of two misread inputs, which gives it robustness; but the level of earnings cannot be stated to better than plus or minus a third, because three concentrated operating drivers (European heart, lung net of a customer whose transition is undisclosed, and management's discretionary cost build) and one currency carry almost the whole of the FY2027 variance, and because the company discloses nothing below the gross-profit line that would let a reader convert those drivers to earnings. The 2027 forecasts rest about 70 percent on strengths the business has demonstrated (lung consumables, clinical evidence, the European trial network, cost discipline when chosen) and 30 percent on strengths it must still acquire (Tier 2 reimbursement, a clean manufacturing transfer, a services turnaround); from 2028 those proportions reverse and one regulator's date sets the slope, with our base at the conservative end of its distribution. The company's disclosure lets the operating thesis be tested each quarter against about a dozen numbers, which is a real help, while its silence on guidance, amortisation, tax and currency leaves the earnings thesis to the reader and has already produced a 60 percent spread between the two brokers who have updated. The share price moves on company news alone (91 percent idiosyncratic in the latest six months), which means the binary calendar, not the sector, will set the path, and the asymmetry of past reactions (minus 30 percent on a miss; plus 15 on a beat) argues for sizing the position for a drawdown of a third. For the memo's riskiness topic: a simple case in words (perfusion adoption plus two launches) that is not simple in numbers; fragilities that are known, dated and concentrated; risks that are trackable but sudden; and a security whose price already discounts more than the brokers' numbers do but less than ours.

D-01

Congruence of the answers across the earlier sets

C55

Question asked How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

Judgement. The three sets are congruent in direction on every line that matters and the congruence is of the useful kind: each set reached the same shape (revenue growth of 20 percent-plus that is easier to believe than margin expansion; margins two to four points below consensus; dates two to four quarters late; earnings per share depressed below EBITDA by amortisation, tax and currency; a self-funded balance sheet) by different routes (external forces, industry structure, management record, the operating model, and now the AlphaSense precedents), which is what makes a forecast robust rather than merely consistent. The incongruities are of three kinds, and I rank them by what they do to predictability. First, two factual premises in the second set are wrong (a near-zero nine-month tax rate; a 190 working-capital release) and I have corrected the inputs above; they offset in earnings and change the cash level, not its sign, so they lower confidence in the modelling base without changing the forecast's shape. Second, the dashboard's valuation reading (rating A; P/E at the 1st percentile of ten years) is built on consensus earnings that every set judges 35 to 40 percent too high for FY2027 and FY2028; on our earnings the stock sits at about 40 times 24-month-forward EPS, the middle of its five-year range, so the "cheap" premise that frames the dashboard is incongruent with our own forecasts and the memo must choose which it believes. Third, a handful of smaller jars (the Q4 2026 heart figure, the nine-month heart total, the FDA date's position in its distribution, the 2027 capitalisation level) are judgements of degree where I state my own view. None of the incongruities reverses a judgement; all of them widen the cone. Net: congruent enough to trust the direction, not congruent enough to trust the earnings level to better than about a third.

Where the sets support each other, and why that matters.

  1. Revenue. The first set's external forces (DCD donor mix, the OPO rule upheld on 28 September 2026, hospital capacity outsourcing, competitor growth of 37 to 65 percent) and the second set's reinforcing loops (hub-and-spoke; the heart clinical network) point to the same lung engine growing 25 to 30 percent excluding the largest customer and the same European heart build to about 155 in 2027. The AlphaSense reports confirm the mechanism from the other side: OneLegacy's July 2026 hub opened to over 70 centres; Australia at 40 to 50 percent of brain-death heart transplants; France reimbursed since 18 May 2026; RedEye and DNB Carnegie both expect European heart to carry 2027. Three independent routes to one number is good congruence.
  2. Margins. The first set said the forces "do not by themselves deliver the margin targets"; the second set modelled 24 percent for 2027; the T-D1 precedents say a ten-point EBITDA step in one to two years during a launch has one clean precedent (Bonesupport, a single 92 percent gross-margin product with a payment decision) and several counter-examples (Ambu took two to three years for ten points and is still short of its 20 percent; TransMedics approaches 30 percent only in 2028 after a decade). The brokers who updated after Q3 model 23.4 percent (RedEye) and 25.0 percent (DNB Carnegie) for 2027. Our 24 percent is bracketed by the two most informed outside views.
  3. Dates. Every set applied a lag to commercial dates; the own-3 report supplies the base rates (a 92 percent major-deficiency rate on the 2026 PMA cohort; a panel referral extends a review to about 320 days; TransMedics' OCS Heart took nearly three years; Shockwave's breakthrough PMA took six months). Congruent, with the caveat on skew below.
  4. Below EBITDA. The first set identified the three items (amortisation, tax, currency), the second set carried them, and the own-6 report confirms the facts (CE-mark amortisation 39 a year, raised from the 25 management estimated in July; five-to-ten-year lives; deferred tax assets of 21 at end-2025; DNB Carnegie's development amortisation of only 38 for 2027 against 39 for the CE-mark asset alone).
  5. Management and risk. "Careful, honest, scientifically excellent, commercially slow" (first set) matches the second set's lean ("revenue near consensus, margins below, dates late") and the T-D1 report's "clinical delivery exceptional, commercial timelines slipped, services six quarters down".
  6. Balance sheet. Self-funded, no raise, strategic floor from 11 to 20 times sales paid for peers: all three sets agree, and my cash correction does not disturb it (net cash still rises from about 190 to about 800 by 2030 on the revised inputs, before any model opening-balance correction).

Where they jar, and what I conclude.

  • Working capital and the cash narrative. The first set's C-52 and the second set's P-02 and P-08 read a 190 release off FactSet's quarterly net working capital series; the company's own cash flow (182 of operating cash flow on 142 of EBITDA) shows the real release was about 40. I disagree with both sets on the mechanism and have changed the input. The consequence for the memo: the cash story is "EBITDA converts to cash because cash tax is low and capitalisation is falling", not "working capital has been fixed"; and a Q4 2026 or 2027 inventory build for the heart launch is a cash risk the second set under-weighted because it believed inventory had already been run down.
  • Tax. The second set's "near zero for nine months" is wrong (15 percent); corrected above. The direction of its argument (2026 flattered, 2027 normal) still holds, but the 2026 flattering came from financial income, not tax.
  • Valuation framing. The first set's C-83 computed a fair trough from a 24-month-forward EPS of about 6.4 on "the memo's own path"; the second set's path gives about 5.6 (the model's 39.9x on 223.4). At 20 to 25 times, the trough is 110 to 140, not 128 to 160; and at today's price the stock is at about 40 times our earnings, not 27 times. I disagree with any reading of the stock as being at its valuation floor; it is at the floor of consensus earnings, which we think are wrong. This is the most consequential incongruity for topics #10 to #12.
  • The Q4 2026 heart figure. The second set's FY2026 heart revenue of 90 implies about 35 in Q4 against 22 in Q3 (nine months about 55 on the company's quarterly disclosure of 9, 24 and 22; RedEye's post-Q3 note cites 65.1 for nine months, a discrepancy I cannot resolve from the files and flag). RedEye also says the first CE-marked commercial sales are expected in December 2026, so Q4 is mostly derogation and compassionate-use volume plus a few commercial invoices. I put Q4 heart at 25 to 30, not 35; immaterial to the group revenue figure (about 5 to 10 of 260) but material to the narrative, because the first test of the commercial ramp moves from the Q4 report to the Q1 2027 report in April.
  • The FDA date. The second set placed approval in late 2028 and launch in Q1 2029 by applying the two-to-four-quarter lag rule to the company's end-2027 target. On the own-3 report's evidence I would put the distribution as: approval by end-2027 about 15 percent; H1 2028 about 35 percent; H2 2028 about 30 percent; 2029 or a request for further data about 20 percent. Our base sits at about the 60th to 65th percentile, which is slightly conservative; I keep it because the asymmetry of the share-price reaction argues for a base case the stock can beat, but the Consumer should know that this one item is skewed up relative to our base while the others (customer step-down, margin, currency) are skewed down.
  • 2027 capitalisation. The second set assumes capitalised development falls from about 175 to about 130 in 2027 because the PRESERVE trial is complete and the DeLIVER trial is paused; DNB Carnegie models 204 of intangible capital spending in 2027, rising. If DNB Carnegie is right, our 2027 free cash flow is about -60 rather than +15 and our EBITDA margin is unaffected (the spend is capitalised either way). I keep ours because the two largest trial cost centres have ended and the CAP study carries CMS cost recovery, but this is the widest divergence in the cash cone.
  • Operating costs. The first set's two statements (costs growing at two-thirds of revenue; costs growing at least in line with revenue through 2027) were reconciled by the second set as 2024 versus 2026 behaviour. The 13 percent cost growth modelled for 2027 is, however, below 2026's 14 percent in a year management says will carry 10 to 13 United States commercial hires, launch preparation and a doubled surgical roster (own-4). RedEye expects elevated selling and administrative spend for 24 months. I keep 13 percent as the centre but treat the risk as one-sided: 15 to 17 percent is as likely as 11.

What the congruence means for predictability. The forecasts' direction is robust because it has survived five independent lines of attack; their level is not, because two inputs were built on misread data and a third (the dashboard's valuation) frames the stock as cheaper than our own numbers make it. A Consumer working from these sets can be confident the sell-side will cut FY2027 and that the business will self-fund; they should not be confident of FY2027 EPS to better than plus or minus a third.

D-02

Plausibility of the expected change on demonstrated strengths

C52

Question asked To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

Judgement. The expected change splits cleanly by horizon into the part that rests on strengths the business has demonstrated and the part that rests on strengths it would need to acquire, and the dividing line falls almost exactly at the end of 2027. For the next 18 months (to FY2027) about 70 percent of the forecast change rests on demonstrated strengths: lung consumables growing with the installed base and the hubs (a franchise with about 90 percent share of static lung preservation, patent cover to 2035, thoracic disposable gross margins held at 84 to 85 percent through inflation, and United States EVLP disposables up 92 percent year to date); the European heart build through a clinical network the company already runs (15 trial sites, about 25 percent of European heart centres; eight centres using the device under derogation; Australia at 40 to 50 percent penetration under special access; France reimbursed); and cost discipline the company has shown it can switch on (the H2 2025 programme produced a 25 percent EBITDA quarter in Q4 2025). The remaining 30 percent of the 2027 change rests on things not yet shown: a Tier 2 reimbursement negotiation won in at least two of the Netherlands, Austria, Belgium and the United Kingdom; an abdominal gross margin that rises after a manufacturing transfer (the Nordic precedents say transfers dilute margin by about 1.5 points and take two to three years to reach cost parity); and a services unit that stops losing money (six quarters of decline; -19 percent gross margin; a roster that needs about 2.5 times its Q3 utilisation to return to its 2024 margin). For 2028 to 2030 the proportions reverse: the forecasts' re-acceleration (27 and 25 percent growth) requires a United States heart launch against TransMedics' 26 percent share of all United States heart, lung and liver transplants and its 22 aircraft, a United States liver launch on a regulatory route the FDA has not yet confirmed, and a services and logistics capability that the company has so far failed to build; none of those is a demonstrated strength, and the own-3 and own-4 reports give the base rates (OCS Heart needed eight quarters after approval to reach USD 19 million a quarter; the Oklahoma hub failed on staffing and volume). The thesis as the second set modelled it is plausible because it already leans on the demonstrated part and defers the acquired part: it dates the United States heart launch to early 2029, holds services at zero margin through 2027 and reaches the company's "2027" margins in 2029 to 2030. The sell-side's thesis is less plausible because it asks the acquired strengths to deliver in 2027 (a 26.8 percent margin; heart at 181) and 2028 (RedEye's heart revenue above 550, most of it United States).

The demonstrated strengths, tested against what the change needs from them.

  • Lung franchise (needs: +25 to 30 percent ex the largest customer in 2027). Demonstrated. The franchise grew 44 and 38 percent in 2023 and 2024 before one customer's destocking, and recovered to +183 percent in United States EVLP disposables in Q3 2026 with three hubs activated and five in the pipeline. The weakness is not strength but concentration: the step-down at Lung Bioengineering (132 of 2025 revenue) is a known loss of a demonstrated strength, and the own-1 report shows the retained part (STEEN Solution and Perfadex Plus, which the customer must buy because generic alternatives are not FDA-approved for clinical use) is the highest-margin part. Plausibility of the 2027 lung line (625, +8 percent): high on strengths; the risk is in the customer's drawdown speed (a 10 inventory build in Q3 that reverses in Q4 or Q1).
  • Clinical evidence and the European network (needs: about 25 commercial heart centres by end-2027 and 155 of revenue). Demonstrated in the trial sites and in Australia and France; not yet demonstrated as a commercial sales machine outside France. The own-2 report's peer analogues are sobering on speed (TransMedics' ex-United States revenue was still only USD 2.6 million a quarter in late 2024 "due to a lack of broad national reimbursement"; DNB Carnegie's rule that uptake stays limited for about a year after launch) and encouraging on ceiling (Australia; an Emory centre moving from 50 to 90 percent perfusion once price fell). The RedEye statement that first CE-marked sales come in December 2026 dates the commercial clock from Q1 2027, which is why 155 (quarterly 30, 33, 42, 50) rests on the demonstrated network for its first half and on reimbursement negotiations for its second.
  • Cost discipline (needs: operating costs growing about 13 percent against revenue of 17 percent). Demonstrated in single quarters when management chose it (Q4 2025); not demonstrated through a launch year (2026 costs grew 14 percent and absorbed the growth). Plausible but discretionary; the own-4 report's Q3 opex of 150 against forecasts of 147 says the discretion currently runs toward spending.
  • Supply chain and manufacturing (needs: abdominal gross margin from 59 to 63 percent in 2027). Not demonstrated. The company has outsourced manufacturing to a small network, receives no volume discounts and says savings take one to 1.5 years; the own-5 report's precedents (Ambu's Mexico transfer, 1.5 points of dilution and about three years to cost parity; Vitrolife's 0.7-point under-absorption; Trinity Biotech's USD 3.3 million of stranded cost) argue the 2027 recovery is modest and 2028 is where it shows. Our 63 percent for 2027 is plausible on mix normalisation alone (Q3 was already 61 percent); the 70 percent target is not plausible before 2029 to 2030.
  • Services and logistics (needs: zero gross margin in 2027, positive from 2028). Not demonstrated; the opposite has been demonstrated. The own-4 report's utilisation arithmetic (2.5 times Q3 volume to reach the 2024 margin) and the former director's account of surgeons lost and a late entry to NRP say that even "zero in 2027" needs contract conversions that have been "taking longer than originally anticipated" for six quarters. This is the leg of the 2027 model that most depends on an acquired capability, and it is small (worth about 1 point of group margin either way).
  • United States commercial execution (needs: nothing in 2027; everything from 2029). Not demonstrated. The first set's management judgement (out-executed by TransMedics for a decade) and the T-D1 report's "we judge that attempting a simultaneous multi-organ launch exceeds Xvivo's current capabilities" are the honest reading. The second set's model defers the test to 2029 and asks for about 90 of United States heart revenue in that year, which is less than TransMedics' OCS Heart did in its first full year; plausible as a floor, not demonstrated.

The precedent base rate. Across the T-D1 and T-D2 reports, the companies that lifted EBITDA margins by ten points in one to two years did so with one product, one payment event and a 90 percent-plus gross margin (Bonesupport, 34 percent adjusted EBIT margin in Q3 2026), or after a multi-year factory transfer (Ambu, 2.7 to 13 percent in two years, then stalled); the companies that set serial targets and missed them were de-rated and restructured (Vitrolife, 9.7 billion of impairments). Xvivo's 2027 targets belong to the first pattern in ambition and to the Ambu pattern in mechanics (a transfer, a launch, a services node), so the plausible path is Ambu's: margin expansion of three to four points a year, not ten in one.

What the answer means for the model. The 2027 forecasts are plausible on demonstrated strengths with two acquired-strength dependencies (Tier 2 reimbursement; a stable abdominal transfer) that each carry about half a point of margin. The 2028 to 2030 forecasts are plausible only if one assumes the company acquires United States commercial capability at about the pace TransMedics did, and our model already discounts that by a year; a Consumer who believes the first set's management judgement in full should run a scenario in which United States heart revenue in 2029 is 40 to 50 rather than 90.

D-03

Concentration or diffusion of the drivers that separate the forecasts from the last four to six years

❌ D35

Question asked In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

Judgement. The drivers are concentrated, and concentrated in a way that changes shape across the horizon: for the next 18 months three drivers and one currency carry almost all of the difference between the forecast and the past; for 2028 and beyond one regulator's date carries most of it; and one segment (Thoracic, 70 percent of sales) houses the drivers that matter at every horizon. This is more concentrated than the history it replaces, not less, because the history's variance came from one customer and one component shortage, and the forecast's variance comes from one customer, one approval and one launch. The practical consequence for the Consumer is that the forecast can be monitored with about six numbers a quarter, and that the FY2027 and FY2028 consensus cannot be met if any one of its three load-bearing assumptions (European heart at 181 in 2027; lung at 647 absorbing the largest customer's decline; a six-point margin step) fails, because none of the other drivers is large enough to compensate.

What separates the forecast from the history, decomposed. The second set's model takes revenue from 812 (FY2025) to 2,250 (FY2030), an increase of about 1,440, and the EBITDA margin from 20 to 31.5 percent. Of the revenue increase: heart contributes about 545 (38 percent; from about 35 to 580, of which the United States about 190), lung about 465 (32 percent; from about 474 to 940, net of a step-down of about 65 at the largest customer), abdominal about 405 (28 percent; of which United States liver 80), services about 22. The last four to six years' growth (258 to 812, 2021 to 2025) was lung consumables about 60 percent, abdominal (acquired in 2020) about 30 percent, services about 10 percent, and heart nil. So the forecast replaces a one-engine history with a two-engine future and asks the new engine, heart, to supply the largest share of the change while its United States half waits on a regulator. Of the 11.5 points of EBITDA margin expansion: operating leverage on a cost base growing slower than revenue about 6 points; heart mix at thoracic-grade gross margin about 2.5 points; abdominal unit cost about 2 points; services from -19 percent to +20 percent gross margin about 1.5 points; administration scaling about 0.5; price nil. Operating leverage is itself a function of the revenue drivers, so the margin leg is not an independent source of diversification.

The 18-month view (FY2027), driver by driver, with the EPS it carries. FY2027 revenue growth of about 169 (17 percent) decomposes into: European heart +65 (155 against 90), lung +45 net (new accounts and hubs +110, the largest customer -65), abdominal +55 (kidney unconstrained from early 2027; European liver +12 percent), services +2. The margin step from 20 to 24 percent is two-thirds operating leverage (costs +13 percent against revenue +17 percent) and one-third the abdominal recovery and the absence of write-downs. The sensitivities the second set ranked, which I accept: the cost base's growth rate (13 against 17 percent: about 0.6 of EPS); the largest customer (-40 to -100 of revenue: about 0.4 of EPS each way); European heart (120 to 190: about 0.5); the dollar (8.5 to 9.6: about 1.0 through EBITDA and the revaluation of cash); tax (15 to 21 percent: about 0.3). Three operating drivers and one currency explain almost the whole of the FY2027 cone; abdominal margin, services, administration and interest are each worth less than 0.3. That is high concentration by any standard: the T-D2 report's own count (three to five critical drivers) is generous because it lists the abdominal and services recoveries, which are small in 2027.

The 2028 to 2030 view: one date. The FDA's handling of the heart PMA (received 28 September 2026) decides whether FY2028 looks like our 1,476 of revenue and 6.1 of EPS (launch Q1 2029), the sell-side's 1,606 to 1,720 and 9.5 to 11.8 (launch late 2027 to early 2028), or a data-request case of about 1,420 and 5.0. The own-3 report frames the width: a breakthrough PMA can clear in six months (Shockwave) or take three years with a deficiency letter and a panel (TransMedics OCS Heart), and the 2026 PMA cohort's major-deficiency rate is 92 percent. Nothing else in 2028 is in the same order of magnitude: the liver 510(k) (about 10 of 2028 revenue), kidney (about 35 of growth), services (about 10) and the abdominal margin (about 12 of gross profit) together move FY2028 EPS by less than the FDA date alone. The forecast's dependence on a single external decision is therefore extreme for 2028 and only partly relieved by 2029, when European heart and abdominal are large enough to carry growth on their own.

Concentration relative to the history and to peers. The history's dispersion was concentrated too: the -15 percent quarter (Q2 2025) was one customer destocking and one hardware shortage, the -25 percent share-price day was one component's CE delay. The forecast simply changes which single items matter. Relative to the cohort, Xvivo is more concentrated than Ambu (one product category, but 100-plus countries of distribution and no regulatory binary), similar to Bonesupport (one product, one payment decision, one market) and more concentrated than TransMedics today (three organs approved, a services network, a geographic mix). The T-D2 report's conclusion, that consensus "cannot be met if any one of (a), (b) or (c) fails", is right and I would state it more strongly: our own FY2027 figure also fails if two of the three fail, because the cone's lower half (EPS 2.1 to 3.0) is where two misses land.

What would diffuse the drivers. Over the horizon the concentration falls as European heart becomes a quarter of revenue (2029), United States liver and kidney add a third abdominal leg, and the largest customer's step-down is in the base. By FY2029 the model's growth rests on five drivers of comparable size (lung ex-customer, European heart, United States heart, abdominal Europe, abdominal United States), which is diffuse by this company's standards. The predictability of the 18-month forecast is therefore lower than that of the four-year forecast's direction, which is the opposite of the usual case and is why the memo should present the near term as the risky part.

D-04

Width and skew of the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, and so the predictability of the case

❌ D30

Question asked How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

Judgement. The cone is narrow at the top of the account and very wide at the bottom, and its skew changes sign as one moves down it. FY2027 revenue is predictable to about plus or minus 6 percent (1,090 to 1,230 around 1,162) with a mild negative skew; the FY2027 EBITDA margin to about plus or minus 3 points (21 to 27 percent around 24) with no clear skew, because the downside (hiring absorbs growth; the transfer runs late) and the upside (management pauses spend as it did in H2 2025; heart arrives at the sell-side's 181) are both discretionary and both evidenced; FY2027 adjusted EPS to about 2.1 to 5.6 around 3.8, a width of 3.5 that is 90 percent of the central figure, with a negative skew because the two largest sources of EPS variance below EBITDA (an unhedged krona on the consensus path to 8.67 against the dollar; amortisation that has already been revised up once, from 25 to 39 for the CE-mark asset) point down, and because the market's reaction function is asymmetric (a 30 percent fall on a 15 percent revenue miss in Q2 2025; a 15 percent rise on a 25 percent-margin beat in Q4 2025). Over 18 months the FDA decision does not yet enter the numbers but enters the price: any Day-100 letter, panel listing or deficiency letter in 2027 would move the shares 15 to 25 percent either way. So the case is predictable in its business trajectory and unpredictable in its reported earnings and its share price, which is a case the Consumer should size for a drawdown of a third and should not describe as low-risk.

The cone built from the drivers. Taking the second set's base case (revenue 1,162; EBITDA margin 24 percent; D&A 120; net financial items -8; tax 20 percent; EPS 3.8) and the five swing items:

  • Downside composite (the largest customer falls by 100 rather than 65 as the Q3 inventory build unwinds into an aggressive 2027 transition; European heart 120 as Tier 2 reimbursement slips to H2 2027; operating costs grow 17 percent as in 2026; the dollar averages 8.5; abdominal gross margin stuck at 61): revenue about 1,090 (+10 percent), EBITDA margin about 21 percent (229), EBIT 109, financial items about -25, pre-tax 84, EPS about 2.1. Nothing in this composite is improbable on its own; the own-1 report rates an aggressive customer transition a "non-consensus downside scenario" with precedent (a near-40 percent fall in lung revenue to the same customer in 2025), the own-2 report says European funding approvals are "historically slow", and 2026's cost growth was 14 percent.
  • Upside composite (customer -40, retained STEEN volumes growing with the customer's own platform; European heart 190 with two Tier 2 decisions in H1 2027; costs +11 percent with a spending pause; the dollar at 9.7; abdominal 64 percent): revenue about 1,230 (+24 percent), margin about 27 percent (332), EBIT 212, financial items +10, pre-tax 222, EPS about 5.6. This is roughly DNB Carnegie's post-Q3 case (5.70) and is the case in which the company's "2027 springboard" language proves right on revenue while still missing its margin targets by three points.
  • Probability weights. I would put about 25 percent on outcomes at or below 3.0, 50 percent between 3.0 and 4.5, 25 percent above 4.5; the mode sits near 3.8 and the mean a little below it. Against the consensus of about 5.8 (post-Q3 FactSet mean), only the upper tail of our cone reaches consensus, and only the upper decile exceeds it.

Why the revenue cone is narrower than the history suggests. The T-D2 report counts quarterly growth from -15 to +34 percent and EBITDA margins from 13 to 25 percent over eight quarters and infers an "unusually wide" range. The quarters are wide; the year is narrower, because the two 2025 events that produced the -15 percent (a destocking that reversed; a hardware shortage that was fixed) and the 2026 timing items (19 of lease recognition and customer inventory in Q3) are the kind that cancel within a year. The customer's 2027 transition is the exception: it does not cancel, and it is the item with the widest range (40 to 100) and the least disclosure (the own-1 report finds no public contract terms, no fluid volumes per run under the new platform and no pricing detail). The OPO decertification transition (agreements expire January 2027; Tier 3 OPOs face decertification) adds a two-quarter contracting pause with a modest range. Kidney and European liver are low-variance; services is low-variance because it is small.

Why the EPS cone is so much wider than the EBITDA cone. Three multipliers sit between them. Amortisation of 120 against EBITDA of 279 means a 10 percent EBITDA miss is an 18 percent EBIT miss. Net financial items of plus or minus 30 a year on 250 to 300 of mostly foreign-currency cash add a further plus or minus 0.75 of EPS that has nothing to do with operations (the second set's P-02 sized it; the own-2 and I2 reports confirm the brokers model it inconsistently). The tax rate's range (15 to 21 percent) adds plus or minus 0.15. The result: a cone of 21 to 27 percent on EBITDA margin becomes 2.1 to 5.6 on EPS. The second set's P-09 noted the compounding ("20 of extra EBITDA is about 0.5 of EPS, not 0.63"); the cone shows the same arithmetic in reverse.

Skew. Revenue: mildly negative (the customer and the OPO transition have a concrete downside; heart's upside is capped by the December 2026 start of CE-marked sales and by reimbursement calendars; kidney's upside is capped by the component replacement until early 2027). Margin: symmetric. EPS: negative, for the currency and amortisation reasons above and because the one positive below-EBITDA surprise available, recognition of United States deferred tax assets, requires United States profitability that is not in prospect before 2029. Share price: negative in the near term, because the consensus still has to come down and the stock's reaction to misses has been two to three times its reaction to beats; positive beyond 18 months in the sense that the strategic floor (11 to 20 times sales paid for OrganOx and Paragonix against about 7 times for Xvivo) and a 8 to 9.5 percent short interest make the upside on a clean FDA outcome discontinuous.

The 18-month cone in share-price terms, for the memo's riskiness topic. On our EPS the trough-multiple arithmetic of the first set's C-83 moves down: 20 to 25 times a 24-month-forward EPS of about 5.6 is 110 to 140, a 37 to 50 percent fall from 223.4; RedEye's bear case is 140 and its base 360 to 369; DNB Carnegie's bear case is a 25 percent discount to TransMedics. The upside on a clean path (consensus EPS held and the multiple at its five-year median of 45 times 8.8) is about 400. The width of that range (110 to 400) around a price of 223 is the market's own statement that the case is unpredictable, and it is wider than for any peer on the dashboard.

Predictability, stated plainly. Business: moderately predictable (revenue plus or minus 6 percent; margin plus or minus 3 points). Earnings: poorly predictable (plus or minus 45 percent). Share price: unpredictable within 18 months, driven by a regulator's calendar and a customer's transition that neither the company nor the brokers can date. The honest description for Richard is "a predictable business inside an unpredictable security".

D-05

Helpfulness of the company's disclosure for monitoring the case over about the next six months

C58

Question asked How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

Judgement. The disclosure is unusually good at the operating level and unusually poor at the financial-bridge level, and that combination is more helpful than it sounds for the next six months, because the two events that decide the case in this window (the first commercial heart quarters in Europe; the largest customer's first quarters on its own platform) are operating events the company does disclose, quarterly and by segment, while the items it does not disclose (hub throughput, the customer's contract terms, programme-level capitalisation, a tax rate) are ones a careful Consumer can bound from the segment data and the balance sheet. The company also helps in two ways most Nordic small caps do not: it publishes a consensus compilation before each report (19 October 2026 for Q3; the next will precede 28 January 2027), so beats and misses are well defined, and it disclosed the Q3 timing items (8.5 of lease recognition; about 10 of customer inventory) unprompted. The material unhelpfulness is the absence of numerical guidance in a year with three launches, the absence of any bridge from the operating blocks to the 2027 targets, and the FDA process, which the company will report only at milestones. For the memo's purpose, the case can be tracked quarterly against about a dozen numbers, listed below with the thresholds I would use.

What the company discloses that matters in the next six months (two reports: Q4 2026 on 28 January 2027; Q1 2027 in late April 2027).

  • Segment sales, organic growth and gross margin for Thoracic, Abdominal and Services every quarter; lung and heart sales within Thoracic, liver and kidney within Abdominal, and heart by region on the call (Q3: Europe 11, Australia and New Zealand 9). This is the single most useful disclosure in the sector: it lets the European heart ramp be read directly.
  • United States EVLP disposables growth (+183 percent in Q3; +92 percent year to date) and activated OPO hubs (three; five in the pipeline). Growth is disclosed; throughput is not, and management has said hub data will follow after six months of operation, so the Q1 2027 report is the earliest plausible date for a volume figure.
  • FlowHawk growth (0 percent in Q3 from 48 percent in Q2, unexplained); capitalised development each quarter (37.1 for heart alone in Q3; 131.7 for nine months); CE-mark amortisation (3.2 for one month, 39 a year); the quarterly cash-flow statement (operating cash flow 53 in Q3) and the consensus compilation.
  • Qualitative steers that have proved accurate: kidney constrained until early 2027; services contracts slow to convert; investment continues; sales to the largest customer to decline; the Q3 margin partly seasonal.

What it does not disclose, and how to bound it.

  • The largest customer's step-down (size and timing). Bound it from lung sales: nine-month lung about 440; a quarter without timing items and with the customer buying only fluids should still exceed about 135 to 140; below 130 means the step-down is running faster than our -65 a year; United Therapeutics' own filings (its Q2 2026 10-Q says LungFX joins its commercial services in 2027) are the second channel.
  • Hub throughput. Bound it from United States EVLP disposables growth against a destocked base; a growth rate that falls below about 30 percent in Q1 2027 (when the base is no longer destocked) would say the hubs are not yet producing.
  • Services contracts. Bound it from services revenue (13.7 in Q3) and gross margin (-19 percent); the company will say "contracts signed" in words.
  • Operating costs. Disclosed as a single line (150 in Q3 including depreciation); bound the launch build from headcount (207 at Q3) and from the opex line excluding D&A (about 125 a quarter).
  • Depreciation and amortisation, tax, financial items. Disclosed only as totals; the CE-mark asset's 9.75 a quarter is known; the rest must be inferred. The nine-month tax rate (15 percent) and financial items (about +34) can be read from the accounts, which is how I found the second set's error.
  • FDA process. The company announced receipt of the PMA (28 September 2026) and will announce a decision; a major deficiency letter or a panel date would be price-sensitive under Swedish rules and likely disclosed; the Day-100 meeting (early January 2027) will probably not be. Monitor the FDA's advisory-committee calendar and CAP enrolment (3 of 60 at Q3, per DNB Carnegie) as proxies.
  • Reimbursement outside France. Disclosed by press release when decided; the Tier 2 countries (the Netherlands, Austria, Belgium, the United Kingdom) are named, timings are not.

The monitoring list for the next six months, with thresholds. Positive and negative readings for the Q4 2026 report (28 January 2027) and the Q1 2027 report (late April 2027):

  1. Heart sales: Q4 at or above 30 and Q1 2027 at or above 32 is on or ahead of our path (quarterly 30, 33, 42, 50 in 2027); Q4 at or below 24 (no conversion) or Q1 below 28 is behind. Confirmation that CE-marked commercial invoices were issued in December 2026 and the commercial price per case relative to derogation pricing.
  2. Lung sales excluding disclosed timing items: Q4 at or above 140 and Q1 2027 at or above 138 is consistent with a 65 step-down; Q1 below 130 says -100.
  3. United States EVLP disposables growth: Q1 2027 above 30 percent against a no-longer-destocked base.
  4. Abdominal gross margin: Q4 at or above 62 percent and Q1 at or above 63; kidney growth back above 25 percent in Q1 2027.
  5. Services gross margin: zero or better in Q1 2027 with named contract conversions; still at or below -10 percent means the 2027 drag is larger than our zero.
  6. Operating costs excluding D&A: at or below about 135 a quarter in Q4 and 140 in Q1 2027 is consistent with 13 percent growth; above 145 says 17 percent.
  7. D&A: at or above 28 in Q4 (confirms the CE-mark charge; ex-acquired about 25) and any statement on when the PMA or liver assets will amortise.
  8. Tax: a Q4 charge near 20 percent; any commentary on the 2027 rate or on United States deferred tax.
  9. Cash: year-end cash at or above 300; Q4 capitalised development at or below about 45; inventory not rising by more than about 20.
  10. Milestones: fourth and fifth hubs; two or more Tier 2 reimbursement decisions by April; the liver Q-sub outcome and confirmation of a H1 2027 510(k) submission; a permanent CFO; any FDA communication.
  11. The 2027 targets: restated, re-dated or dropped at the Q4 report (see I-01).
  12. External: the Riksbank's November decision and USD/SEK against our 9.0 to 9.2 assumption for 2027; TransMedics' Q3 results on or about 4 November 2026 for United States heart and liver volumes; short interest (8 to 9.5 percent).

Against peers. Xvivo's segment disclosure is better than Ambu's (one segment with product splits), comparable to Vitrolife's (three areas) and far better than TransMedics' (product and service revenue, no organ split); its guidance practice is worse than all three (Ambu guides organic growth and EBIT margin annually; Vitrolife gives a long-term target with a bridge; TransMedics guides annual revenue). The memo should credit the operating transparency and charge the guidance vacuum.

D-06

Traceability from what the company says it will achieve to drivers, lines and earnings per share; and the external transmission from the value chain to earnings per share

❌ D45

Question asked How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

Judgement. The internal transmission is clear and quantifiable from the plan to the segment gross-profit line and opaque from there to earnings per share; the external transmission is clear in direction from the value chain to volumes and prices and is then multiplied by the same below-EBITDA opacity. Concretely: every one of the company's stated achievements (hubs, heart centres, the United States launches, the kidney relaunch, the capacity project) can be traced to a disclosed revenue line and a disclosed gross margin, so that a Consumer can say what each milestone is worth in gross profit to within about 20 percent; none of them can be traced to earnings per share without the Consumer supplying four numbers the company does not (the operating-cost build behind each milestone, the amortisation each approval triggers, the tax rate, and the currency), each of which has moved EPS by more than the milestones themselves in at least one of the last three years. The result is a case in which the operating thesis is legible and the earnings thesis is not, and in which the sell-side's models diverge most exactly where the company is silent (DNB Carnegie and RedEye differ by 60 percent on 2027 net income on sales that differ by 10 percent). For predictability this is a negative that is partly self-inflicted and fully fixable: a capital-markets day with an EBITDA-to-EPS bridge would close most of the gap, and none is scheduled.

The internal chain, link by link, with the clarity of each.

Link What the company says Traces to Clarity What the Consumer must supply
Hubs and United States lung Four to five hubs (three active); EVLP disposables growth; "platform for expansion beyond the largest customer" Lung sales (disclosed quarterly); thoracic gross margin (disclosed) High to revenue; medium to gross profit (machine placements dilute the margin, as in Q3: 80 percent from 89) Hub throughput (undisclosed); the largest customer's step-down (undisclosed); finance-lease recognition timing
